Table 1
The Psychology of Everyday Things and Human-Centered Design
1. Q: What is the central argument of Chapter 1, “The Psychopathology of Everyday Things”?
A: Show answer
Norman argues that many everyday-use difficulties are not evidence that people are unintelligent or careless; they are evidence that products communicate poorly and fail to accommodate ordinary human capabilities and behavior. A person should be able to discover likely actions, understand the relationship between controls and results, and recognize what the product is doing without unnecessary guesswork. The chapter uses confusing doors, appliances, watches, and a refrigerator to show how attractive or technically sophisticated products can still be difficult to use. Its larger argument is that design must connect technology with psychology: designers should take responsibility for making products understandable, usable, and satisfying rather than expecting users to memorize arbitrary rules.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the chapter's argument in your own words, then identify one everyday product whose difficulty appears to arise from its design rather than from the user's ability."
Key point:
- Usability problems often originate in poor communication between a product and its user.
- Good design accommodates real human behavior instead of demanding perfect users.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- ISO 9241-210:2019 — Human-centred design for interactive systems
2. Q: Why can an apparently simple object such as a door be difficult to use?
A: Show answer
A door may offer only a few possible operations, but the user still needs to discover whether to push, pull, slide, or act on a particular part. If its shape and hardware do not indicate the intended action, the user must guess or experiment. Norman's examples include doors whose handles suggest pulling even though they must be slid, and glass swinging doors that conceal the hinge side. These failures show that functional simplicity does not guarantee interaction simplicity. The design must communicate the correct action at the point where the user needs to act. A simple object can therefore be confusing when its physical cues are ambiguous, absent, or misleading.
Study instruction:
- "Inspect several doors in a public building and record the action each appears to invite, the action actually required, and any hesitation or trial-and-error."
Key point:
- Simple functions still require clear action cues.
- Ambiguous hardware transfers the designer's problem to the user.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: Preventing User Errors
3. Q: What does the term “Norman door” illustrate?
A: Show answer
A “Norman door” is a door that makes its intended operation difficult to infer from its design. The label is used for doors that invite the wrong action, conceal where force should be applied, or require instructions that should not be necessary. The important lesson is not that doors are uniquely difficult. Doors are an accessible example of a broader design failure: a product does not make the available action and its location sufficiently clear. The same failure can occur in appliances, vehicles, software, public services, and industrial controls. The term is a diagnostic reminder to examine the design before blaming the person who encounters difficulty.
Study instruction:
- "Find a door that causes hesitation and explain precisely which visual or physical cue creates the wrong expectation."
Key point:
- A Norman door is a discoverability failure, not merely an unattractive door.
- Design should make the intended operation evident before the user acts.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: Preventing User Errors
4. Q: What is discoverability in product design?
A: Show answer
Discoverability is the extent to which a person can determine what actions are possible and where and how to perform them. A discoverable design exposes meaningful controls, action possibilities, and cues without forcing users to rely on memory, labels, or trial and error. In the chapter, a vertical plate on the push side of a door or visible hinges can communicate where and how to act. In a digital interface, recognizable buttons, icons, and gesture cues can reveal available operations. Discoverability does not require displaying every possible function at once; it requires making relevant actions findable when they are needed. It is a foundation of learnability and confidence.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a product with several controls and test whether a first-time user can identify the main actions without a manual."
Key point:
- Discoverability answers: what can I do, and where do I do it?
- Important actions should not depend on hidden knowledge or guesswork.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: 10 Usability Heuristics for User Interface Design
5. Q: How is understanding different from discoverability?
A: Show answer
Discoverability concerns whether users can identify the actions available and locate where to perform them. Understanding concerns whether they can interpret what the product means, how it is intended to work, and what its controls or settings signify. A person might discover a refrigerator dial but misunderstand which compartment it affects. A user might find a software command but misunderstand whether it saves locally or to a remote service. A product therefore needs both visible opportunities for action and an intelligible explanation of the system's behavior. Discoverability helps a person begin an interaction; understanding helps the person predict consequences, interpret results, and recover when something unexpected occurs.
Study instruction:
- "Use one product example to distinguish locating a control from understanding what the control changes."
Key point:
- Discoverability reveals possible actions.
- Understanding explains the meaning and consequences of those actions.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: Mental Models and User Experience Design
6. Q: Why does Norman treat design as communication?
A: Show answer
A product communicates through its physical form, controls, labels, displays, sounds, documentation, and responses. These elements tell users what the product is for, what actions are possible, what state it is in, and what happened after an action. Designers cannot normally explain their intentions directly to every user, so the design itself must carry the necessary information. When communication is weak, people invent explanations, make incorrect assumptions, and rely on memorized procedures. When communication is coherent, users can form a useful mental model and act with greater confidence. Communication is therefore not decoration added after engineering; it is part of the product's functional design.
Study instruction:
- "Review a product's physical controls, screen, sounds, and instructions as one communication system rather than as separate features."
Key point:
- Every design decision can communicate information, intentionally or unintentionally.
- Good communication helps users form a useful model of how the product works.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: 10 Usability Heuristics for User Interface Design
- NN/g: Mental Models and User Experience Design
7. Q: What is meant by the “psychopathology” of everyday things?
A: Show answer
In the chapter's title, the term points to the recurring frustration and confusion produced by badly designed everyday objects. The phrase is deliberately provocative: ordinary products can make capable people feel incompetent because their operation is opaque, inconsistent, or needlessly complex. Norman uses this experience to challenge the assumption that users are at fault whenever an interaction fails. The analysis asks what the design communicates, what assumptions it makes about human behavior, and why a person would reasonably take the action they did. The term is not a medical diagnosis; it describes the dysfunctional relationship between people and poorly designed things.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why the chapter's title is rhetorical rather than a clinical description."
Key point:
- The title highlights avoidable frustration created by everyday design.
- Analysis should focus on the interaction between people and products.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
8. Q: What are the main design specializations introduced in the chapter?
A: Show answer
Norman distinguishes industrial design, interaction design, and experience design as overlapping areas with different emphases. Industrial design focuses on the form, materials, function, value, and appearance of products and systems. Interaction design focuses on how people interact with technology and how the design helps them understand available actions and system behavior. Experience design considers the quality and enjoyment of the overall experience across products, processes, services, events, and environments. These are not sealed disciplines with rigid borders. A successful product usually needs all three perspectives because its physical form, interactive behavior, and emotional effect jointly shape whether it works well for people.
Study instruction:
- "Take a familiar product and identify one industrial-design, one interaction-design, and one experience-design decision."
Key point:
- Industrial design emphasizes product form and function.
- Interaction and experience design address use and the overall quality of the encounter.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
9. Q: What is industrial design in the context of the chapter?
A: Show answer
Industrial design is presented as the professional creation and development of concepts and specifications that optimize the function, value, and appearance of products and systems for the benefit of users and manufacturers. Its concerns include physical form, material choices, manufacturability, and the way an object fits into its intended setting. Norman's wider point is that attractive form must not obscure operation. A door can be visually elegant yet unusable if its hardware hides the hinge or fails to indicate where to push. Industrial design therefore contributes to usability when physical features are shaped to communicate purpose as well as appearance.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate a physical object for both visual quality and the information its form communicates about use."
Key point:
- Industrial design is not limited to making products look attractive.
- Form should support function and make intended use easier to understand.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
10. Q: What is interaction design and what problem does it address?
A: Show answer
Interaction design focuses on how people act with technology and how a system communicates before, during, and after those actions. It helps users understand what they can do, what is happening, and what has just happened. This includes the arrangement and behavior of controls, the clarity of system responses, and the emotional quality of the interaction. The chapter emphasizes that a technically capable device can still fail if people cannot interpret its controls or results. Interaction design draws on psychology, design, art, and emotion so that the product is not merely operational but understandable and enjoyable.
Study instruction:
- "Trace one complete interaction with an appliance or application, noting the cue before action, the action itself, and the system response."
Key point:
- Interaction design makes system behavior understandable to people.
- Controls and responses must be designed as parts of one interaction.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: 10 Usability Heuristics for User Interface Design
11. Q: What is experience design?
A: Show answer
Experience design considers the quality and enjoyment of the whole encounter with a product, process, service, event, or environment. It extends beyond whether an individual control works. The experience includes expectations, ease of learning, physical and emotional responses, interruptions, recovery from problems, and the way the product fits into a person's broader activity. Norman argues that people remember whether an interaction felt satisfying or frustrating. A product can be technically correct yet produce a poor experience if it creates confusion, anxiety, or unnecessary effort. Conversely, a well-designed interaction can create feelings of control, mastery, satisfaction, and even pride.
Study instruction:
- "Describe a recent positive or negative service experience and identify the design decisions that shaped the overall feeling."
Key point:
- Experience design considers the whole encounter, not only isolated functions.
- Emotional responses are part of product quality.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- ISO 9241-210:2019 — Human-centred design for interactive systems
12. Q: How do industrial, interaction, and experience design relate to human-centered design?
A: Show answer
Norman describes human-centered design as a philosophy and a set of procedures, while industrial, interaction, and experience design are areas of focus. HCD can therefore guide work in any of these specializations. It adds disciplined attention to the needs, capabilities, behavior, and context of the people for whom the product or service is intended. The specializations contribute different expertise, but HCD helps keep their decisions oriented toward real users rather than toward isolated technical or aesthetic goals. A project can use industrial designers, interaction designers, and experience designers while following one shared human-centered process.
Study instruction:
- "Map a product development team by specialty and identify how HCD would connect each discipline to user needs."
Key point:
- HCD is an approach that can guide multiple design specialties.
- Human needs should remain central across physical, interactive, and experiential decisions.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- ISO 9241-210:2019 — Human-centred design for interactive systems
13. Q: Why does Norman argue that machines should accommodate people rather than the reverse?
A: Show answer
People rely on experience, context, flexible interpretation, and common-sense expectations. Machines, by contrast, generally execute rules that may be precise but narrow and invisible to the user. When a product requires people to know arbitrary sequences or hidden rules, it demands a level of precision and memory that ordinary users cannot reliably provide. Norman argues that the responsibility should be reversed: designers and machines should understand human capabilities and limitations. This does not mean that every system can behave like a person. It means that the system should communicate its requirements clearly, tolerate predictable variation where possible, and guide people rather than punish reasonable mistakes.
Study instruction:
- "Identify one arbitrary rule in a product and propose how the interface could communicate or remove the requirement."
Key point:
- People should not have to infer secret operating rules.
- Technology should be designed around realistic human capabilities.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- ISO 9241-210:2019 — Human-centred design for interactive systems
14. Q: Why is the assumption that “people will read the instructions” insufficient?
A: Show answer
Instructions can support learning, especially for genuinely complex systems, but they should not compensate for avoidable confusion in ordinary tasks. Users may not have the manual available, may not know which section applies, may forget instructions, or may interpret a control differently from the designer. A product that requires repeated consultation for routine actions places excessive cognitive work on the user. Norman's washer-dryer example illustrates how a complicated interface and an equally confusing manual defeat the purpose of the product. Good design communicates essential actions through the device itself and reserves manuals for detail that cannot reasonably be conveyed at the point of use.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a routine operation and test whether it can be completed correctly without opening the manual."
Key point:
- Documentation is a supplement, not a substitute for understandable design.
- Routine actions should be discoverable at the point of use.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: 10 Usability Heuristics for User Interface Design
15. Q: What does the chapter's discussion of the Three Mile Island accident teach about human error?
A: Show answer
Norman describes his participation in an analysis of the Three Mile Island nuclear accident and explains that the control-room design contributed to operator confusion. The chapter challenges the reflex to label a failure as “human error” without examining the equipment, displays, procedures, training, and conditions that shaped the operator's decisions. The broader lesson is that errors can be predictable consequences of poor system design. Independent investigations also described a combination of equipment malfunctions, inadequate training, poor design, and deficient operating and emergency procedures. A responsible analysis therefore examines the entire human-system environment rather than treating the operator as the sole cause.
Study instruction:
- "Compare an operator's immediate action with the information and controls available at the time of the decision."
Key point:
- Human error should trigger investigation of system conditions, not automatic blame.
- Safety depends on equipment, interface, procedures, and training working together.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- U.S. GAO: Three Mile Island — The Most Studied Nuclear Accident in History
- NUREG/CR-1270 Volume II: Human Factors Evaluation of Control Room Design and Operator Performance at Three Mile Island-2
16. Q: What does it mean to design on the assumption that people will make errors?
A: Show answer
It means that a product should remain understandable and as safe as reasonably possible when a person makes a predictable slip, forgets a step, misreads a cue, or acts under pressure. Designers should not assume that users will always be attentive, consistent, or perfectly trained. They can reduce the likelihood of error through clear controls and feedback, prevent dangerous actions through suitable constraints, and make recovery easier through reversible operations and useful explanations. Chapter 1 introduces this principle and points forward to the book's later treatment of human error. The practical implication is to test realistic use, including mistakes and interruptions, rather than only ideal task completion.
Study instruction:
- "List three plausible user mistakes for a product and examine whether the design prevents, detects, or helps recover from each."
Key point:
- Human error is foreseeable and should be anticipated in design.
- Error-tolerant products make mistakes less likely and recovery more manageable.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- ISO 9241-210:2019 — Human-centred design for interactive systems
- U.S. GAO: Three Mile Island — The Most Studied Nuclear Accident in History
17. Q: What is human-centered design (HCD)?
A: Show answer
Human-centered design is an approach that begins with an understanding of the people who will use a product or service and the needs it is intended to meet. It considers human capabilities, behavior, tasks, and context before deciding what should be built. Norman describes HCD as a philosophy supported by observation, repeated approximation, and rapid testing. The goal is not to force users to adapt to a predetermined technical solution but to shape the solution around actual human needs. ISO 9241-210 similarly sets out human-centred design principles and activities across the life cycle of interactive systems, including understanding context, specifying requirements, producing solutions, and evaluating them.
Study instruction:
- "Write a short context-of-use description for a product, naming its users, goals, tasks, environment, and constraints."
Key point:
- HCD begins with people and their context.
- Design decisions should be tested against user needs throughout development.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- ISO 9241-210:2019 — Human-centred design for interactive systems
18. Q: Why does HCD emphasize observing people rather than relying only on what they say they need?
A: Show answer
People are not always aware of their own difficulties, habits, workarounds, or unmet needs. They may describe an idealized version of their behavior, forget small but important steps, or be unable to articulate why a task is difficult. Observation reveals what actually happens in context: interruptions, improvised techniques, overlooked controls, repeated checks, and environmental constraints. Norman therefore treats observation as a primary way to understand users. Contextual inquiry adds structured observation and questions in the user's normal environment, helping researchers uncover work practices that interviews alone may miss.
Study instruction:
- "Observe a person completing a real task and record actions and workarounds before asking them to explain the process."
Key point:
- Stated preferences do not always reveal actual needs or behavior.
- Observation in context exposes problems that users may not report unaided.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- ISO 9241-210:2019 — Human-centred design for interactive systems
- NN/g: Contextual Inquiry
19. Q: Why should designers delay fixing the problem definition for as long as practical?
A: Show answer
At the beginning of a project, the apparent problem may be only a symptom or an incomplete interpretation of the user's real difficulty. If a team commits too early to a specification, it may optimize a solution to the wrong problem. Norman recommends repeated approximations: observe, propose an idea, test it, learn from the result, and revise both the solution and the problem definition. This does not mean working without direction. It means keeping early assumptions provisional until evidence from users and prototypes clarifies what matters. The approach reduces the risk of building an elegant solution to an irrelevant or misunderstood need.
Study instruction:
- "Take a proposed product requirement and list the assumptions that should be tested before it becomes fixed."
Key point:
- Early problem definitions are hypotheses, not unquestionable facts.
- Iteration can refine both the design solution and the understanding of the problem.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- ISO 9241-210:2019 — Human-centred design for interactive systems
- NN/g: Iterative User Interface Design
20. Q: How do rapid tests and iteration contribute to HCD?
A: Show answer
Rapid tests allow a team to expose an idea to actual use before investing heavily in a complete solution. A prototype or partial workflow can reveal whether users understand the controls, can complete the task, interpret the result, and recover from errors. The team then changes the design and tests again. Iteration is not simply producing multiple versions; each cycle should respond to evidence from the previous one. ISO 9241-210 treats user-centred evaluation and iteration as core HCD principles, while usability research describes iterative refinement as a way to identify and correct problems that cannot reliably be anticipated at a desk.
Study instruction:
- "Plan a small usability test with a clear task, observable success criteria, and a decision rule for the next design change."
Key point:
- Testing turns assumptions into evidence.
- Iteration improves a design by linking each revision to observed user difficulties.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- ISO 9241-210:2019 — Human-centred design for interactive systems
- NN/g: Iterative User Interface Design
21. Q: Why should designers focus on what happens when things go wrong?
A: Show answer
Products may appear easy to use while everything proceeds as expected. The real test of a design often comes when a user misunderstands a cue, a component fails, information is missing, or a task is interrupted. At that point, people need to know what happened, what the system is doing, and what action is safe or useful next. Norman argues that good design makes failures intelligible and supports recovery rather than leaving users confused or encouraging them to make matters worse. This is particularly important in complex and safety-critical environments, where an unclear warning or misleading display can have consequences far beyond inconvenience.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a failure scenario and trace what the user sees, what they may infer, and how the design could guide recovery."
Key point:
- Failure conditions reveal weaknesses that normal operation can conceal.
- Good design helps users understand and recover when events do not go as planned.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- U.S. GAO: Three Mile Island — The Most Studied Nuclear Accident in History
- NUREG/CR-1270 Volume II: Human Factors Evaluation of Control Room Design and Operator Performance at Three Mile Island-2
22. Q: How are cognition and emotion connected in the experience of using a product?
A: Show answer
Cognition concerns processes such as perceiving, interpreting, remembering, and deciding. Emotion concerns how people feel during those processes. Norman argues that the two are tightly intertwined: confusion can produce frustration and anger, while understanding can create control, mastery, satisfaction, and pride. These feelings affect how people remember and evaluate the interaction. A product is therefore not successful merely because its functions technically operate. Its design must also support clear interpretation and an appropriate emotional experience. This does not mean that every product must entertain users; it means that avoidable anxiety and frustration are design outcomes that should be considered.
Study instruction:
- "Describe the cognitive event and emotional response in one frustrating interaction, then identify the design change that could alter both."
Key point:
- Understanding can create a sense of control.
- Emotional consequences are part of the user experience, not an optional extra.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- ISO 9241-210:2019 — Human-centred design for interactive systems
23. Q: What six concepts does Norman identify as central to discoverability and understanding?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies affordances, signifiers, constraints, mappings, feedback, and the conceptual model of the system. Affordances concern possible interactions between an agent and an object. Signifiers communicate where and how an action should occur. Constraints limit or guide possible actions. Mapping expresses the relationship between controls and their effects. Feedback communicates the result of an action. The conceptual model provides an explanatory structure that helps users understand and predict how the system works. Chapter 1 develops affordances, signifiers, mapping, feedback, and conceptual models, while noting that constraints are examined in later chapters.
Study instruction:
- "Create a one-page diagram showing how each of the six concepts contributes to discoverability or understanding."
Key point:
- Discoverability is supported by several complementary design principles.
- The conceptual model connects individual cues and actions into an explanation of the system.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
24. Q: What is an affordance?
A: Show answer
An affordance is a relationship between the properties of an object and the capabilities of the person or other agent interacting with it. That relationship determines what interactions are possible. A chair affords support and sitting for a person whose body can use it in that way; it may also afford lifting for someone strong enough to move it. A slot affords insertion of objects that fit its dimensions. Affordance is not simply a label attached to an object: it depends on both the object's characteristics and the agent's abilities. This relational meaning is essential because the same object can afford different actions to different people or machines.
Study instruction:
- "Choose three objects and describe the interaction each affords, naming the relevant capability of the user."
Key point:
- Affordances describe possible interactions between an agent and the environment.
- An object's affordances depend on both object properties and agent capabilities.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: Preventing User Errors
25. Q: Why is an affordance a relationship rather than a fixed property of an object?
A: Show answer
An object does not afford an action in isolation. Its dimensions, weight, shape, surface, and other characteristics interact with the abilities, size, strength, sensory access, and skills of the agent. A chair may be liftable by one adult but not by a small child or a person with limited strength. A button may be pressable by a finger but not usable by someone who cannot reach or operate it. Calling “liftable” an inherent property of the chair ignores the person-object relationship. This relational view helps designers avoid assuming that one physical configuration will support every intended user.
Study instruction:
- "Compare how the same object can afford different actions to two users with different physical capabilities."
Key point:
- Affordance is relational, not an intrinsic label.
- Designers must consider variation among the people who will use the product.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- ISO 9241-210:2019 — Human-centred design for interactive systems
26. Q: How do affordances differ for people with different capabilities?
A: Show answer
The existence of an affordance depends on the capabilities of the interacting agent. A heavy chair may afford lifting to a strong adult but not to a child; a small control may be usable by a person with precise finger movement but difficult for someone with limited dexterity. A visual indicator may communicate an action to a sighted user but not to a person who cannot see it. The design implication is to consider the range of intended users rather than treating one average user as universal. Alternative interaction methods, accessible placement, sufficient target size, and non-visual cues can broaden access to an action. The appropriate solution depends on the task and users involved.
Study instruction:
- "Assess one control from the perspectives of users with different reach, strength, vision, hearing, or dexterity."
Key point:
- Capabilities influence which actions are genuinely available.
- Accessible design broadens the set of people who can use an affordance.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- ISO 9241-210:2019 — Human-centred design for interactive systems
27. Q: What is the difference between an affordance and a perceived affordance?
A: Show answer
An affordance is an actual possibility for interaction between an agent and an object. A perceived affordance is an action possibility that the user believes is available based on what they can perceive. The two may align, but they can also diverge. A door handle may suggest pulling even though the door must slide; a flat panel may appear to invite pushing even though it is fixed. A user can also fail to notice a real affordance if the design conceals it. This distinction matters because successful interaction requires both a workable physical or digital possibility and cues that help users recognize it accurately.
Study instruction:
- "Find an example where appearance suggests an action that is unavailable, and another where an available action is difficult to notice."
Key point:
- Actual possibility and perceived possibility are not always the same.
- Misalignment creates confusion, failed actions, and avoidable errors.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: Preventing User Errors
28. Q: What is an anti-affordance?
A: Show answer
An anti-affordance is a property or relationship that prevents an interaction. Glass, for example, allows people to see through it while preventing most people and objects from passing through it. A barrier may prevent entry, and a closed cover may prevent access to a mechanism. The design challenge is that an anti-affordance can be physically effective but perceptually invisible. A transparent glass door blocks passage even though its visual appearance may suggest open space. To prevent accidents, the blocking function must be made perceivable through suitable markings, framing, contrast, or other cues.
Study instruction:
- "Identify an object that prevents an action and evaluate whether the prevention is obvious to someone encountering it for the first time."
Key point:
- Anti-affordances prevent interactions.
- A prevention that cannot be perceived may fail to protect the user.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
29. Q: Why can transparent glass create a serious design problem?
A: Show answer
Transparency is useful because it allows people to see through a surface, but it can conceal the fact that the surface blocks physical movement. People may walk into a glass door or window because the anti-affordance is not sufficiently visible. Birds may also attempt to fly through a window. The issue is not that glass lacks a physical boundary; it is that the boundary may be perceptually difficult to detect. Designers can use visible framing, contrast, patterns, handles, or other markers to communicate that a barrier exists. The signal should be visible from relevant approaches and should not depend on a person already knowing that the glass is present.
Study instruction:
- "Inspect a glass entrance from different approach angles and determine whether the barrier is detectable before contact."
Key point:
- Physical barriers must also be perceptually apparent.
- Transparent materials need deliberate signals where collision is foreseeable.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- ISO 9241-210:2019 — Human-centred design for interactive systems
30. Q: What is a signifier?
A: Show answer
A signifier is any perceivable indicator that communicates meaningful information about an action, object, or system. It may be a word, label, arrow, icon, sound, texture, shape, handle, or other visible or audible cue. Its role is to tell people where to act, what action is appropriate, or how to interpret what is happening. Norman uses the term broadly: a signifier can be deliberately designed, such as a PUSH label, or incidental, such as a worn path that shows where people usually walk. A signifier succeeds only if the intended user can perceive and interpret it in the relevant context.
Study instruction:
- "List the signifiers on a familiar product and explain what each one tells the user."
Key point:
- Signifiers communicate information about where and how to act.
- A signifier must be perceivable to perform its communication role.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: Preventing User Errors
- NN/g: 10 Usability Heuristics for User Interface Design
31. Q: How do affordances and signifiers differ?
A: Show answer
Affordances determine what interactions are possible; signifiers communicate where and how an action should take place. A touchscreen affords touch across its surface, but a visible arrow or icon may signify the particular region where a swipe or tap should occur. A flat plate on a door may afford pushing and also signify where to push. The concepts are related but not interchangeable. Designers sometimes call a visible cue an affordance even though it does not create a new action possibility; it merely communicates an existing one. Clear design needs both a real action possibility and an understandable signal when the action is not self-evident.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the distinction using a touchscreen gesture and a physical door as examples."
Key point:
- Affordances make interaction possible.
- Signifiers make the intended interaction understandable.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: Preventing User Errors
32. Q: Why are signifiers often more important to practical design work than hidden affordances?
A: Show answer
Users cannot act confidently on possibilities they cannot perceive or infer. Designers may know that a screen supports swiping in four directions, but if users see no indication of those gestures, they may discover only a subset of the available functions. A signifier communicates the location and nature of the action at the point of use. Norman therefore says designers can often focus their practical attention on signifiers because communication is the immediate design problem. This does not make affordances irrelevant: the underlying action must still exist and work. The point is that an undiscoverable capability has little practical value to a person who does not know it is available.
Study instruction:
- "Review an interface for hidden gestures and add only the cues needed to make useful actions discoverable."
Key point:
- An unused capability may be functionally present but practically inaccessible.
- Design must communicate useful actions, not merely implement them.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: Preventing User Errors
- NN/g: 10 Usability Heuristics for User Interface Design
33. Q: What is the difference between deliberate and accidental signifiers?
A: Show answer
Deliberate signifiers are intentionally placed to communicate an action or meaning, such as a PUSH label, arrow, raised button, or marked control. Accidental signifiers arise from features that were not necessarily designed for communication but that people interpret as useful clues. A worn path across grass may reveal the preferred route; a bookmark indicates both the current reading position and, indirectly, how much of a book remains. Norman argues that the intention behind a signal is not decisive. If people perceive and use it as information, it functions as a signifier. Designers should therefore consider the unintended messages that emerge from wear, layout, repeated behavior, and environmental context.
Study instruction:
- "Observe a shared space and identify one deliberate and one accidental signifier that influence behavior."
Key point:
- Signifiers can be intentional or incidental.
- People interpret any perceivable cue that helps them understand the environment.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
34. Q: How can an accidental signifier influence behavior in a shared environment?
A: Show answer
People use visible traces of previous activity to infer what is normal or permitted. A worn route through a field may suggest the preferred path; objects placed on a flat ledge may suggest that the ledge is an acceptable place to leave items. Norman describes a wall whose flat top was intended as a safety barrier but became a surface for discarded containers. The containers then acted as signifiers for later visitors, encouraging similar behavior. The lesson is that the meaning of a space is shaped not only by formal signs but also by repeated user behavior. Designers and facility managers should monitor these emergent cues because they can support or undermine intended use.
Study instruction:
- "Look for evidence of repeated behavior in a public area and consider what it communicates to new visitors."
Key point:
- User behavior can create signals that designers did not intend.
- Environmental cues can reinforce desirable or undesirable social practices.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
35. Q: What is a misleading signifier?
A: Show answer
A misleading signifier suggests an action or interpretation that does not match the actual behavior of the product. A handle that looks as though it should be pulled but actually requires rotation and sliding is one example. A row of flexible pipes that appears to block a road but can be driven over is another: it communicates a restriction to most users while permitting passage for those who know its construction. Misleading cues can arise accidentally through poor design or be intentional in special contexts. In ordinary products, they create errors because users reasonably trust the signal they can perceive. Designers should test the interpretation of cues rather than assuming their intention will be obvious.
Study instruction:
- "Find a misleading cue in a product and state what action it invites and what action is actually required."
Key point:
- Users act on the meaning a cue communicates, not on the designer's private intention.
- Signifiers should accurately represent available actions and restrictions.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: Preventing User Errors
36. Q: How can door hardware communicate whether a person should push or pull?
A: Show answer
Hardware can communicate the intended action through its shape, orientation, and location. A broad, flat plate naturally suggests pushing, while a graspable handle commonly suggests pulling. A visible hinge or supporting pillar can also reveal which side of a swinging door moves. These physical cues can eliminate the need for a separate label if they are clear and consistent. The chapter's problem-door photographs show that identical bars on doors requiring different actions create conflicting signals. The design task is to make the hardware and required action agree so that a person can infer the operation without trial and error.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a push plate, pull handle, and ambiguous horizontal bar; predict the action each suggests."
Key point:
- Hardware itself can be a signifier.
- The physical cue should match the required operation.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: Preventing User Errors
37. Q: What do sliding-door examples reveal about misleading hardware?
A: Show answer
A sliding door may have a handle that looks like a conventional pull handle even though the user must rotate it and slide the door sideways. The handle's familiar shape activates an expectation that does not match the mechanism. A person may pull repeatedly, assume the door is locked, or search for instructions. The problem is not a lack of physical capability; it is a mismatch between the signifier and the required sequence of actions. Designers should choose hardware that communicates both the release mechanism and the direction of movement, and should test the complete action sequence with people unfamiliar with the product.
Study instruction:
- "Write the actual sequence for opening a sliding door and compare it with the sequence suggested by its handle."
Key point:
- A familiar control shape creates expectations.
- Unusual operating sequences need cues that explain the sequence and direction.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: Preventing User Errors
38. Q: What does the sink-stopper example teach about signifiers and human effort?
A: Show answer
Norman describes a sink stopper that could be opened only by pushing down on it, a solution that was not apparent from its appearance. He searched for a control, tried to pry the stopper open, and eventually had to ask hotel staff. Even after learning the mechanism, he noted that the user had to put clean hands back into dirty water. The case illustrates two distinct design failures: the action is poorly signified, and the chosen interaction imposes unnecessary inconvenience. A good redesign would make the release mechanism discoverable and avoid requiring contact with contaminated water where a simple lever or accessible control could perform the task.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate a stopper, latch, or release control for both discoverability and the effort or contamination it imposes."
Key point:
- Clear instructions do not excuse an unnecessarily awkward interaction.
- Good design communicates the action and considers the user's physical experience.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- ISO 9241-210:2019 — Human-centred design for interactive systems
39. Q: How should designers distinguish useful signifiers from decorative elements?
A: Show answer
A useful signifier changes what a person can correctly infer or do. It identifies an actionable control, indicates direction, communicates status, or clarifies a restriction. A decorative element may improve appearance without helping the user understand the product. Decoration becomes harmful when it resembles an interactive control, hides a real control, reduces contrast, or creates a false expectation. The distinction should be tested in context: can representative users identify the intended action and distinguish it from non-actionable elements? Norman's approach does not reject aesthetics; it argues that aesthetics should be integrated with clear communication rather than achieved by removing necessary cues.
Study instruction:
- "Ask first-time users to point out interactive elements in a product and note any decorative elements they mistake for controls."
Key point:
- Visual simplicity is not the same as interaction clarity.
- Every visual cue should support the intended interpretation or remain clearly non-interactive.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: Preventing User Errors
- NN/g: 10 Usability Heuristics for User Interface Design
40. Q: What does the restaurant-app dialogue demonstrate about affordances and signifiers?
A: Show answer
In the chapter's dialogue, a designer finds that users swipe left and right to see restaurant recommendations but fail to discover upward and downward swipes that reveal menus and friends. The designer proposes adding arrows and labels. Norman explains that the new cues do not create the swiping possibilities; those possibilities already exist. The arrows and labels signify where and how to perform the actions. The example shows why designers should not confuse an interaction capability with the signal that reveals it. A feature is not truly discoverable merely because the software supports it. The interface must communicate the available operation clearly enough for intended users to find and remember it.
Study instruction:
- "Apply the dialogue to a mobile application and identify hidden gestures that need visible cues or alternative controls."
Key point:
- Gestures may be available without being discoverable.
- Signifiers reveal the location and direction of an existing affordance.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: Preventing User Errors
41. Q: What is mapping in interaction design?
A: Show answer
Mapping is the relationship between elements in one set and elements in another. In product design, it often describes how a control corresponds to the object, function, or result it affects. If a row of switches controls a row of ceiling lights, mapping determines which switch operates which light. A clear mapping allows users to predict the result of an action without memorizing arbitrary associations. A poor mapping forces them to experiment or remember a code. The concept applies to physical controls, digital interfaces, vehicle steering, household appliances, and complex control rooms.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a control panel and draw the relationship between every control and the function it changes."
Key point:
- Mapping connects controls to their effects.
- Clear correspondence reduces guesswork and memory demands.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: Natural Mappings and Stimulus-Response Compatibility
42. Q: What is a natural mapping?
A: Show answer
A natural mapping uses a relationship that people can readily understand, often through spatial similarity, familiar conventions, or an intuitive analogy between a control and its result. If the controls for several lights are arranged in the same pattern as the lights themselves, users can infer which switch operates each light. If moving a control upward increases a value, the physical direction corresponds to the expected result. Natural mapping reduces the need to memorize arbitrary rules. However, what feels natural can depend on experience and culture, so designers should validate the mapping with the actual user population.
Study instruction:
- "Redesign a set of mismatched controls so their arrangement mirrors the items or outcomes they control."
Key point:
- Natural mappings make control-effect relationships easier to infer.
- Spatial and familiar correspondences can reduce learning effort.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: Natural Mappings and Stimulus-Response Compatibility
43. Q: How does spatial correspondence improve the mapping between controls and results?
A: Show answer
Spatial correspondence places controls in a pattern that resembles the layout of the things they control. When a group of switches is arranged like the ceiling lights, the user can match positions directly. When a seat-adjustment control is shaped like the seat, moving the front part of the control can adjust the front of the seat. The user does not need to learn an arbitrary numbered sequence. Spatial mapping is particularly useful when several similar controls operate several similar components. It becomes less reliable when the layout is crowded, visually distorted, or inconsistent with the user's point of view.
Study instruction:
- "Draw the physical layout of a device and compare it with the layout of its controls; identify any mismatch."
Key point:
- Spatial similarity supports quick control selection.
- Controls should resemble the arrangement of the things they affect where practical.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: Natural Mappings and Stimulus-Response Compatibility
44. Q: Why can a mapping that seems natural in one culture be confusing in another?
A: Show answer
Some mappings rely on shared biological or perceptual tendencies, while others depend on learned cultural conventions. Norman notes that upward movement is commonly associated with more and downward movement with less, but not every symbolic convention is universal. Color meanings, reading direction, spatial organization, and representations of time can vary across cultures. A designer who assumes that one convention is universally understood may create confusion for people whose prior experience differs. The correct response is not to avoid all conventions; it is to identify which conventions the intended users actually share and to test the interface with representative people.
Study instruction:
- "List interface symbols or directional conventions that may depend on culture, and plan how to test them with users."
Key point:
- Some mappings are learned conventions rather than universal truths.
- Validate culturally dependent cues with the intended audience.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: Natural Mappings and Stimulus-Response Compatibility
- ISO 9241-210:2019 — Human-centred design for interactive systems
45. Q: How do proximity and grouping help create good mappings?
A: Show answer
Controls that affect related functions should be grouped together, and a control should be near the item it affects when that arrangement is practical. Proximity reduces the mental work needed to infer which control belongs to which component. Grouping can also communicate functional relationships by placing related options within one visual or physical region. These principles draw on perceptual organization: people tend to interpret nearby or similarly arranged elements as related. Poor grouping can make a panel appear arbitrary, particularly when many controls have similar shapes. Clear grouping should reflect the product's actual functional structure rather than merely fill available space.
Study instruction:
- "Rearrange a crowded control panel into functional groups and explain the relationship communicated by each group."
Key point:
- Proximity can communicate functional association.
- Grouping should reflect meaningful relationships among controls and tasks.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: Natural Mappings and Stimulus-Response Compatibility
46. Q: How do different vehicle controls illustrate the importance of conceptual mapping?
A: Show answer
Norman compares steering wheels, tillers, joysticks, and separate track controls. Each uses a different relationship between the operator's movement and the vehicle's direction. A steering wheel is familiar because rotating it in the direction of the intended turn produces that turn. A tiller may move opposite the desired direction because it controls a rudder whose effect is mediated by water. Tracked vehicles and powered wheelchairs can turn by changing the relative speeds of left and right tracks or wheels. These systems can all be usable when the user has a clear conceptual explanation of how the control changes motion. The best mapping is not always the same physical arrangement; it is one that users can understand and predict.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how a tiller or differential-wheel control changes direction, using a simple sketch or verbal model."
Key point:
- A mapping can be learned through a coherent explanation of cause and effect.
- Controls are easier to use when their effects are predictable.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: Natural Mappings and Stimulus-Response Compatibility
47. Q: What is feedback in product and interaction design?
A: Show answer
Feedback is information about the result of an action. It tells a person whether the system received the request, what state it has reached, and sometimes what will happen next. Feedback may be visual, auditory, tactile, or a combination. A button that changes state after being pressed, a progress indicator during a download, or a clear confirmation after a setting is saved all provide feedback. Without feedback, people may repeat an action, assume the system has failed, or continue without knowing whether the desired result occurred. Feedback closes the loop between action and evaluation.
Study instruction:
- "List the feedback provided after each major action in an application or appliance and identify any silent transitions."
Key point:
- Feedback communicates the outcome or status of an action.
- Users need enough information to know whether the system responded as intended.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: 10 Usability Heuristics for User Interface Design
- NN/g: The Two UX Gulfs — Evaluation and Execution
48. Q: Why should feedback be timely?
A: Show answer
Feedback should arrive quickly enough that users can associate the response with the action that caused it. A delayed response creates uncertainty: a person may press an elevator button again, wonder whether a traffic signal detected a vehicle, or assume that an application has frozen. Norman notes that even a short delay can be disconcerting in direct interactions. For longer operations, the system should communicate that work is continuing and provide useful progress or completion information. Timing should be appropriate to the task: an instant state change is expected for a simple button, while a long-running process needs ongoing status rather than silence.
Study instruction:
- "Measure the delay between an action and the first meaningful response, then assess whether the user can tell that the system is working."
Key point:
- Timely feedback preserves the connection between action and result.
- Long operations need status information so users know what to expect.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: Response Times — The 3 Important Limits
- NN/g: 10 Usability Heuristics for User Interface Design
49. Q: What makes feedback informative rather than merely noticeable?
A: Show answer
Informative feedback explains what happened, which component responded, and, when useful, what the user should do next. A generic beep or flashing light may attract attention but provide little information about whether a task succeeded, failed, or requires intervention. A message such as “File saved to the project folder” is more useful than an unexplained flash because it confirms both the action and its result. The design should use language and signals that users can interpret without memorizing a different code for every device. Information should be specific enough to support the next decision without overwhelming the user.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite three vague alerts so each identifies the event, current status, and any necessary next action."
Key point:
- Attention-getting is not the same as communicating meaning.
- Useful feedback helps users interpret the state and decide what to do next.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: 10 Usability Heuristics for User Interface Design
50. Q: How can poor feedback be worse than no feedback?
A: Show answer
Poor feedback can distract users while failing to resolve their uncertainty. An unexplained beep may interrupt concentration, but the user may not know which device produced it or what happened. A tiny light may be missed entirely, or its meaning may be ambiguous. If the same signal represents several different states, users must remember a code that may differ across products. This can create irritation and anxiety while adding little practical value. In some cases, users learn to ignore the signals or disable them, increasing the risk that an important warning will also be missed. Feedback must therefore be designed for interpretation, not merely for visibility or low cost.
Study instruction:
- "Audit a device's alerts and classify each as actionable, ambiguous, redundant, or easy to miss."
Key point:
- Uninformative signals add noise without supporting decisions.
- Feedback must identify meaningful system states in a consistent way.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: 10 Usability Heuristics for User Interface Design
51. Q: What are the risks of providing too much feedback?
A: Show answer
Excessive feedback interrupts the user's primary task and can make the environment noisy, distracting, and stressful. Norman compares machines that constantly beep or announce information to a backseat driver whose continuous commentary interferes with concentration. If users receive too many low-value alerts, they may ignore them all or disable notifications, including important ones. Excessive signals can also compete with one another, making it difficult to identify the most urgent event. Good design distinguishes routine completion, informational updates, warnings, and emergencies, and communicates each with an appropriate level of prominence.
Study instruction:
- "Count the alerts generated during a normal task and determine which could be delayed, grouped, or suppressed."
Key point:
- More feedback is not automatically better feedback.
- Signal frequency and prominence should reflect the importance of the information.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: 10 Usability Heuristics for User Interface Design
52. Q: How should designers prioritize feedback and alarms?
A: Show answer
Feedback should be prioritized according to urgency, consequence, and the action required. Routine information can be quiet or visually subtle, while warnings that require attention should be more prominent. A genuine emergency may justify an interruptive signal, but if every subsystem labels every condition as an emergency, the result is a cacophony in which critical information is lost. Norman notes that operators in complex environments may need to silence alarms simply to regain the concentration needed to diagnose the situation. Designers should coordinate alarm meanings, avoid incompatible codes, and ensure that high-priority alerts remain distinguishable and actionable.
Study instruction:
- "Create an alert hierarchy for a system and define what qualifies as information, warning, urgent warning, and emergency."
Key point:
- Alarm priority must reflect real operational significance.
- Competing urgent signals can make the overall system less safe.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- U.S. GAO: Three Mile Island — The Most Studied Nuclear Accident in History
- NUREG/CR-1270 Volume II: Human Factors Evaluation of Control Room Design and Operator Performance at Three Mile Island-2
53. Q: What is a conceptual model?
A: Show answer
A conceptual model is a simplified explanation of how a product or system works. It helps users understand the relationship between actions, controls, internal processes, and results. It does not need to describe every technical detail or reproduce the engineering design exactly. It needs to be useful enough to support prediction and appropriate action. A computer's folders and files are examples of a conceptual model: they make stored information easier to organize and manipulate even though the computer does not literally contain paper-like folders. A good conceptual model gives users a coherent explanation of normal behavior and helps them reason when something unexpected occurs.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how one familiar device works using a simple model that predicts the effects of its main controls."
Key point:
- Conceptual models simplify systems so people can understand and predict them.
- A useful model need not expose every internal technical detail.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: Mental Models and User Experience Design
54. Q: How do mental models relate to conceptual models?
A: Show answer
Mental models are the conceptual models held in people's minds. They represent what users know or believe about how a product works and are shaped by experience, training, instruction, and interaction. Different people may hold different mental models of the same product because they have different goals and levels of expertise. One person may understand only how to perform a routine operation, while a technician understands internal components and failure modes. A mental model may be incomplete or inaccurate yet still support everyday use. Designers should therefore investigate what users actually believe rather than assuming that the intended explanation has been understood.
Study instruction:
- "Ask two users to explain the same product and compare the assumptions and causal relationships in their mental models."
Key point:
- Mental models are users' internal explanations of system behavior.
- Designers should test what users believe, not what they intended users to believe.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: Mental Models and User Experience Design
55. Q: Must a conceptual model be technically complete or perfectly accurate?
A: Show answer
No. Norman says a conceptual model can be highly simplified and need not be complete or even technically accurate, provided it is useful for the user's purposes. The model should help people predict the effects of actions and make sensible decisions. However, simplification becomes harmful when it creates expectations that fail under common conditions. The chapter's cloud example illustrates this limit: material displayed on a device may appear to be stored locally even though it depends on a remote service. That simplified model works until the network connection fails, at which point the user cannot explain why saving or retrieval is unavailable. A useful model must make its important assumptions understandable.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a simplifying metaphor in a digital product and test the conditions under which it stops explaining behavior."
Key point:
- Simplification is valuable when its assumptions remain reliable for the task.
- A misleading model creates confusion when normal conditions change.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: Mental Models and User Experience Design
56. Q: Why can different users hold different conceptual models of the same product?
A: Show answer
Users approach a product with different goals, knowledge, responsibilities, and experience. An everyday driver may understand regenerative braking only as a feature that helps slow a vehicle and recover energy. A service technician may understand sensors, control units, and diagnostic states. An engineer may hold a more detailed model of the underlying energy conversion and control logic. These models need not be identical because each supports a different task. The design challenge is to provide the right level of explanation for each user without overwhelming novices or withholding information needed by experts. Role-specific information should remain consistent with the product's actual behavior.
Study instruction:
- "Describe the mental model needed by a novice user, an advanced user, and a service technician for one product."
Key point:
- Conceptual models vary with user goals and expertise.
- Different levels of explanation can coexist if they remain consistent.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: Mental Models and User Experience Design
57. Q: Where do users obtain the information from which they construct a mental model?
A: Show answer
Users infer how a product works from its visible structure, controls, labels, sounds, responses, previous experience with similar products, instruction manuals, training, sales information, advertising, websites, and help channels. Often the device itself provides only partial clues, so users fill gaps with assumptions based on familiar objects or past interactions. Those assumptions may be wrong. Designers should therefore treat every source of product information as part of the learning environment and make sure the signals do not contradict one another. A manual that describes a different control sequence from the interface, for example, weakens rather than strengthens the user's model.
Study instruction:
- "List all the sources from which a new user learns a product and check whether they communicate a consistent operating explanation."
Key point:
- Users build mental models from many sources, not only the interface.
- Inconsistent instructions and cues produce conflicting expectations.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: Mental Models and User Experience Design
58. Q: How does the design of scissors illustrate a good conceptual model?
A: Show answer
Scissors expose their operating structure. The two holes suggest where fingers go, their different sizes constrain likely finger placement, and the blades visibly indicate the cutting action. The holes are affordances because they allow fingers to be inserted, and signifiers because they show where the fingers should go. Their dimensions constrain the possible placement, while the movement of the handles maps directly to the movement of the blades. The operation remains understandable even if a person uses a less comfortable finger arrangement or the opposite hand. The user can infer how to operate the tool without a complex manual because its form communicates the essential relationships.
Study instruction:
- "Break down a familiar hand tool into its affordances, signifiers, constraints, mapping, and feedback."
Key point:
- A well-designed physical structure can communicate how a tool works.
- Visible relationships between controls and effects support an intuitive model.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
59. Q: Why is Norman's five-button digital watch difficult to use?
A: Show answer
The watch has several buttons but provides little visible indication of what each one does. Button functions change by mode, short and long presses produce different results, and some operations require simultaneous button presses. The controls do not map clearly to the functions or outcomes. The user must consult and remember a sequence of instructions instead of inferring operation from the product. The watch may be attractive and accurate, yet its interaction design is poor because its functions are not discoverable and its conceptual model is weak. The example shows that adding functions can reduce usability when the control scheme becomes opaque.
Study instruction:
- "Inspect a multifunction device and identify controls whose meanings change across modes or depend on hidden press durations."
Key point:
- Multiple functions increase the need for clear mappings and feedback.
- An attractive product can still have a poor conceptual model.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: 10 Usability Heuristics for User Interface Design
60. Q: How can a good conceptual model help when a product behaves unexpectedly?
A: Show answer
A useful conceptual model allows a person to reason about causes and likely effects instead of blindly repeating memorized steps. If a user understands which component controls temperature, airflow, storage, or connectivity, the user can form a hypothesis about an unexpected result and choose a sensible next action. Without a model, the person may repeat controls at random or make changes that worsen the problem. A conceptual model does not guarantee correct diagnosis, but it provides a structured starting point. Good feedback and visible system state then help the user test whether the explanation is correct.
Study instruction:
- "Describe a failure and show how a causal model would guide diagnosis more effectively than random button pressing."
Key point:
- Conceptual models support prediction and troubleshooting.
- Understanding the system helps users recover when normal operation breaks down.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: Mental Models and User Experience Design
- NN/g: The Two UX Gulfs — Evaluation and Execution
61. Q: Why did the refrigerator controls create a false conceptual model?
A: Show answer
The refrigerator had two compartments and two controls labeled for those compartments. This layout suggested that each control independently set the temperature of the compartment named on it. In reality, the cooling system had one thermostat and one cooling mechanism; one control affected the freezer temperature while the other regulated how much cold air was directed to the fresh-food compartment. The controls interacted, so adjusting one could change both compartments. The system image implied independent control even though the mechanism was coupled. Users were therefore led to expect a relationship that the product did not actually provide.
Study instruction:
- "Draw the conceptual model suggested by a product's labels, then compare it with the actual control relationships described in its technical documentation."
Key point:
- Labels and control layout can imply a causal model.
- The implied model should match the system's actual behavior closely enough for users to predict outcomes.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: Mental Models and User Experience Design
62. Q: How do interacting refrigerator controls make temperature adjustment difficult?
A: Show answer
When two controls affect overlapping parts of a system, changing one setting can disturb the result of another. In Norman's refrigerator, the user could not simply adjust the freezer control to warm the freezer while assuming the fresh-food compartment would remain unchanged. The control relationships were coupled, and the actual mechanism was not evident from the labels. The manual also required waiting about twenty-four hours for temperatures to stabilize after an adjustment. This combination of interacting controls, uncertain causal relationships, and delayed feedback made trial-and-error adjustment slow and frustrating. A better design would either make the compartments independent or clearly explain and display the interaction.
Study instruction:
- "Identify whether controls in a device act independently or interact, and test how a change to one affects the others."
Key point:
- Interdependent controls require an intelligible model of their interaction.
- Delayed feedback makes poorly mapped controls especially difficult to tune.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: Mental Models and User Experience Design
63. Q: What role does feedback play in the refrigerator example?
A: Show answer
The refrigerator provided weak and delayed feedback. After changing a setting, the user could not immediately tell whether the adjustment had moved the temperature in the desired direction. The manual advised waiting twenty-four hours for stabilization. Even if the user understood the control model, the delay made it difficult to learn through experimentation because the cause and effect were separated in time. The example shows that conceptual models and feedback work together: a clear explanation helps users predict an outcome, while timely, meaningful feedback helps them confirm or revise that prediction. When both are weak, routine adjustment becomes a prolonged experiment.
Study instruction:
- "Assess a product that changes slowly and propose an interim status display or measurement that would help users understand progress."
Key point:
- Feedback is essential for learning cause and effect.
- Long delays make clear models and intermediate status information more important.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: Response Times — The 3 Important Limits
64. Q: What is the system image?
A: Show answer
The system image is the total information about a product that users can perceive or obtain from its built form and supporting materials. It includes physical structure, controls, displays, signifiers, instructions, documentation, websites, help lines, and other information available to the user. Users rely on this system image to construct their own mental model because they cannot normally speak directly with the designer. If the system image is incomplete, contradictory, or misleading, the user's model will likely be incomplete or wrong. The term therefore describes the communication channel between the designer's intentions and the user's understanding.
Study instruction:
- "Inventory every element that communicates how a product works, including the interface, packaging, manual, website, and support information."
Key point:
- The system image is the information available to the user about the product.
- Its coherence determines how well users can infer the intended model.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: Mental Models and User Experience Design
65. Q: How do the designer's model, the system image, and the user's model relate?
A: Show answer
The designer's model is the designer's understanding of how the product should look, feel, and operate. The system image is the information conveyed by the product as built, including its interface and documentation. The user's model is the explanation the user constructs from that information and from prior experience. These form a communication chain rather than a direct conversation: the designer communicates through the system image, and the user interprets it. The designer may expect the user's model to match the intended model, but that match will not occur automatically. It depends on whether the system image communicates the important relationships clearly and consistently.
Study instruction:
- "Draw the three-part relationship and annotate where a misunderstanding could enter the communication chain."
Key point:
- Designers communicate with users through the system image.
- The user's model is an interpretation, not a copy of the designer's intention.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: Mental Models and User Experience Design
66. Q: Why is the burden of communication placed on the system image?
A: Show answer
The designer is usually absent when the product is being used. The user cannot inspect the designer's private intentions and must infer the operating model from the product and its supporting information. Consequently, the product's visible structure, controls, labels, feedback, and documentation must communicate the necessary relationships. If those elements are unclear, the designer's technically correct internal model does not help the user. This is why Norman treats communication as a core design responsibility. A successful design makes the intended operation evident through what the user can actually perceive rather than relying on what the design team knows.
Study instruction:
- "Imagine using a product without access to its designer and list what information the product itself must communicate."
Key point:
- The user can interpret only the information made available.
- Clear system images are the designer's main means of communicating the operating model.
Source links:
- The Design of Everyday Things: Revised and Expanded Edition
- NN/g: 10 Usability Heuristics for User Interface Design
67. Q: What can happen when the system image is incomplete or contradictory?
A: Show answer
Users may construct conflicting explanations, make incorrect predictions, and develop workarounds that appear to work only under certain conditions. A control may be labeled one way while the manual describes another behavior; a display may imply that data is stored locally while the service actually depends on a remote connection. When the expected result does not occur, the user has no reliable explanation for the discrepancy. The person may repeat actions, blame themselves, or assume the product is broken. The remedy is to align the physical or digital interface, labels, feedback, documentation, and support content around one coherent model.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the same task as described by the interface, manual, and support website; identify any contradictions."
Key point:
- Inconsistent information weakens the user's mental model.
- All communication channels should describe compatible system behavior.
Source links:
