- Evaluate a proposed artificial engine sound for an electric vehicle. Separate the familiarity benefit from the information the sound actually communicates.
Key point:
- Familiar imitation can aid recognition but may not convey the new system's real state.
- The sound should serve pedestrian understanding before brand nostalgia.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Knowing What to Do
87. Q: How should designers evaluate an auditory signifier in a real environment?
A: Show answer
Evaluation should test whether users notice the sound, recognize its source, infer the intended state, and respond correctly under realistic conditions. Testing should include background noise, different distances, multiple simultaneous sources, different listening positions, and users with varied hearing abilities. Designers should also measure annoyance, fatigue, privacy implications, and whether the cue masks other important sounds. A sound that works in a quiet laboratory may fail on a busy street or in a workshop. The evaluation should therefore include both controlled testing and observation in the environments where the sound will actually be used.
Study instruction:
- Develop a test plan for a device alert. Include participants, environmental conditions, comprehension measures, response accuracy, and nuisance assessment.
Key point:
- Auditory cues must be evaluated in realistic acoustic environments.
- Detection alone is insufficient; users must correctly interpret and act on the cue.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Knowing What to Do
88. Q: How can visual, auditory, and physical constraints complement one another?
A: Show answer
A robust design can provide several mutually reinforcing forms of information. A physical keyway may prevent incorrect insertion, a visual mark may show the intended orientation, and a click may confirm that the component has engaged. These channels serve different purposes: the constraint prevents, the signifier guides, and the feedback confirms. Redundancy is especially valuable when users are distracted or when one channel may be unavailable. However, the cues must agree. A visual instruction that conflicts with the physical geometry or a sound that implies success before the mechanism is secure can create dangerous uncertainty.
Study instruction:
- Choose a connector or safety device and propose one preventive physical cue, one visual signifier, and one feedback cue. Check that all three communicate the same state.
Key point:
- Prevention, guidance, and confirmation are complementary functions.
- Redundant cues are useful only when they are consistent and truthful.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Knowing What to Do
89. Q: What should a designer examine when evaluating discoverability?
A: Show answer
Discoverability should be assessed by asking whether a first-time user can identify what actions are possible, where to act, how to act, and what result to expect. The evaluation should include the object itself and the environment: lighting, reach, viewing angle, noise, time pressure, and surrounding activity. It should also consider whether users share the conventions assumed by the design. A control may be technically visible but still difficult to interpret, or its signifier may be clear only from one viewing position. Observing untrained users is more informative than asking designers who already know the intended operation.
Study instruction:
- Create a discoverability checklist for a product. Test it with someone unfamiliar with the product and record where the person hesitates or guesses.
Key point:
- Discoverability is about whether users can infer the action before being taught.
- Realistic context and first-time testing expose hidden assumptions.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Knowing What to Do
90. Q: How can a designer distinguish an error caused by the user from an error induced by the design?
A: Show answer
The designer should examine what information and constraints were available at the moment of action, what the user reasonably expected, and whether the system made the correct action discoverable. If several users make the same mistake, the pattern may indicate ambiguous mapping, misleading signifiers, inconsistent conventions, or missing constraints. The analysis should include environmental conditions and task demands rather than assuming carelessness. Individual mistakes still matter, but a design that repeatedly invites the same error should be improved. This approach shifts attention from blame to the conditions that make safe and successful action more likely.
Study instruction:
- Observe a repeated use error and document the user's goal, available cues, expected result, actual result, and environmental conditions before assigning a cause.
Key point:
- Repeated errors often reveal a systematic design weakness.
- Analyze the interaction conditions before blaming an individual user.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Knowing What to Do
91. Q: What role does feedback play after a user acts on a constrained control?
A: Show answer
Constraints can guide or prevent an action, but users still need feedback to know whether the intended result occurred. A component may fit into a connector but not be fully engaged; a switch may move without activating the expected light; a door may close without latching. Feedback confirms the system's actual state and supports correction when the result differs from the goal. It can be visual, auditory, tactile, or a combination. The feedback should correspond to the real outcome, arrive soon enough to guide the next action, and remain perceivable under normal operating conditions.
Study instruction:
- For a device with a safety interlock, identify the signal that confirms the protected state and the signal that indicates a failed or incomplete action.
Key point:
- Constraints guide action; feedback confirms the resulting state.
- A false or delayed confirmation can undermine an otherwise good constraint.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Knowing What to Do
92. Q: How can designers prevent users from relying on trial and error?
A: Show answer
They can make the intended action visible, align controls with their outcomes, use physical constraints to remove invalid options, apply familiar conventions consistently, and provide immediate feedback. Instructions can supplement these features, but they should not compensate for avoidable ambiguity in a simple interaction. Trial and error is especially problematic when an incorrect attempt can damage equipment, cause injury, or create a costly delay. Designers should test whether a first-time user can predict the result before acting and whether a mistake can be recovered from safely. The goal is not to eliminate exploration but to prevent guessing from being the only way to discover basic operation.
Study instruction:
- Choose a product that requires users to experiment to discover its basic operation. Propose changes to its form, mapping, constraints, and feedback.
Key point:
- Good design replaces avoidable guessing with visible and testable clues.
- The cost and safety of trial-and-error should determine how strongly errors are prevented.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Knowing What to Do
93. Q: How should a designer handle conflicting constraints?
A: Show answer
Conflicting constraints occur when physical form, cultural convention, semantic meaning, and logical relationships suggest different actions. For example, a component may physically fit in two positions, while its color convention suggests one placement and the surrounding layout suggests another. The designer should identify which cue users are likely to trust, determine whether one clue is outdated or culturally specific, and revise the design so that the cues reinforce one another. Where conflict cannot be eliminated, the system should provide an explicit signifier or instruction at the decision point. Safety-critical choices should not depend on users resolving ambiguous clues through guesswork.
Study instruction:
- Find an object whose shape, label, and layout suggest different actions. Identify the source of each conflict and propose a coherent redesign.
Key point:
- Conflicting clues create uncertainty even when each clue is individually understandable.
- The design should establish one clear, reliable interpretation at the point of action.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Knowing What to Do
94. Q: What should be considered when applying constraints to users with different abilities?
A: Show answer
A constraint that works for one group may be invisible, inaccessible, or difficult for another. Small labels may not be readable; color-only cues may not be distinguishable by some users; a forceful interlock may be difficult for people with limited strength; and a sound-only alert may be unavailable to someone with hearing loss. Designers should use multiple channels, appropriate contrast, reachable controls, manageable force, and clear feedback. They should also test with people who have varied sensory, motor, and cognitive abilities. Accessibility is not an optional layer added after the interaction is complete; it affects whether the intended constraints and signifiers can function at all.
Study instruction:
- Audit a control for visual, auditory, motor, and cognitive accessibility. Recommend at least one alternative cue or interaction for each barrier identified.
Key point:
- A constraint is useful only if intended users can perceive and operate it.
- Multiple accessible channels reduce dependence on a single ability.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Knowing What to Do
95. Q: How do constraints affect safety-critical systems differently from ordinary consumer products?
A: Show answer
In ordinary products, a confusing control may cause inconvenience, delay, or frustration. In safety-critical systems, the same type of ambiguity can lead to injury, equipment damage, or loss of life. Designers should therefore use stronger preventive measures, clearer mappings, unmistakable signifiers, and reliable feedback when the consequences of error are severe. Forcing functions and interlocks may be justified where warnings are not enough. However, a safety mechanism must also be usable in emergencies and must not create a greater hazard through delay or obstruction. The design must be assessed across normal operation, foreseeable misuse, maintenance, and failure conditions.
Study instruction:
- Compare a household light switch with a machine control whose wrong operation could cause injury. Explain how the acceptable level of ambiguity differs.
Key point:
- The required strength of constraints depends on the consequence of error.
- Safety mechanisms must remain usable in emergency and maintenance conditions.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Knowing What to Do
96. Q: How can a designer test whether a standard or convention is understood by users?
A: Show answer
Testing should include users from the relevant regions, age groups, professions, and levels of experience. Participants should be shown the control or signal without being told its meaning and asked to predict the action or interpret the result. The designer should record correct interpretation, hesitation, confidence, and any alternative meanings. Testing should also examine whether users transfer the convention consistently across related products. If a significant group misunderstands the cue, the design may need a more explicit signifier or a revised standard. The goal is evidence of shared understanding, not merely agreement among the design team.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a short recognition test for a symbol or control convention. Recruit varied participants and define what counts as successful interpretation.
Key point:
- A convention should be validated with intended users rather than assumed to be universal.
- Recognition, confidence, and transfer are useful measures of shared meaning.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Knowing What to Do
97. Q: What should a design review include for a multi-control environment such as an auditorium?
A: Show answer
A review should begin with the activities the room supports, such as presenting slides, showing video, conducting discussion, recording, or cleaning. It should map each activity to the required lighting, sound, projection, and other settings, then identify the controls and the sequence in which users need them. The review should check spatial mapping, labeling, mode behavior, exceptions, accessibility, and manual overrides. It should also involve the people who install, operate, and maintain the system. Testing with actual lecturers or presenters can reveal workflow interruptions that a component-by-component technical inspection would miss.
Study instruction:
- Create a review checklist for an auditorium control system. Include activity scenarios, mappings, exceptions, user roles, and acceptance tests.
Key point:
- Complex control environments must be reviewed as complete activity systems.
- Real users reveal integration failures that technical component tests may miss.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Knowing What to Do
98. Q: How should a designer assess whether an activity mode is too restrictive?
A: Show answer
The designer should observe users performing both typical and unusual versions of the activity. If a mode prevents a common adjustment, resets unrelated equipment, or forces users to leave the current task to change one variable, it is too restrictive. The review should identify which settings truly need to be coordinated and which should remain independently adjustable. It should also test whether users understand the mode's effects and can recover from an accidental change. A mode should simplify routine work without assuming that every instance of the activity is identical.
Study instruction:
- Test an activity mode with one normal scenario and three exceptions. Record any point where the mode blocks a legitimate adjustment or changes an unrelated setting.
Key point:
- Automation should encode useful patterns without erasing legitimate variation.
- Exception testing is necessary to determine whether a mode is genuinely usable.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Knowing What to Do
99. Q: How can a system communicate the state of an interlock or lockout?
A: Show answer
The system should make clear which condition is preventing operation, what action is required, and whether the safeguard is currently active. A vague message such as operation unavailable gives little guidance. A more useful indication identifies the open guard, missing credential, unengaged latch, or other unmet prerequisite and explains how an authorized user can resolve it. The signal should not encourage unsafe bypassing, and it should distinguish a normal safety condition from a fault in the interlock itself. For maintenance and emergency response, diagnostic information and a controlled recovery procedure may also be necessary.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite a generic safety interlock warning as a state-specific message. Include the unmet condition and safe next step without inviting a bypass.
Key point:
- A safeguard should explain the condition that blocks the next action.
- State-specific feedback supports safe recovery and diagnosis.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Knowing What to Do
100. Q: What is the risk of using brand-specific sounds for safety-critical signals?
A: Show answer
A brand-specific sound may be memorable and support product identity, but it can make the signal less predictable for people who encounter many different products. Pedestrians should not need to learn a separate acoustic language for every vehicle manufacturer to recognize a nearby car. Core safety information should therefore use common acoustic properties that users can learn once. Limited variation may be acceptable if it does not interfere with detection, source recognition, or interpretation. Marketing differentiation should never obscure the signal's safety function or create confusion in environments where multiple products operate together.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate a proposal for distinctive brand sounds on a fleet of vehicles. Identify which acoustic properties should remain common and why.
Key point:
- Safety meaning should not depend on learning a separate brand language.
- Differentiation is acceptable only within a stable and recognizable shared signal.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Knowing What to Do
101. Q: How can sound help users detect faults before a visible failure occurs?
A: Show answer
Mechanical changes often alter vibration, resonance, pitch, or rhythm before the equipment stops working. A loose part may rattle, a blocked airflow path may change a motor's pitch, and a damaged component may produce a new scraping or knocking sound. Experienced users can learn these patterns and use them as early warning signs. Designers can support this by preserving informative operating sounds or generating carefully designed diagnostic signals. However, a sound should not be treated as a definitive diagnosis without inspection; it is an indicator that can prompt investigation and maintenance.
Study instruction:
- Choose a machine or appliance and list sounds that may indicate normal operation, wear, obstruction, or failure. State what inspection would confirm each suspicion.
Key point:
- Changes in sound can be early indicators of mechanical condition.
- Auditory warning should trigger appropriate verification, not replace diagnosis.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Knowing What to Do
102. Q: What does the chapter imply about the relationship between elegance and usability?
A: Show answer
An elegant appearance does not guarantee that a product communicates its operation. Smooth cabinet fronts, hidden controls, and sculptural faucet forms may look refined while removing the signifiers users need. Conversely, visible hardware can contribute to a coherent visual language when it is designed as part of the object rather than added as an afterthought. The relevant question is not whether an object looks simple, but whether users can understand and perform the intended action with reasonable effort. Aesthetic decisions should be evaluated alongside discoverability, mapping, feedback, accessibility, and error recovery.
Study instruction:
- Select a visually minimalist product and evaluate whether its controls are discoverable. Propose a signifier that preserves the intended aesthetic while improving use.
Key point:
- Visual simplicity can conceal operational complexity.
- Aesthetic quality and usability should be designed together, not traded blindly.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Knowing What to Do
103. Q: How can a designer decide what information belongs in the world rather than in the user's memory?
A: Show answer
Information should be externalized when it is important, difficult to remember, frequently needed, or costly to misapply. A spatial switch map, visible polarity marking, clear state indicator, or standardized control direction can reduce the need to memorize arbitrary relationships. Knowledge can remain in the user's head when it is stable, familiar, or necessary for flexible interpretation, but designers should not force users to remember details that can be made available at the point of action. The decision should consider frequency of use, interruption, cognitive load, environmental conditions, and the consequence of forgetting.
Study instruction:
- For a multi-step task, identify which facts users currently memorize and which could be displayed or embodied in the environment at the moment of need.
Key point:
- Externalize important information when memory is an avoidable source of error.
- The right balance depends on task frequency, context, and consequences.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Knowing What to Do
104. Q: How can a designer evaluate the quality of a constraint system as a whole?
A: Show answer
A complete evaluation should ask whether the constraints reduce inappropriate choices, whether users can perceive and understand them, whether they support the task's actual sequence, and whether legitimate exceptions remain possible. The review should include first-time use, repeated use, distracted use, misuse, maintenance, and emergency conditions. It should measure errors, hesitation, recovery time, user confidence, and any workarounds. Designers should also check whether the constraints conflict with cultural expectations or accessibility needs. A strong system does not merely make the desired action possible; it makes the action understandable, reliable, and safe in context.
Study instruction:
- Create an evaluation plan for a product with several constraints. Include usability measures, safety outcomes, exception scenarios, and participant diversity.
Key point:
- Constraint quality must be judged in context, not by mechanism alone.
- Effective evaluation includes errors, recovery, accessibility, and real-world workarounds.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Knowing What to Do
105. Q: How can the lessons of Chapter 4 be applied to the design of digital interfaces?
A: Show answer
Digital interfaces also rely on constraints, conventions, mappings, and feedback. A disabled button can prevent an action until required information is supplied; a confirmation dialog can lock in a decision long enough to prevent accidental loss; a familiar icon can rely on cultural convention; and a spatial control layout can create a logical mapping. Yet digital constraints can also be invisible or arbitrary. Users need to understand why an action is unavailable, what information is missing, and how to recover. Designers should avoid relying solely on hidden rules or generic error messages and should ensure that touch targets, states, and system responses are discoverable and accessible.
Study instruction:
- Choose a digital workflow and identify its physical, cultural, semantic, and logical constraints. Evaluate whether the interface explains blocked actions and supports recovery.
Key point:
- The same constraint principles apply to software and physical products.
- Digital restrictions should be visible, meaningful, and recoverable.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Knowing What to Do
106. Q: How can a designer avoid over-constraining a product?
A: Show answer
Over-constraint occurs when the design prevents users from performing legitimate actions or adapting to circumstances that were not anticipated. The solution is to distinguish dangerous actions from harmless variation, provide controlled overrides where appropriate, and test unusual but valid use cases. Constraints should be as strong as the risk requires, not stronger merely because the designer prefers a single workflow. A system should also explain why an action is blocked and how an authorized user can proceed. Good design narrows harmful possibilities while preserving useful flexibility and a clear path for exceptions.
Study instruction:
- Identify one product whose restrictions may block legitimate use. Define which actions truly need prevention and which should remain possible through a controlled override.
Key point:
- Constraint strength should be proportionate to the risk and task.
- Flexibility and recovery are necessary complements to error prevention.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Knowing What to Do
107. Q: What is the relationship between standardization and future innovation?
A: Show answer
Standards help users transfer learning and make products interoperable, but they can also stabilize a particular solution and make alternatives harder to introduce. A standard may encode assumptions about older technology, physical form, or a cultural convention that later becomes less appropriate. Designers and standards bodies should therefore preserve stable meanings where they benefit users while allowing revision when a new approach provides substantial improvement. A transition should be coordinated so that users do not face incompatible versions without clear guidance. Standardization should support usability and safety rather than become an unquestioned reason to preserve an obsolete design.
Study instruction:
- Identify a standard that may constrain a newer technology. Explain which user benefits it protects and what evidence would justify revising it.
Key point:
- Standards create consistency but can also preserve legacy assumptions.
- Standards should evolve when meaningful user and safety benefits justify change.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Knowing What to Do
108. Q: How should a designer handle a missing or ambiguous signifier in a safety-critical setting?
A: Show answer
The designer should first determine what action the user must take, where it must occur, and what information is needed to choose correctly. The signifier should then be made prominent, unambiguous, and perceivable under the actual operating conditions. If a wrong action could cause harm, the system should also use physical constraints or interlocks to prevent the dangerous alternative where feasible. Feedback should confirm the actual state. The solution should be tested with users who have not been trained on the device, including under distraction, poor lighting, noise, and emergency conditions.
Study instruction:
- Select a safety-critical control with ambiguous operation. Propose a visible signifier, a preventive constraint, and feedback that confirms the safe state.
Key point:
- Safety-critical interactions need both clear guidance and robust prevention.
- First-time and adverse-condition testing is essential.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Knowing What to Do
109. Q: What is the importance of designing for the user's psychological model rather than the engineer's internal model?
A: Show answer
Engineers understand the mechanism and may find its operation obvious because they know how its components are connected. Users see the external form and infer what actions will produce which outcomes. If the interface reflects internal mechanical symmetry while violating the user's perceived movement pattern, it can feel inconsistent. Faucet handles illustrate this difference: users may think in terms of turning a knob or pushing a blade, not the direction in which a hidden valve rotates. Design should align visible action, expected result, and feedback with the user's conceptual model while retaining reliable engineering underneath.
Study instruction:
- Explain a mechanism first from an engineer's perspective and then from a user's perspective. Identify any mismatch between internal operation and perceived control.
Key point:
- The user interacts with the visible model, not the hidden mechanism.
- Engineering should implement a coherent and predictable user experience.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Knowing What to Do
110. Q: How can an organization introduce a new convention without leaving users confused?
A: Show answer
The organization should explain the reason for the change, provide consistent instructions at the point of use, train staff who support users, and coordinate deployment across locations and related products. During transition, old and new procedures should be clearly distinguished, and users should have a way to ask for help or recover from an error. The organization should measure whether people understand the new convention rather than assuming that publication of a notice is sufficient. Where possible, the transition should avoid unnecessary coexistence of incompatible systems. A new convention succeeds when users can apply it reliably in real work.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a transition plan for a new control or service convention. Include communication, training, rollout consistency, support, and evaluation.
Key point:
- A convention change is a system-wide learning and adoption task.
- Consistent rollout and practical support reduce transition errors.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Knowing What to Do
111. Q: How can sound design support accessibility without excluding people who cannot hear the cue?
A: Show answer
Sound should be treated as one information channel among several. A warning that matters for safety or task completion should also have a visual, tactile, or other accessible representation where appropriate. The alternative should communicate the same underlying state, not merely flash decoratively. Designers should consider users who are deaf or hard of hearing, people in noisy environments, people using hearing protection, and situations where audio is muted. Redundant channels should be coordinated so that the user receives consistent information and can identify the source and urgency of the event.
Study instruction:
- Choose a critical audible alert and design an equivalent visual or tactile cue. Check that the alternative communicates the same state and urgency.
Key point:
- Critical information should not depend on hearing alone.
- Accessible redundancy must preserve meaning, timing, and priority.

- Chapter 4: Knowing What to Do
112. Q: What are the main design lessons from the chapter's discussion of electric vehicle sounds?
A: Show answer
The discussion shows that a reduction in noise can remove a signifier that pedestrians depend on. It also demonstrates that sound design must balance detection, orientation, low annoyance, and consistency. A useful signal should indicate vehicle presence, help listeners estimate location and movement, remain recognizable across manufacturers, and avoid unnecessary disturbance. Limited individualization may help distinguish vehicles, but it must not weaken the common meaning. The case also illustrates that safety requirements, public expectations, industry competition, and technical standards interact. A successful solution needs input from affected users, including blind and visually impaired pedestrians, rather than relying only on manufacturers' preferences.
Study instruction:
- Summarize the four criteria for electric vehicle warning sounds and explain how each affects pedestrians and the wider community.
Key point:
- Vehicle sound is an accessibility and public-safety issue as well as a product-design choice.
- The solution must balance detection, orientation, annoyance, and shared interpretation.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Knowing What to Do
113. Q: How should a designer use user observation to improve a control or constraint system?
A: Show answer
Observation reveals what people actually do rather than what designers assume they will do. Users may hesitate, reach for the wrong part, interpret a control through an old convention, or invent a workaround that is invisible in a design meeting. The observer should record the context, goal, sequence, cues noticed, decisions made, errors, and recovery behavior without immediately coaching the participant. Repeated patterns can reveal missing signifiers, poor mappings, unsuitable constraints, or workflow conflicts. The findings should then inform a revised design that is tested again with representative users.
Study instruction:
- Plan an observation session for a confusing control. Define the task, participant profile, evidence to record, and how findings will inform redesign.
Key point:
- Observation exposes real behavior and hidden design assumptions.
- Iterative testing is necessary to confirm that a redesign solves the observed problem.
Source links:
- Chapter 4: Knowing What to Do
114. Q: How can the chapter's principles be integrated into a practical design process?
A: Show answer
Begin by defining the user's goal and observing the actual task. Identify what the user must know, what can be made visible, and which conventions are likely to be shared. Analyze the affordances, signifiers, mappings, and four constraint types; then determine whether errors should be discouraged, prevented, or safely recoverable. Design feedback for the result of each important action, including auditory cues where they add useful information. Test with first-time and experienced users in realistic conditions, including exceptions and accessibility needs. Revise the system until the intended action is discoverable, the result is predictable, and safeguards do not create unnecessary workarounds.
Study instruction:
- Apply this process to a product of your choice and produce a brief design review covering task, knowledge, constraints, mappings, feedback, safety, exceptions, and user testing.
Key point:
- Start with the task and user context, then design cues, constraints, and feedback around them.
- A design is complete only after realistic evaluation and revision.

Table 5
Human Error, System Design, and Resilient Safety
1. Q: What is the central argument of Chapter 5, “Human Error? No, Bad Design”?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that many incidents attributed to human error are better understood as failures of system and product design. People have predictable limits in attention, memory, perception, reasoning, and physical capability. A system that requires uninterrupted vigilance, perfect recall, precise data entry, or flawless execution under pressure is designed around unrealistic assumptions. When the same type of error recurs across different people, investigators should examine the task, equipment, procedures, incentives, training, and organizational conditions rather than repeatedly blaming individuals. The design objective is not to assume that people will never err; it is to reduce the opportunities for error, make errors easier to detect, and limit their consequences.
Study instruction:
- Explain the chapter’s argument in your own words, then identify one product and one workplace procedure that depend on unrealistic assumptions about human performance.
Key point:
- Recurring human error is often evidence of a system weakness, not proof of widespread incompetence.
- Design for predictable human limitations instead of demanding perfect behavior.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
2. Q: How should the chapter’s estimate that 75–95 percent of industrial accidents are attributed to human error be interpreted?
A: Show answer
The chapter opens by citing estimates that a large proportion of industrial accidents are labelled as human error. The point is not that this percentage proves every accident is caused by bad design, nor that people never make culpable choices. It challenges the usefulness of a label that ends an investigation instead of explaining the event. If a high proportion of failures are assigned to people, investigators should ask whether work conditions systematically make errors likely. The estimate is a prompt to examine the quality of the classification and the investigation process, not a substitute for incident-specific evidence. A sound review distinguishes the immediate action from the conditions that shaped it.
Study instruction:
- Separate the chapter’s rhetorical use of the estimate from the evidence needed to establish the causes of a particular accident.
Key point:
- A high rate of human-error attribution should trigger scrutiny of the system and the investigation method.
- Do not treat a broad percentage as proof about an individual incident.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
3. Q: Why is blaming and punishing an individual usually insufficient to prevent recurrence?
A: Show answer
Punishment may identify who performed the final action, but it does not necessarily explain why that action made sense to the person at the time or why the system permitted it. If a control is ambiguous, a procedure is impractical, a worker is interrupted, or production pressure rewards shortcuts, replacing or retraining one person leaves those conditions intact. The same conditions can affect the next employee. Blame can also discourage people from reporting near misses and mistakes, depriving the organization of information needed for improvement. Accountability may still be necessary for deliberate misconduct, but it should not replace analysis of equipment, task design, supervision, staffing, workload, communication, and incentives.
Study instruction:
- Take a recurring workplace error and list the system conditions that would remain unchanged if the employee were simply replaced.
Key point:
- Punishing the last person in the chain does not remove the conditions that produced the failure.
- Separate fair accountability from root-cause investigation and system improvement.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
4. Q: What is the difference between a proximate cause and an underlying cause?
A: Show answer
A proximate cause is the event or action immediately preceding an outcome, such as a pilot failing to initiate a recovery or an operator selecting the wrong control. An underlying cause explains why that action or event occurred. It may involve an unclear display, an unsafe procedure, inadequate equipment, fatigue, competing tasks, misleading feedback, or a culture that discourages questioning. A useful investigation records the proximate action but continues to examine the context that made it possible or likely. The objective is to identify changes that will reduce recurrence, not merely to describe the final link in the sequence.
Study instruction:
- Classify the final action, the immediate conditions, and the deeper organizational conditions in a sample incident.
Key point:
- Proximate causes describe what happened immediately; underlying causes explain why the conditions arose.
- Investigations should continue beyond the first identifiable human action.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
5. Q: Why do complex accidents rarely have one single root cause?
A: Show answer
Complex accidents usually emerge from several interacting conditions. A design weakness may combine with an equipment fault, an ambiguous procedure, a misleading indication, fatigue, poor communication, and an organizational pressure to continue operating. Each condition may be manageable in isolation, but their alignment can defeat the safeguards intended to protect the system. Removing one factor might have prevented the particular accident, yet that does not make it the sole cause. A strong investigation reconstructs the sequence and relationships among contributing factors, identifies failed and missing defenses, and considers how different combinations could produce similar harm.
Study instruction:
- Draw a causal map for a real or hypothetical accident and identify at least four contributing conditions and their interactions.
Key point:
- Accidents are commonly produced by interacting failures rather than one isolated mistake.
- Use causal networks and contributing factors instead of searching only for one culprit.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
6. Q: How does the Five Whys technique support root-cause analysis?
A: Show answer
The Five Whys technique encourages investigators to continue asking why after an immediate explanation has been found. The name does not require exactly five questions. It is a reminder not to stop at statements such as “the operator made an error” or “the component failed.” Each answer becomes the basis for another question until the investigation reaches causes that can be acted upon. In the chapter’s aircraft example, asking why the aircraft crashed leads to the uncontrolled dive, then the failure to recover, then the possibility that the pilot was incapacitated, and then to further questions about why incapacitation might have occurred. The method turns a description of an action into an investigation of its conditions.
Study instruction:
- Apply successive why questions to a failure in a process you know. Continue until the answers identify a changeable system condition rather than merely naming a person.
Key point:
- The Five Whys is a disciplined prompt to keep investigating beyond the first plausible explanation.
- Ask why about the explanation itself, not only about the original event.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
7. Q: What are the limitations of the Five Whys method?
A: Show answer
The Five Whys is useful but does not guarantee a correct analysis. The word “why” can mean different things to different investigators, and answers may reflect assumptions rather than evidence. Investigators may stop when they reach the limit of their own knowledge, or when they find an answer that feels satisfying. The method can also encourage an oversimplified single-cause story even when several causal paths contributed to the event. It should therefore be supported by records, interviews, physical evidence, timeline reconstruction, technical expertise, and consideration of alternative explanations. More or fewer than five questions may be necessary, and parallel lines of inquiry are often essential.
Study instruction:
- Review a Five Whys analysis and test each answer against evidence, alternative explanations, and other contributing factors.
Key point:
- Five Whys structures inquiry; it does not replace evidence or systems thinking.
- Avoid forcing a complex event into a single linear chain.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
8. Q: What does the F-22 accident discussion illustrate about investigating alleged pilot error?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes an Air Force investigation that attributed a fatal F-22 crash to the pilot’s failure to recognize and recover from an abnormal flight condition. A later Department of Defense Inspector General review questioned whether sudden incapacitation or unconsciousness had been considered as a contributing factor. The example illustrates why an investigation should ask whether the person was physically and cognitively capable of performing the expected action. A failure to act may be the visible event, while hypoxia or another impairment may explain it. The chapter uses the disagreement to show how an investigation can stop too early when it treats the operator’s final behavior as the explanation.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish the observed failure to recover from possible physiological conditions that could explain why recovery did not occur.
Key point:
- An apparent failure to act does not establish that the operator was capable of acting.
- Investigate impairment and system conditions before concluding that an action was simply negligent.

- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
9. Q: What did the utility-worker electrocution examples reveal about organizational learning?
A: Show answer
The chapter recounts incidents in which highly trained utility workers were electrocuted while servicing high-voltage lines. Investigating committees identified worker actions as errors but did not continue far enough to examine the circumstances and procedures that led to those actions. The author believed that practical procedural changes could have prevented many incidents, yet the organization made no changes and injuries continued. The case shows that technical competence does not eliminate exposure to poorly designed work. It also shows that an organization can receive recommendations politely while failing to translate them into operational change. Learning requires implementation, ownership, and verification, not merely a completed report.
Study instruction:
- Identify what an incident committee should examine after finding that a worker contacted an energized line, including procedure design, equipment, coordination, and implementation of corrective actions.
Key point:
- An investigation has little preventive value if its recommendations are not implemented.
- Track corrective actions to completion and verify that they change the conditions that caused the incident.

- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
10. Q: Why does the statement “I knew better” fail as a complete explanation of an error?
A: Show answer
After an incident, people may say that they knew the correct procedure and should have acted differently. That statement may accurately describe their knowledge after the event, but it does not explain why the error occurred at the time. The person may have faced ambiguous controls, distraction, time pressure, fatigue, competing goals, or a misleading mental model. Knowing a rule in principle does not guarantee that it will be retrieved and applied under real operating conditions. A useful investigation asks what made the incorrect action possible and what system changes would prevent or contain it, even when a competent person is distracted or under pressure.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite “the employee knew better” as a set of investigable questions about the task, environment, information, and pressures at the time.
Key point:
- Retrospective knowledge of the correct action does not explain the conditions of the failure.
- Investigate performance in context rather than relying on hindsight statements.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
11. Q: Why should designers accommodate mental limitations as carefully as physical limitations?
A: Show answer
Designers routinely account for physical limits such as strength, reach, and size, yet systems often assume unlimited attention, memory, and precision. Human cognition is affected by fatigue, stress, interruptions, workload, familiarity, and the similarity of competing tasks. People cannot reliably monitor an unchanging display for hours, remember obscure procedures used once a year, or enter long strings of characters without occasional mistakes. A design that ignores these constraints transfers the burden of system complexity to the user. Human-centered design makes important information visible, reduces unnecessary memory demands, supports recovery after interruption, and uses constraints to prevent hazardous actions.
Study instruction:
- Audit a device or procedure for assumptions about attention, working memory, precision, and sustained vigilance.
Key point:
- Mental capabilities are limited and variable; they must be considered in the design specification.
- Reduce cognitive burden instead of treating perfect attention as a permanent user requirement.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
12. Q: How does time stress contribute to human error?
A: Show answer
Time stress arises when people must complete tasks quickly because of production targets, service demands, weather, traffic, emergencies, financial pressure, or fear of delaying others. Under pressure, workers may shorten checks, skip steps, work while tired, or continue despite uncertainty. These actions may appear successful many times and may even be praised as heroic efficiency. When a failure eventually occurs, the same behavior may be condemned. This inconsistency teaches workers that speed is rewarded until an accident happens. Designers and managers should assess whether schedules, staffing, throughput targets, and procedures make safe performance realistically achievable, especially during unusual or degraded conditions.
Study instruction:
- Map the pressures surrounding a rushed task and identify which checks or safeguards are most likely to be bypassed.
Key point:
- Time pressure changes behavior and can make unsafe shortcuts seem necessary or rewarding.
- Design workloads and schedules so that safe work is feasible in practice.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
13. Q: What are deliberate violations, and how do they differ from unintentional errors?
A: Show answer
A deliberate violation is an intentional departure from a known procedure, rule, or expected behavior. The person knows that the action departs from the prescribed method, even if the person believes the risk is acceptable or sees no practical alternative. The chapter distinguishes these violations from unintentional slips and mistakes, which occur without an intention to depart from the correct procedure. Violations matter in accident analysis because they can contribute to harm, but they require a different inquiry: why did the person choose to deviate, what pressures or incentives shaped that choice, and was the rule workable? The distinction prevents investigators from treating every noncompliant action as an accidental slip.
Study instruction:
- Classify examples as unintentional slips, mistaken plans, or deliberate deviations and explain what evidence supports each classification.
Key point:
- An intentional rule violation is analytically different from an unintended action error.
- Investigate the motives, constraints, and incentives behind deliberate deviations.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
14. Q: What is the difference between routine violations and situational violations?
A: Show answer
Routine violations occur when departures from a rule become common and are tolerated as part of normal work. Because the behavior is repeated without immediate consequence, people may stop recognizing it as exceptional. Situational violations occur in response to a particular circumstance, such as time pressure, a perceived emergency, or an unusual obstacle. The chapter gives the example of a driver going through a red light because no other cars appear to be present and the driver is late. Both types can create risk, but they have different organizational implications. Routine violations suggest that the accepted way of working may have diverged from formal procedures; situational violations require analysis of the specific pressures and judgments at the time.
Study instruction:
- Find one routine and one situational violation in a work setting and identify the different corrective actions each would require.
Key point:
- Routine violations become normalized; situational violations arise from particular conditions.
- Do not assume a written rule describes how work is actually performed.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
15. Q: How can poorly designed rules encourage deliberate violations?
A: Show answer
Rules may be written primarily to demonstrate legal compliance rather than to reflect how work must safely be completed. If a procedure is impossible, excessively slow, outdated, or inconsistent with the equipment and operating environment, workers may have to bypass it to complete the task. When the shortcut succeeds, supervisors may praise the result, unintentionally reinforcing noncompliance. Over time, the formal procedure becomes disconnected from actual practice, and workers learn that following it is optional. The remedy is to review rules with the people who perform the work, understand operational constraints, revise impractical requirements, and ensure that safe compliance is achievable without sacrificing essential safeguards.
Study instruction:
- Compare a formal procedure with the actual steps workers use. Identify where the procedure creates pressure to bypass it and how it could be redesigned.
Key point:
- An impractical rule can create the very noncompliance it is intended to prevent.
- Align procedures with real work while preserving the safety objective.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
16. Q: How do organizational rewards and social norms reinforce unsafe violations?
A: Show answer
People learn from what an organization rewards, tolerates, and celebrates. If a worker who bypasses a safeguard completes a task quickly and receives praise, the organization may reinforce the shortcut even if the written policy prohibits it. If colleagues mock people who stop work or ask for help, individuals may conceal uncertainty and accept risks to preserve their reputation. Conversely, when safe decisions are publicly supported and workers are protected from unfair criticism for stopping a task, the social cost of caution decreases. Safety therefore depends on more than written procedures: it is shaped by incentives, leadership behavior, peer expectations, and the consequences of speaking up.
Study instruction:
- Review a workplace incentive system and identify whether it rewards safe process or only speed and output.
Key point:
- Informal rewards can undermine formal safety rules.
- Make safe decisions socially and operationally acceptable.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
17. Q: What is human error in the chapter’s terminology?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses human error as a broad term for a deviation from behavior considered appropriate. It acknowledges that “appropriate” can be uncertain and may only be determined after an event. The term therefore requires care: an investigator must establish what the person intended, what information was available, what the task required, and whether the expected action was realistic. The chapter then separates unintentional errors into slips and mistakes, while treating deliberate violations as a distinct category. This classification is useful because different mechanisms require different design responses. A generic label such as human error is not sufficiently precise to guide prevention.
Study instruction:
- Define the expected behavior in a case before deciding whether the action was an error, a reasonable adaptation, or a deliberate violation.
Key point:
- “Human error” is a starting classification, not a complete causal explanation.
- Identify the specific mechanism before selecting a corrective measure.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
18. Q: What is the difference between a slip and a mistake?
A: Show answer
A slip occurs when the person has the correct goal or plan but performs the wrong action or fails to carry out an intended step. The execution is flawed even though the intended outcome was appropriate. A mistake occurs when the person selects an incorrect goal, forms a faulty plan, misdiagnoses the situation, or evaluates the outcome incorrectly. In a mistake, the actions may be executed exactly as planned, but the plan itself is wrong. For example, pouring coffee into a refrigerator instead of placing the cup on a table is a slip; choosing an unsafe procedure because the situation was misdiagnosed is a mistake. The distinction directs attention to different design remedies.
Study instruction:
- Use several examples to identify whether the intention was correct and execution failed, or whether the goal or plan itself was inappropriate.
Key point:
- Slips are errors in execution; mistakes are errors in goals, plans, or evaluation.
- Diagnose the error type before deciding whether to redesign controls, information, or decision support.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
19. Q: How are slips divided into action-based slips and memory-lapse slips?
A: Show answer
Action-based slips occur when a person performs an unintended action, often because a familiar movement or response is triggered in the wrong context. Memory-lapse slips occur when a person forgets to perform an intended action or forgets to evaluate its result. The chapter’s examples include placing a coffee cup in the refrigerator instead of the intended object, and forgetting to turn off a stove burner. Both can happen even when the person knows exactly what should be done. Action-based slips often require more distinctive controls, objects, and mappings; memory lapses call for visible reminders, fewer steps, continuity information, or forcing functions.
Study instruction:
- Classify a set of everyday errors into action-based and memory-lapse slips, then propose a design intervention for each.
Key point:
- An incorrect action and an omitted action are different failure mechanisms.
- Design the remedy around whether the person did the wrong thing or forgot to do the right thing.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
20. Q: How are mistakes classified into rule-based, knowledge-based, and memory-lapse mistakes?
A: Show answer
Rule-based mistakes occur when a person selects or applies an inappropriate rule to a situation. Knowledge-based mistakes occur when an unfamiliar situation is misunderstood because the person’s knowledge or diagnosis is incomplete or incorrect. Memory-lapse mistakes occur when the person forgets a goal, plan, or earlier evaluation, so the overall course of action becomes wrong or incomplete. The categories distinguish a wrong rule, a faulty understanding, and a failure to retain the higher-level plan. They matter because a checklist may help with forgotten steps, while better diagnosis support and conceptual models are needed for unfamiliar or misclassified situations.
Study instruction:
- Create one example of each mistake type and explain which information or support would have helped prevent it.
Key point:
- Rule selection, situation understanding, and memory for goals are distinct sources of mistakes.
- Choose interventions that match the mechanism rather than applying generic retraining.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
21. Q: How does the seven-stage action cycle help locate slips and mistakes?
A: Show answer
The chapter relates error types to the seven stages of action introduced earlier in the book. Slips arise mainly during the execution stages or during perception and interpretation of the resulting state. Mistakes arise at higher levels when goals and plans are selected or when results are compared with expectations. Memory lapses can interrupt transitions between stages: the person may lose track of what was intended, what has been done, or what should happen next. This framework helps identify where a design failed to support the user. A poor control may cause an execution slip, while an unclear status display may lead to a mistaken evaluation or plan.
Study instruction:
- Map an incident onto the action cycle and mark whether the failure occurred during goal formation, planning, execution, perception, or evaluation.
Key point:
- The action cycle links error classification to the point where support is needed.
- Use the stage of failure to decide whether to improve planning information, controls, feedback, or continuity.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
22. Q: Why can skilled people experience more slips than beginners?
A: Show answer
Skilled people often perform familiar activities automatically, without directing conscious attention to every movement. This automaticity makes routine performance fast and efficient, but it can also allow a well-practiced action sequence to take over when the current task differs at a critical point. Beginners tend to pay more conscious attention because they have not yet automated the sequence, so they may make more deliberate planning mistakes but fewer automatic-action slips in some circumstances. This does not mean expertise is undesirable. It means that systems must protect experts from predictable slips, especially when a rare task resembles a familiar one.
Study instruction:
- Compare the attention demands of an expert and a novice performing a familiar task with one unusual step.
Key point:
- Expertise improves routine performance but can make automatic actions vulnerable to slips.
- Design rare or exceptional procedures so they are clearly distinguishable from familiar routines.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
23. Q: What is an action-based slip?
A: Show answer
An action-based slip occurs when the person intends one action but performs another. The wrong action may be triggered by habit, a similar control, a nearby object, or a competing action sequence. The person’s goal can be correct; the failure lies in translating that goal into the intended physical or interface action. Such slips are common in familiar environments where people act automatically. Distinctive controls, clear signifiers, appropriate spacing, physical constraints, and immediate feedback can reduce their likelihood. When a wrong action is reversible, undo mechanisms can limit its consequences.
Study instruction:
- Identify a device where two controls are easy to confuse and explain how their shape, spacing, labels, or feedback could be differentiated.
Key point:
- Action slips arise when execution departs from the correct intention.
- Make competing actions perceptibly different and provide feedback that exposes a wrong action quickly.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
24. Q: What is a capture slip, and why does it occur?
A: Show answer
A capture slip occurs when a more frequent or more recently performed activity takes over from the intended activity. The two action sequences share an initial portion, but the person continues along the familiar sequence at the point where they should have switched to the less familiar one. The chapter’s example of counting photocopied pages and unexpectedly switching to “Jack, Queen, King” illustrates how a recently used sequence can capture the current task. Capture slips are partial memory lapses and are more likely when attention is diverted at the point of divergence. They are particularly relevant to experts whose routine actions are highly automated.
Study instruction:
- Find two procedures that begin with identical steps but diverge later. Identify the point at which a capture slip could occur.
Key point:
- Familiar action sequences can capture a less familiar intended sequence.
- Make competing procedures different from the beginning, not only at the final step.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
25. Q: How should procedures be designed to reduce capture slips?
A: Show answer
Where possible, procedures with different outcomes should not share identical opening steps if those similarities can cause users to continue automatically along the wrong sequence. The chapter recommends making sequences dissimilar from the start. If the first steps must be shared, the divergence should be made conspicuous through a distinct prompt, physical cue, confirmation of the selected task, or other signifier. Rare procedures should not rely on memory alone when they resemble frequently performed routines. Designers should consider how the procedure will be used under interruption, fatigue, and time pressure, rather than only during careful training.
Study instruction:
- Redesign a pair of similar procedures so that the user receives an unmistakable cue before the sequences diverge.
Key point:
- Capture risk increases when familiar and unfamiliar routines share early steps.
- Differentiate the start of competing workflows and make the divergence explicit.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
26. Q: What is a description-similarity slip?
A: Show answer
A description-similarity slip occurs when a person performs the intended action on the wrong object because the target description is not sufficiently distinctive. The person may intend to place an item in one container but select another that shares the relevant features. The chapter’s example contrasts a laundry basket with a toilet: both can fit a vague description such as a large container, even though only one is the intended destination. The likelihood increases when objects look or function alike, when many alternatives are present, and when the person is tired, stressed, or overloaded. The action may be correct in form but applied to the wrong target.
Study instruction:
- List the visual and functional similarities between two objects that could be confused in a real task, then identify the distinguishing cues.
Key point:
- Ambiguous descriptions can cause a correct action to be applied to the wrong object.
- Make the intended target distinctive enough to separate it from plausible alternatives.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
27. Q: How can controls and displays be designed to prevent description-similarity slips?
A: Show answer
Controls and displays that serve different purposes should be distinguishable by more than their position in a uniform row. Designers can vary shape, size, color, texture, labels, spacing, grouping, and physical feel. The chapter describes cockpit controls that use different shapes for throttle, flap, and landing-gear functions so pilots can distinguish them by sight and touch. The design should also consider the user’s viewing angle, lighting, gloves, stress, and the possibility of selecting a control without looking directly at it. Distinctive coding reduces confusion, but it should be consistent and supported by clear feedback.
Study instruction:
- Inspect a control panel and identify groups of similar-looking controls. Propose redundant visual and tactile distinctions for critical functions.
Key point:
- Similarity among competing controls increases wrong-target selection.
- Use multiple meaningful cues to distinguish controls with different consequences.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
28. Q: What are memory-lapse slips, and what forms can they take?
A: Show answer
Memory-lapse slips occur when an intended action or a component of a procedure is forgotten. They can involve failing to complete a step, repeating a step, forgetting the result of an action, leaving an item behind, or abandoning a task because the original goal has been lost. The chapter gives examples such as leaving an original document in a copier, forgetting a child during a journey, misplacing a pen during an interrupted activity, and leaving a bank card in an ATM. These incidents do not necessarily reflect ignorance. They often arise because the task requires people to maintain several intentions and intermediate states in working memory.
Study instruction:
- Record the sequence of a task and mark each point at which an interruption could cause an intended action to be forgotten.
Key point:
- Memory-lapse slips are omissions or lost task steps, not necessarily failures of knowledge.
- Make task progress and outstanding actions visible outside the user’s memory.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
29. Q: Why are interruptions a major cause of memory-lapse errors?
A: Show answer
An interruption separates the decision to perform an action from the moment when it is completed. During that interval, another task or event competes for limited working-memory capacity. The user may return without remembering the original goal, the last completed step, or whether an action was already performed. Complex equipment can worsen the problem by requiring many operations between the start and finish of a task. A design that assumes uninterrupted attention is therefore fragile. Systems should preserve task state, display incomplete steps, and provide a clear way to resume after an interruption.
Study instruction:
- Choose a multi-step task and simulate an interruption halfway through. Note what information is needed to resume safely.
Key point:
- Interruptions create a resumption problem as well as a time cost.
- Design for interruption as a normal condition, not an exceptional event.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
30. Q: What design measures can reduce memory-lapse slips?
A: Show answer
Useful measures include reducing unnecessary steps, providing clear reminders at the point of need, making task status visible, and using forcing functions where an omitted step would create serious risk. The ATM example requires the card to be removed before cash is delivered because users are strongly motivated to retrieve the money and are therefore likely to notice the card prompt. A public pen can be attached to a counter so it cannot be carried away. These interventions move the burden from memory to the environment or the system. The best measure depends on the task: reminders may be adequate for low-risk omissions, while high-risk steps may require a constraint that prevents completion until the step is done.
Study instruction:
- Select a common omission error and compare a reminder, a checklist, and a forcing function as possible safeguards.
Key point:
- External cues and constraints can compensate for unreliable prospective memory.
- Use stronger safeguards when the consequences of omission are severe.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
31. Q: What is a forcing function, and how does it differ from a reminder?
A: Show answer
A forcing function makes it difficult or impossible to proceed until a required condition has been met, or until an unsafe action has been avoided. A reminder merely tells the user what should be done; the user can overlook or dismiss it. Requiring removal of an ATM card before cash is dispensed is a forcing function because the transaction sequence is designed around retrieving the card. A checklist prompt is generally weaker because the user may skip the item. Forcing functions are especially valuable for critical steps, but they must be designed carefully so they do not create a new hazard or block a necessary emergency action.
Study instruction:
- Classify safeguards in a process as reminders, warnings, constraints, or forcing functions and assess whether their strength matches the risk.
Key point:
- A reminder informs; a forcing function changes what actions are possible.
- Match safeguard strength to the severity and likelihood of the error.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
32. Q: What is a mode error?
A: Show answer
A mode error occurs when a device has different operating states, or modes, in which the same control has different meanings, and the user acts while believing the device is in another mode. A person may press a familiar control correctly but produce an unintended result because the system interprets it according to a hidden or misunderstood state. Mode errors occur in clocks, watches, computers, phones, entertainment systems, vehicles, and aircraft. They are particularly dangerous when the mode persists after the user’s attention has shifted elsewhere. The underlying problem is not simply user forgetfulness; it is a design that makes the active state difficult to perceive or remember.
Study instruction:
- Identify a device with multiple modes and describe one control whose meaning changes across those modes.
Key point:
- Mode errors arise when the user’s understanding of system state differs from the actual state.
- Make the active mode visible at the moment a control is used.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
33. Q: Why do designers introduce modes, and what trade-off do they create?
A: Show answer
Modes allow a limited number of controls to operate many functions, saving space, cost, and apparent complexity. A function selector and a setting control may replace numerous dedicated switches. The trade-off is that the user must know which mode is active and remember that the same control has a different effect in another state. The interface may look simpler while the cognitive task becomes more complex. Modes can be justified where space is limited or functions are rarely used, but designers should compare the apparent simplicity with the increased risk of mode confusion, especially in safety-critical applications.
Study instruction:
- Compare a dedicated-control design with a mode-based design. Evaluate space, cost, discoverability, memory burden, and error consequences.
Key point:
- Modes reduce physical controls but increase dependence on accurate state awareness.
- Do not equate fewer controls with easier or safer operation.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
34. Q: How can mode errors be reduced when modes cannot be eliminated?
A: Show answer
The active mode should be conspicuous, persistent, and unambiguous. It should be displayed near the control whose meaning changes, use wording or symbols that users can distinguish, and remain visible after interruptions or long periods of inactivity. Different modes should have clearly differentiated displays and control responses. Designers should avoid relying on a small indicator that can be overlooked or on the user remembering a selection made much earlier. Where mode changes have serious consequences, the system can require a deliberate confirmation or provide a preview of the action in the current mode before execution.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate a multi-mode interface and test whether a user can identify the active mode after being distracted or returning after a delay.
Key point:
- Mode awareness must be supported by the interface rather than left to memory.
- Make state persistent, local to the relevant control, and perceptually distinct.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
35. Q: What does the Airbus flight-control example teach about mode confusion?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes an aircraft system with modes for controlling vertical speed and descent-path angle. The pilots believed they had selected the angle mode, but the system was actually interpreting the entered value as a vertical speed. A number appropriate as an angle therefore commanded an excessively steep rate of descent. The resulting mode confusion contributed to a fatal accident. The display was later changed so that the two modes used visibly different numeric formats: four digits for vertical speed and two for angle. The lesson is that mode distinctions should be reflected in the representation of the value, not merely in a small mode label.
Study instruction:
- Explain why the same numeric input can be safe in one mode and hazardous in another, then identify a display change that would make the distinction unmistakable.
Key point:
- Mode-specific information must be represented so that the user can detect a mismatch before acting.
- Use differentiated formats and feedback for functions with different operating meanings.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
36. Q: How are skill-based, rule-based, and knowledge-based behaviors different?
A: Show answer
Skill-based behavior is highly practiced and largely automatic. Rule-based behavior is used when a situation is familiar enough to match a known procedure or learned if-then rule. Knowledge-based behavior is used when the situation is unfamiliar and the person must reason through the problem and develop a plan. The modes differ in how much conscious reasoning is required and in the kinds of errors they tend to produce. Slips are common during automatic skill-based performance; rule-based behavior can produce a wrong rule selection or an execution slip; knowledge-based behavior is vulnerable to misdiagnosis and faulty reasoning. The classification helps designers tailor support to the user’s task.
Study instruction:
- Classify three tasks from your own experience according to skill-based, rule-based, or knowledge-based behavior and identify their likely error modes.
Key point:
- Routine skill, familiar rules, and novel reasoning require different kinds of support.
- Design assistance around the cognitive demands of the task.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
37. Q: Why are slips especially associated with skill-based behavior?
A: Show answer
Skill-based actions are performed with little conscious attention because practice has made them automatic. This is efficient for routine tasks, but a familiar sequence may continue even when the current situation requires a small change. If the user is distracted at the point where the sequence should diverge, an automatic response may take over. The error is often a slip because the intended goal remains correct while execution goes wrong. The solution is not to make every expert consciously monitor every movement. Instead, systems should distinguish exceptional conditions, make unusual steps salient, and provide feedback that reveals an unintended action.
Study instruction:
- Find a routine task in which an automatic sequence could continue into the wrong context. Propose a design cue at the point of divergence.
Key point:
- Automaticity is useful but can make familiar routines vulnerable to context-dependent slips.
- Protect transitions between routine and exceptional actions.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
38. Q: What causes rule-based mistakes?
A: Show answer
A rule-based mistake occurs when a person applies the wrong rule or uses a rule that is unsuitable for the actual conditions. The initial situation may be misclassified, causing an inappropriate procedure to be selected. Alternatively, the rule itself may be incomplete, outdated, or based on assumptions that no longer hold. A person may also choose the correct rule but evaluate its result incorrectly, allowing the action cycle to continue on a false understanding. The central challenge is often diagnosis: once a situation has been placed in the wrong category, the selected rule can appear logical and the resulting actions may be executed competently.
Study instruction:
- Review a procedure-based decision and identify the assumptions that must be true before the rule is appropriate.
Key point:
- Rule-based mistakes can arise from wrong classification, defective rules, or faulty evaluation.
- Test both the diagnosis and the rule, not only whether the steps were followed.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
39. Q: How did the Kiss nightclub fire illustrate failures in rules and emergency design?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a fire in a Brazilian nightclub in which pyrotechnics ignited acoustic ceiling material and more than 230 people died. The account illustrates several errors: the band believed the flares were safe, some attendees mistook rest rooms for exits, and guards reportedly blocked exits because patrons sometimes left without paying. A rule intended to prevent unpaid departure had not been designed for an emergency. The chapter suggests that an alarm-triggering exit could deter misuse while still permitting escape. The broader lesson is that rules and physical arrangements must account for exceptional conditions, including the need to override routine controls during an emergency.
Study instruction:
- Analyze the conflict between preventing unauthorized departure and preserving emergency egress. Identify a design that addresses both objectives.
Key point:
- Rules designed for routine control must not obstruct emergency actions.
- Test procedures and physical controls against foreseeable abnormal conditions.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
40. Q: Why is the oven thermostat example a combination of conceptual-model error and memory lapse?
A: Show answer
Turning an oven to its maximum setting to make it reach the desired cooking temperature faster reflects a mistaken mental model of how the thermostat works. The user assumes that a higher setting changes the rate of heating rather than simply setting a higher target temperature. If the user then walks away and forgets to return to check the oven, a memory-lapse slip compounds the initial mistake. The example demonstrates that several error types can interact within one event. A useful design response would make the operating principle clearer, communicate actual temperature and heating state, and reduce the chance that a high setting remains unnoticed.
Study instruction:
- Separate the incorrect belief about the thermostat from the forgotten follow-up action and propose a different safeguard for each.
Key point:
