- One incident can contain both a planning mistake and an execution or memory slip.
- Identify each mechanism separately instead of assigning one label to the whole event.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
41. Q: What does the anti-lock braking example show about interpreting feedback?
A: Show answer
A driver unfamiliar with anti-lock brakes may feel the brake pedal vibrate during hard braking on a slippery road. If the driver interprets the vibration as a malfunction and releases the pedal, the response is inappropriate because the vibration is evidence that the system is operating as intended. The mistake lies in evaluating feedback through an incorrect mental model. A good design should make system behavior understandable before an emergency occurs and should provide clear information about what the user should do. Training and interface feedback should reinforce the meaning of normal system responses, not merely describe the hardware.
Study instruction:
- Explain how a correct system response can be misread as a failure and identify the user information needed before an emergency.
Key point:
- Feedback is useful only when users can interpret what it means.
- Build an accurate conceptual model of system behavior and communicate it in context.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
42. Q: Why are rule-based mistakes difficult to detect?
A: Show answer
Once a person has classified a situation and selected a rule, the resulting actions may be internally consistent with that decision. The person may receive evidence that appears to support the diagnosis while overlooking contradictory information. In complex environments, many signals compete for attention, and decisions may need to be made quickly. Human memory also favors recent, frequent, or distinctive experiences, encouraging a person to match a novel event with a familiar pattern even when important differences exist. Because the chosen rule can produce plausible short-term results, the original misclassification may remain hidden for hours or days.
Study instruction:
- Use a case with conflicting indicators to show how a plausible but wrong diagnosis can remain convincing.
Key point:
- A coherent sequence of actions does not prove that the original diagnosis was correct.
- Design displays and review practices that expose contradictory evidence.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
43. Q: How do recency, regularity, and uniqueness influence human diagnosis?
A: Show answer
People retrieve and interpret events partly through what they remember most readily. Recent experiences are often more accessible than older ones; frequent events are remembered through their regular pattern; unusual events are memorable because of their distinctiveness. These tendencies help people recognize familiar situations quickly, but they can also distort diagnosis. When a current event is genuinely novel, a person may still force it into the closest remembered category. The chapter emphasizes that this matching ability is powerful but imperfect. Decision support should therefore present current system state clearly and help users compare plausible explanations rather than relying only on memory.
Study instruction:
- Describe a situation where a recent or familiar event could bias the interpretation of a new problem.
Key point:
- Memory-based pattern matching is useful but can misclassify novel conditions.
- Make current evidence visible and support comparison with alternative explanations.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
44. Q: How should designers help decision makers manage too much information?
A: Show answer
Designers should organize information about the current state of a system so that it can be assimilated and interpreted quickly. In a complex plant, hundreds of alarms and minor faults may compete with the few signals that indicate a developing emergency. A useful display should prioritize relationships and trends, make significant deviations apparent, and support comparison of alternative explanations. It should not simply present more data or louder alerts. The chapter acknowledges that there is no easy solution because operators must distinguish important signals from normal variation under time pressure. The design goal is to support judgment without demanding that every event be scrutinized equally.
Study instruction:
- Sketch a control-room display that separates critical deviations from routine status information and explains how the prioritization is determined.
Key point:
- More information is not automatically better information.
- Present system state coherently and make relevant patterns and contradictions visible.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
45. Q: What is the hindsight bias, and why does it matter after an accident?
A: Show answer
Hindsight bias is the tendency to see an outcome as more obvious or predictable after it is known than it was beforehand. The chapter discusses research by Baruch Fischhoff in which people were poor at predicting outcomes when they did not know what happened, but judged the actual outcome as plausible after it was revealed. Accident investigators know the final result and can identify which signals mattered. Operators at the time faced uncertainty, workload, stress, and many competing signals. A fair investigation must reconstruct what information was available then, not judge the person as if the outcome had already been known.
Study instruction:
- Compare a decision made before an outcome with an evaluation made after the outcome is known. Identify what hindsight changes in the interpretation.
Key point:
- Knowing the outcome changes how obvious the earlier warning signs appear.
- Evaluate decisions using the information available at the time.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
46. Q: What is retrospective decision making, and how can it distort accident investigations?
A: Show answer
Retrospective decision making occurs when an event is reviewed with knowledge of the outcome. Investigators can separate relevant signals from irrelevant ones because they know which signals preceded the accident. Operators did not have that advantage and may have been overwhelmed by incomplete or contradictory information. A signal that looks obvious afterward may have had a routine explanation at the time. Good investigations therefore reconstruct the timeline, workload, training, available displays, prior experience, and competing demands. They should ask what a reasonable operator could infer at each moment, while still examining whether the system should have made the important signal easier to recognize.
Study instruction:
- Build a minute-by-minute timeline for an incident and list what the operator knew, did not know, and could reasonably infer at each point.
Key point:
- Retrospective clarity must not be confused with real-time certainty.
- Reconstruct the operator’s information environment before assigning responsibility.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
47. Q: Why do people sometimes explain away warning signs?
A: Show answer
People often encounter unusual signals that have plausible everyday explanations. Most anomalies are harmless, so dismissing them is often reasonable. The danger is that an explanation based on past experience may not fit the current situation. Once a person has accepted an explanation, subsequent evidence may be interpreted to support it. The chapter’s wrong-turn story illustrates this: increasingly frequent Las Vegas signs were explained as unusually extensive advertising, while the travelers had actually missed a turn. In industrial systems, a series of minor faults can be treated as unrelated until they combine into a major failure.
Study instruction:
- Identify an anomaly in a familiar process and generate at least two competing explanations, including one that would require escalation.
Key point:
- An apparently reasonable explanation can conceal a developing abnormal condition.
- Keep alternative explanations open when anomalies accumulate or contradict expectations.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
48. Q: How can organizations avoid dismissing a series of small anomalies?
A: Show answer
Organizations should make it possible to record, correlate, and review small events across shifts and departments. A single alarm may be low priority, but repeated alarms, recurring workarounds, minor equipment faults, or near misses may reveal a pattern. Operators need clear thresholds for escalation and a way to see whether similar signals have occurred elsewhere. Reviewers should distinguish normal variation from emerging trends and avoid treating each event in isolation. The objective is not to escalate every minor fault, which would overwhelm staff, but to preserve enough context to recognize when several individually explainable events form a meaningful pattern.
Study instruction:
- Design an anomaly log that captures time, equipment, operating condition, action taken, recurrence, and related events.
Key point:
- Small signals can become meaningful when viewed together over time.
- Preserve incident context and review recurring anomalies as patterns.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
49. Q: Why is designing for failure as important as designing for normal operation?
A: Show answer
A product may work well when users follow the intended sequence and no unusual event occurs, yet fail dangerously when a user makes a plausible mistake. The chapter argues that systems should be designed for the conditions in which things go wrong: incorrect commands, interruptions, unexpected states, and incomplete information. This means minimizing opportunities for error, checking whether actions are reasonable, making operations reversible where possible, improving error visibility, and supporting recovery. A robust design does not merely optimize the ideal path; it anticipates foreseeable deviations and limits their consequences.
Study instruction:
- Choose a product and describe how it behaves under normal use, mistaken input, interruption, and recovery.
Key point:
- Safety depends on how a system behaves when use is imperfect.
- Design and test failure paths, not only the ideal sequence.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
50. Q: What does it mean to treat a user action as an approximation rather than immediately as an error?
A: Show answer
The chapter proposes that systems should not always respond to an imperfect command with a blunt rejection or warning. In human conversation, people clarify ambiguous statements and infer intended meaning rather than treating every grammatical or factual imperfection as a fatal failure. Products can adopt a similar approach where safe: identify likely intent, ask for clarification when necessary, and guide the user toward a valid action. This does not mean silently guessing when the stakes are high. It means helping users complete their goal while preserving safeguards, explaining uncertainty, and avoiding unnecessary punishment for small deviations in input.
Study instruction:
- Find a user input that a system currently rejects. Propose a clarification or guided correction that preserves safety.
Key point:
- Helpful systems support recovery from imperfect inputs rather than merely declaring failure.
- Interpret intent cautiously and ask for clarification when consequences matter.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
51. Q: Why are interruptions and multitasking especially damaging to complex work?
A: Show answer
An interruption costs more than the time spent responding to it. The user must also reconstruct the goal, current stage, completed steps, pending actions, and state of the system. If that information was held only in working memory, resumption can be difficult or impossible. Deliberate multitasking further divides attention and can increase completion time, errors, and quality problems. The chapter cites examples involving driving and mobile-phone use, including reduced awareness while walking. In safety-critical work, concurrent tasks can interfere with the monitoring and judgment needed for the primary task.
Study instruction:
- Break a complex task into steps and identify what state information must be preserved whenever the user is interrupted.
Key point:
- Interruption creates a costly resumption task; multitasking can degrade both speed and accuracy.
- Preserve task state and protect attention during critical operations.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
52. Q: What is a sterile cockpit period, and where can the idea be applied?
A: Show answer
In aviation, a sterile cockpit configuration restricts conversation and activity unrelated to aircraft control during critical phases such as takeoff and landing. The purpose is to reduce interruptions when the pilots’ attention is most needed. The chapter notes that cabin crew communication may also be restricted, while recognizing that this can create a different risk if an emergency needs to be reported. Similar protected periods may help in medicine, industrial operations, and other safety-critical work. They should be carefully defined so that nonessential distractions are reduced without blocking urgent safety communication.
Study instruction:
- Identify the critical phases of a task in your workplace and define which interruptions should be deferred and which must always be communicated.
Key point:
- Protected attention periods can reduce distraction during high-risk task phases.
- Define clear exceptions for urgent safety information.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
53. Q: Why can warning alarms make a system less safe?
A: Show answer
Warnings can compete for attention when many devices use similar tones or lights, especially during an emergency when several alarms may activate together. If alarms frequently signal minor or already-known conditions, operators may learn to ignore them, silence them, disconnect them, or cover warning lights. This creates a dangerous situation if a critical warning occurs while the system is disabled or if the operator can no longer distinguish urgent signals. Effective alarm design must manage priority, meaning, timing, coordination, and workload. The purpose is not to maximize the number or loudness of alerts but to make important information noticeable and actionable.
Study instruction:
- Inventory alarms in a system and classify them by urgency, action required, frequency, and likelihood of masking other signals.
Key point:
- Uncoordinated or excessive alarms can create distraction and alarm fatigue.
- Prioritize meaningful alerts and manage the total alarm burden.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
54. Q: What makes a warning signal effective?
A: Show answer
A warning should attract attention without becoming so frequent, loud, or intrusive that users learn to ignore it. It should also communicate what condition has occurred and what response is appropriate, not merely announce that something is wrong. Different instruments should coordinate their signals so that simultaneous alerts do not overwhelm the operator. Visual and auditory cues should be selected according to the environment, the user’s attention, and the urgency of the condition. The chapter notes that speech warnings can convey precise information when visual attention is elsewhere, but they may fail in noisy settings or interfere with necessary conversation.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate a warning for detectability, clarity, urgency, actionability, nuisance rate, and interaction with other warnings.
Key point:
- An effective alarm both attracts attention and communicates useful meaning.
- Design warnings as part of a coordinated system, not as isolated beepers.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
55. Q: What are the strengths and limitations of speech-based warnings?
A: Show answer
Speech warnings can convey more specific information than a simple tone and can be useful when the user is looking elsewhere. They may tell the operator which condition has occurred or which action is required. However, speech can be masked by noise, misunderstood, or lost when several messages occur at once. It can also interfere with conversations that are themselves necessary for coordination. Designers should therefore consider the acoustic environment, message length, priority, repetition, and whether another channel can communicate the same information. Speech is a tool, not a universal replacement for visual or nonverbal alerts.
Study instruction:
- Compare a speech warning with a tone and a visual display for one operational scenario. Identify what each channel communicates best.
Key point:
- Speech provides detail but can compete with listening and conversation.
- Choose warning channels according to context and operator workload.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
56. Q: How can physical constraints prevent incorrect actions?
A: Show answer
Physical constraints make an unsafe or incorrect action difficult or impossible by using shape, size, position, or mechanical compatibility. The chapter describes vehicle fluid reservoirs with different openings and locations so that the wrong fluid is less likely to be added. Asymmetric screw holes can ensure that a component fits only in the correct orientation. Covers can protect critical switches from accidental activation. These are examples of error-proofing through the physical form of the product. Constraints are most effective when they prevent a hazardous mistake without blocking legitimate use or emergency response.
Study instruction:
- Inspect a physical assembly or maintenance task and identify where incompatible shapes or connectors could prevent an incorrect connection.
Key point:
- Physical constraints reduce reliance on memory and visual checking.
- Make the safe action easy and the hazardous action difficult or impossible.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
57. Q: How can electronic interfaces use constraints to reduce error?
A: Show answer
Electronic systems can separate controls, group functions, hide irrelevant options until needed, and require additional deliberate steps for high-consequence actions. A control panel can keep unrelated functions apart, while a screen can display only the controls relevant to the current operation. Such constraints reduce the chance that a user will select a similar or irrelevant control. They should be designed to preserve discoverability and avoid excessive navigation. The key is to reduce the number of plausible wrong actions at the moment of decision, while keeping the current state and available options clear.
Study instruction:
- Redesign a crowded screen by grouping related functions and temporarily removing controls that are irrelevant to the active task.
Key point:
- Interface constraints can reduce selection errors by limiting competing actions.
- Reduce unnecessary choices without hiding essential status or recovery options.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
58. Q: Why is Undo one of the most powerful tools for mitigating error?
A: Show answer
Undo reverses a previous operation and therefore reduces the cost of an unintended action. Multiple levels of undo are especially useful because a sequence of actions may need to be reversed as a unit. Undo is not possible for every physical or external action, and some operations can only be reversed immediately. Nevertheless, where reversal is technically and operationally feasible, it provides a strong recovery mechanism. Systems should make the availability and scope of undo clear, preserve enough history to support it, and distinguish reversible changes from actions that have already affected people, money, or external systems.
Study instruction:
- List actions in a software workflow that should be reversible and identify where reversal must be limited or require additional controls.
Key point:
- Reversibility reduces the impact of errors after they occur.
- Provide undo where possible and make irreversible actions especially clear.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
59. Q: Why are generic confirmation dialogs often ineffective?
A: Show answer
Confirmation dialogs frequently appear after users have already committed to an action. The user may focus on the command itself and respond automatically without carefully reading the prompt. If the dialog merely repeats “Are you sure?”, it may not help the user notice that the wrong object or window is selected. A more effective confirmation makes both the action and the affected object prominent, describes the consequence, and offers a clear way to cancel. Confirmation should be reserved for meaningful risk; repeated low-value prompts encourage habituation and reduce attention to important ones.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite a generic confirmation dialog so that it identifies the object, action, consequence, and safe alternative.
Key point:
- Confirmation works only when it directs attention to the actual consequence.
- Make the affected object and operation explicit instead of asking a vague question.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
60. Q: How can a confirmation dialog help detect a slip but fail to prevent a mistake?
A: Show answer
If a user accidentally invokes a command that was not intended, an unexpected confirmation dialog can reveal the slip because the prompt itself is surprising. The user can cancel the operation. But if the user has already formed an incorrect plan, the dialog may match what the user expects and will be accepted without careful reading. The chapter’s example of closing the wrong computer window shows that a user may intend to close a window but misidentify which window is active, then approve a confirmation without noticing the loss. The system should make the actual object more visible and preserve a recoverable copy where possible.
Study instruction:
- Create two scenarios: one where a confirmation reveals an accidental command and another where it fails to reveal a wrong target.
Key point:
- Unexpected prompts can expose slips; expected prompts may not challenge a mistaken plan.
- Combine meaningful confirmation with target highlighting and reversibility.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
61. Q: What are sensibility checks?
A: Show answer
Sensibility checks are automated checks that test whether a requested action or value is reasonable in context. A system can compare an input with normal ranges, known limits, previous patterns, or the expected scale of a transaction. The chapter gives examples of extreme radiation or medication doses and a currency-entry error that turns a modest transfer into a million-dollar instruction. A system that detects an implausible value can pause and ask for verification before execution. Sensibility checks do not prove that a value is wrong; they identify anomalies that deserve attention and should be designed to avoid excessive false alarms.
Study instruction:
- Define normal ranges and escalation thresholds for one high-impact data-entry field, and explain how exceptions will be reviewed.
Key point:
- Automated reasonableness checks can catch errors that users may not notice.
- Flag unusual values before execution, with a clear path to verify legitimate exceptions.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
62. Q: How should a sensibility check distinguish an error from a legitimate unusual action?
A: Show answer
A sensibility check should compare the input with relevant context rather than reject every value outside an arbitrary range. Context may include the user’s role, transaction history, patient or equipment parameters, approved limits, units, and the current operating condition. When a value is unusual but potentially valid, the system should request a meaningful review, display the entered value and its units, and require appropriate authorization if the consequences are high. The check should record the decision and allow a legitimate exception through a controlled process. Thresholds need periodic review so they remain useful as operations change.
Study instruction:
- Design an exception pathway for an unusual but valid transaction or operating value, including review authority and audit evidence.
Key point:
- An anomaly is a reason to verify, not automatic proof of error.
- Use context-sensitive checks and controlled exception handling.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
63. Q: Why should designers not simply require users to pay more conscious attention?
A: Show answer
Conscious attention is limited and cannot be sustained at a high level for every routine action. Skilled performance depends on automatic processes that allow people to type, walk, drive, and perform familiar tasks without consciously supervising every movement. Requiring constant attention would make work slower and less effective, while still failing to eliminate distraction. The chapter argues that design should support normal human processing rather than assume that users can permanently compensate through vigilance. Better approaches include distinctive controls, visible state, timely feedback, fewer unnecessary steps, and recovery mechanisms.
Study instruction:
- Identify a task where constant vigilance is currently expected. Explain how the design could reduce the need for sustained attention.
Key point:
- Vigilance is not a reliable substitute for good design.
- Make correct action natural and provide support when attention is diverted.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
64. Q: How can feedback reduce slips?
A: Show answer
Feedback helps users perceive what action actually occurred and what state the system entered afterward. If a user selects the wrong control, immediate visible or tactile feedback can expose the discrepancy before it causes harm. The chapter emphasizes feedback about both the action and the resulting state, combined with a way to undo an error. For example, medication scanning can verify the match between a prescription, the dispensed medicine, and the patient’s wristband. Feedback should be timely, perceptible, and relevant to the user’s goal. A silent or delayed state change leaves users unable to recognize that an unintended action has occurred.
Study instruction:
- Trace one interaction from control selection to system response. Identify the feedback needed to reveal an incorrect selection or result.
Key point:
- Visible feedback makes unintended actions easier to detect.
- Show both what the system did and the resulting state.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
65. Q: What is the Swiss cheese model of accidents?
A: Show answer
The Swiss cheese model, associated with James Reason, represents safety defenses as layers that each contain weaknesses or gaps. An accident occurs when the gaps in several layers align, allowing a hazard to pass through all defenses. Each layer may be a procedure, piece of equipment, training measure, supervision check, warning, or organizational control. No single layer is expected to be perfect. The model explains why a failure in one component should not automatically produce catastrophe if other defenses are effective. It also explains why complex accidents cannot usually be reduced to one person’s action or one defective component.
Study instruction:
- Draw a layered defense model for a process and identify the gaps that could align to produce harm.
Key point:
- Accidents occur when multiple defenses fail in a connected sequence.
- Build independent layers so one failure does not become a disaster.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
66. Q: How can the Swiss cheese model guide accident prevention?
A: Show answer
The model suggests three broad strategies: add effective layers of defense, reduce the number and size of weaknesses within each layer, and alert operators when several weaknesses appear to be aligning. Additional defenses might include independent verification, checklists, automatic interlocks, monitoring, and emergency response. Reducing weaknesses involves clearer controls, safer procedures, fewer error opportunities, and better maintenance. Alerts can help identify combinations of abnormal conditions before the final barrier is breached. The layers should be sufficiently independent; if all rely on the same information or assumption, one failure can defeat several defenses at once.
Study instruction:
- Select a high-risk process and propose additional independent defenses, then identify common dependencies that could defeat them together.
Key point:
- Layered defenses are strongest when their failure modes are not identical.
- Add barriers, reduce vulnerabilities, and detect when defenses are being eroded.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
67. Q: Why is redundancy important in safety-critical systems?
A: Show answer
Redundancy provides alternative means of preventing, detecting, or containing a failure. A single component or person should not be the only protection against a catastrophic outcome. The chapter connects aviation safety improvements to design redundancy and layers of defense. Redundancy may involve backup equipment, independent checks, separate sensors, multiple barriers, or a recovery procedure. However, simply duplicating the same weak design does not create true independence. Two controls that rely on the same incorrect input or the same misunderstood display can fail together. Redundancy should be designed so that one failure does not disable all protective layers.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate whether two safeguards in a process are genuinely independent or merely repeat the same assumption.
Key point:
- Redundancy reduces dependence on any single safeguard.
- Design independent protections rather than identical copies of one vulnerable control.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
68. Q: When can a person genuinely be at fault?
A: Show answer
The chapter acknowledges that people sometimes make choices for which individual responsibility is appropriate. Intoxication, serious fatigue, deliberate disregard of known safety requirements, or performing work without required competence can create culpable risk. Licensing, training, recency of practice, rest limits, and fitness-for-duty rules exist because some activities require specific capability. The chapter’s warning is against assuming that every accident is the person’s fault. A fair assessment distinguishes deliberate or clearly unreasonable conduct from predictable human limitations interacting with poor design. Even where individual accountability is justified, investigators should still examine whether the system could prevent similar harm.
Study instruction:
- Compare an unintentional slip, a deliberate unsafe choice, and an assignment of work to an unqualified person. Explain the evidence needed for each conclusion.
Key point:
- People can be responsible for some failures, but this does not excuse weak system design.
- Apply individual accountability proportionately while continuing to improve the system.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
69. Q: How do fatigue, sleep deprivation, and impairment affect safe performance?
A: Show answer
Fatigue and impairment reduce the ability to sustain attention, perceive hazards, make sound judgments, and respond quickly. The chapter compares strict limits on pilots’ drinking and flight hours with work environments, including hospitals, where staff may remain awake for long periods. The contrast raises a question about whether safety requirements are applied consistently across professions. Competence is not fixed: a qualified person may temporarily lose the capability needed for a task. Organizations should manage duty hours, rest opportunities, impairment risks, staffing, and escalation when a worker is not fit to perform safety-critical duties.
Study instruction:
- Review a safety-critical roster and identify how duty duration, night work, recovery time, and impairment reporting are managed.
Key point:
- Training and licensing do not eliminate the performance effects of fatigue or impairment.
- Treat fitness for duty as an operational safety control.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
70. Q: What is resilience engineering?
A: Show answer
Resilience engineering focuses on designing and managing systems so that people, equipment, procedures, and organizations can respond to changing conditions and recover from disruption. It is not limited to counting past errors and trying to reduce that number. A resilient organization anticipates that knowledge of risk is incomplete and that the environment changes. It continually tests its equipment, backup arrangements, communication, training, and response procedures. It also learns from normal operations and near misses, not only from disasters. The goal is to maintain essential functions and limit harm when unexpected events occur.
Study instruction:
- Assess one organization’s resilience by reviewing its ability to anticipate, respond, adapt, recover, and learn from disruption.
Key point:
- Resilience is the capacity to cope with complexity and changing threats, not simply the absence of recorded errors.
- Continuously test and improve the whole operating system.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
71. Q: How do organizations test resilience in complex technical systems?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes technology providers deliberately creating failures, such as shutting down critical facilities, to test whether backup systems and response teams work under realistic conditions. Such tests can reveal weaknesses that small simulations do not reproduce, including dependencies, communication delays, workload, and unexpected interactions. Live testing can itself create risk, so it requires careful scope, safeguards, monitoring, rollback arrangements, and clear authority to stop the exercise. The principle is that resilience cannot be established solely by documentation; it must be demonstrated under conditions that meaningfully challenge the system.
Study instruction:
- Design a controlled resilience exercise with a defined failure scenario, safety boundaries, success criteria, rollback plan, and post-test review.
Key point:
- Resilience claims should be tested, not assumed from the existence of backup plans.
- Use realistic exercises while protecting customers, staff, and essential services.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
72. Q: What is the paradox of automation described in the chapter?
A: Show answer
Automation can perform routine, repetitive tasks efficiently and often more accurately than people. It can reduce fatigue and free human attention for other work. Yet when conditions become unusual or complex, the automated system may fail or reach its limits precisely when human assistance is most needed. Meanwhile, the person may have stopped monitoring the process and may not immediately understand the changed state. Automation can therefore reduce routine workload while creating a difficult recovery problem. The design challenge is to support cooperation between people and machines rather than simply transferring easy tasks to automation and leaving humans with the hardest exceptions.
Study instruction:
- Identify the routine tasks automated in a system and the exceptional conditions that cause the system to need human support.
Key point:
- Automation can remove routine work while leaving people responsible for complex failures.
- Design for graceful handover and human-machine cooperation.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
73. Q: What does it mean for a human operator to be “out of the loop”?
A: Show answer
An operator is out of the loop when automation performs most of the active task and the human is no longer closely monitoring the system. If automation fails, the person may not notice immediately, may lack an accurate understanding of the current state, and may need time to diagnose the problem and decide how to respond. The risk is especially severe when the failure requires a rapid response. The chapter contrasts aircraft, where altitude may provide some time and pilots receive extensive training, with automobiles, where a driver may have only a fraction of a second. Handover design must account for the time needed to regain situational awareness.
Study instruction:
- Measure the time available after an automated failure and compare it with the time a human needs to detect, understand, and respond.
Key point:
- Human takeover is not instantaneous when the operator has been disengaged.
- Keep operators informed and provide realistic time and support for handover.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
74. Q: What does the Royal Majesty grounding illustrate about automation and mode awareness?
A: Show answer
The chapter recounts a cruise ship whose GPS antenna connection became disconnected. The navigation system switched to dead reckoning, estimating position from speed and direction, but the display did not make the change sufficiently apparent. The ship continued on an incorrect course for days and grounded on Cape Cod. Because the automated navigation had worked reliably for years, the crew trusted it and did not carry out the normal manual checks that might have exposed the problem. The case combines a hidden mode change, overreliance on automation, weak status information, and insufficient cross-checking.
Study instruction:
- Map the transition from GPS navigation to dead reckoning and identify the status cues and independent checks that could have exposed it.
Key point:
- Reliable automation can create overconfidence when its operating mode changes silently.
- Make degraded modes obvious and preserve independent verification.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
75. Q: How should people and machines be treated as a collaborative system?
A: Show answer
People and machines have different strengths. Machines can perform precise, repetitive calculations and monitoring consistently, while people can interpret context, recognize novel patterns, set goals, and adapt creatively. Problems arise when tasks are divided according to what can be automated rather than according to the complementary capabilities of each. Humans may be left monitoring machines for long periods or performing precise repetitive tasks for which they are poorly suited. A collaborative design uses automation for tasks it can reliably perform, gives people meaningful information and control, and provides support when the system encounters ambiguity or failure.
Study instruction:
- Create a task-allocation table showing which activities are best performed by automation, by people, or jointly, including the handover conditions.
Key point:
- Effective automation complements human strengths instead of merely offloading routine work.
- Design the human-machine partnership around capability, context, and recovery.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
76. Q: What does it mean to put required knowledge in the world rather than in the user’s head?
A: Show answer
Information needed to operate a product should be available in the environment or interface instead of depending entirely on memory. Labels, prompts, diagrams, visible state indicators, checklists, and contextual instructions can support both novices and experienced users returning to an infrequent task. Experts may perform routine operations without consulting these aids, but the information should remain available when needed. This is particularly important after a long absence, during interruptions, or when a rare procedure differs from a familiar one. External knowledge reduces memory demands and makes correct action less dependent on perfect recall.
Study instruction:
- Identify information currently memorized by users and redesign the task so that essential guidance is available at the point of action.
Key point:
- External representations support reliable performance when memory is weak or the task is unfamiliar.
- Make critical knowledge visible, timely, and easy to interpret.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
77. Q: How do physical, logical, semantic, and cultural constraints help prevent errors?
A: Show answer
Constraints limit the actions that are possible or make the correct action easier to infer. Physical constraints use shape, size, or fit; logical constraints make the available options follow from the task or system state; semantic constraints rely on the meaning of the situation; and cultural constraints use conventions and shared practices. The chapter encourages designers to combine natural and artificial constraints with forcing functions and natural mappings. Each type can reduce ambiguity, but a convention may vary across cultures and a logical constraint may be misunderstood if the user lacks the relevant model. The designer should select constraints that match the user, task, and risk.
Study instruction:
- Find one example of each constraint type in a product or workflow and explain how it guides or blocks action.
Key point:
- Constraints reduce the number of plausible incorrect actions.
- Use complementary constraints and verify that users understand their meaning.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
78. Q: How do the Gulfs of Execution and Evaluation relate to error prevention?
A: Show answer
The Gulf of Execution is the gap between what a person wants to do and how to discover the available actions. The Gulf of Evaluation is the gap between what the system has done and the user’s ability to understand the result. The chapter’s final design principles connect error prevention to bridging both gulfs. On the execution side, feedforward information makes options and possible actions visible. On the evaluation side, feedback makes the results and current system state apparent. If either gulf is large, users may choose the wrong action, act on a false assumption, or fail to notice an error.
Study instruction:
- Analyze one interface interaction and identify what feedforward information would help before acting and what feedback would help afterward.
Key point:
- Clear options support execution; understandable results support evaluation.
- Make both the available actions and the resulting state visible.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
79. Q: How can a designer apply the chapter’s principles to a high-risk workflow?
A: Show answer
A practical review begins by mapping the task and identifying likely slips, mistakes, memory lapses, mode errors, interruptions, and deliberate deviations. The designer then examines whether controls are distinguishable, procedures are workable, task state is visible, and important steps are protected by constraints or forcing functions. The workflow should provide timely feedback, reasonableness checks, reversible actions where possible, and clear escalation paths for anomalies. Multiple independent defenses should prevent one error from causing harm. Finally, the design should be tested under realistic workload, interruption, fatigue, and abnormal conditions, with reports and near misses used to refine it.
Study instruction:
- Apply this sequence to a real workflow and produce a risk-control matrix linking each error mechanism to a specific design safeguard and verification test.
Key point:
- Error-resistant design combines prevention, detection, recovery, and organizational learning.
- Match each safeguard to a known failure mechanism and test it in realistic conditions.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
80. Q: What should an organization do after an error or near miss is reported?
A: Show answer
It should preserve relevant evidence, protect people from immediate danger, record what happened, and create conditions for a fair investigation. The review should establish the sequence, intentions, system state, workload, interruptions, procedures, equipment, supervision, and incentives involved. Investigators should distinguish slips, mistakes, and deliberate violations, while avoiding premature blame. They should identify contributing factors and corrective actions, assign owners and deadlines, and verify whether changes reduce risk. Reporting should be encouraged because near misses and small errors provide information before a more serious event occurs.
Study instruction:
- Draft an incident-review workflow from initial report through evidence preservation, causal analysis, corrective action, and effectiveness review.
Key point:
- Reporting creates learning opportunities only when the organization acts on the information.
- Close the loop between reports, system changes, and verified risk reduction.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
81. Q: Why is it important to make error reporting easy and psychologically safe?
A: Show answer
People may conceal errors because they fear fines, dismissal, embarrassment, damaged relationships, or legal consequences. They may also hesitate to report another person’s mistake because of personal repercussions. Organizations can therefore have an incomplete picture of how work actually fails. The chapter argues that errors must be acknowledged and collected so that patterns can be identified and systems improved. Reporting arrangements should be accessible, trusted, and proportionate, with clear distinctions between honest mistakes, reckless conduct, and intentional misconduct. A reporting system that punishes every admission will suppress the very information needed for prevention.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate an error-reporting channel for accessibility, confidentiality, protection against retaliation, feedback to reporters, and clarity about how reports are used.
Key point:
- Fear and stigma hide the data needed for improvement.
- Encourage reporting while retaining fair processes for serious misconduct.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
82. Q: What is Jidoka in the Toyota Production System?
A: Show answer
Jidoka is described as automation with a human touch. When a worker notices something wrong, the worker is expected to report it and may stop the production line so that a defect does not proceed to the next stage. An andon cord or similar signal alerts the relevant experts, who investigate why the problem occurred and how to prevent recurrence. The philosophy treats quality problems as reasons to stop and learn rather than defects to pass downstream. It makes abnormal conditions visible and gives workers a recognized mechanism to trigger assistance. The chapter notes that reporting is expected and that failure to report errors can itself attract consequences.
Study instruction:
- Explain how a worker can signal an abnormal condition, who responds, and how the organization prevents the same defect from moving downstream.
Key point:
- Jidoka combines detection, authority to stop, and root-cause problem solving.
- Make abnormalities visible and empower timely intervention.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
83. Q: What is poka-yoke, and how does it relate to affordances and constraints?
A: Show answer
Poka-yoke means error proofing or avoiding error. It uses simple devices, fixtures, shapes, cues, or constraints to make an operation correct or prevent a common mistake. The chapter’s examples include asymmetric screw holes that allow a plate to fit only one way, protective covers over critical switches, and colored dots beside locks to indicate the correct turning direction. These techniques combine affordances, signifiers, mapping, constraints, and sometimes forcing functions. Poka-yoke does not depend on the user remembering a warning; it changes the task so that the error is less likely or less consequential.
Study instruction:
- Design a poka-yoke solution for a recurring assembly, filing, or maintenance error. Explain which affordance, signifier, mapping, or constraint it uses.
Key point:
- Error proofing embeds prevention in the physical or informational design.
- Prefer a design that makes the correct action natural over repeated reminders.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
84. Q: How did the NASA Aviation Safety Reporting System address barriers to reporting?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a voluntary aviation reporting system administered by NASA, an organization separate from airline employers and enforcement authorities. Pilots could submit reports about errors they made or observed. Contact information was used to obtain clarification, then detached from the report so the submitted information became semi-anonymous. The arrangement helped reduce fear that an employer or regulator would identify the reporter and impose a penalty for minor infractions. NASA could analyze recurring patterns and issue safety recommendations. The example shows that trust, independence, and protection from punitive consequences can be essential to collecting useful safety information.
Study instruction:
- Identify the features that made the reporting arrangement credible to pilots and compare them with the safeguards in another industry’s reporting process.
Key point:
- Independent administration and confidentiality can increase willingness to report.
- Design reporting systems around trust, learning, and appropriate protections.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
85. Q: How should errors be detected before they cause harm?
A: Show answer
Detection depends on whether the system provides observable evidence that something has gone wrong. Action slips are often easier to detect because the performed action differs from the intended action, provided the result is visible. Memory-lapse slips are harder because the missing action leaves no immediate object or event to inspect. Mistakes are harder still because the actions may be consistent with an incorrect plan. Systems should therefore make actions and state changes visible, display incomplete steps, compare results with expectations, and monitor for unusual patterns. Detection mechanisms should be designed around the error type rather than assuming every error will announce itself.
Study instruction:
- Build a detection plan for one slip, one omission, and one mistaken plan. Specify the signal that would reveal each before harm occurs.
Key point:
- Different error types leave different evidence and require different detection methods.
- Do not rely on users to notice an error that the interface does not reveal.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
86. Q: Why are action slips generally easier to detect than memory-lapse slips?
A: Show answer
An action slip usually produces something that can be observed: the wrong control is activated, the wrong object is moved, or an unexpected state appears. If the system provides timely feedback, the user or another person can compare the result with the intended action. A memory-lapse slip is an omission, so there may be nothing visible at the moment the action should have occurred. It may only become apparent when a later event reveals that a step was missed. This is why task-state displays, completion indicators, checklists, and forcing functions are especially important for omissions.
Study instruction:
- Compare the evidence left by an incorrect action and a missing action in a multi-step process.
Key point:
- Presence of a wrong action is often easier to observe than absence of a required action.
- Make omissions visible through progress tracking and completion controls.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
87. Q: Why are mistakes often harder to detect than slips?
A: Show answer
In a mistake, the user may execute the chosen plan correctly, so each action appears consistent with the current goal. If the original diagnosis or goal was wrong, monitoring the execution alone may reinforce confidence in the faulty plan. A mistaken interpretation may also fit some of the available evidence, making it difficult to recognize contradictory signals. Detection therefore requires checks on assumptions, goals, and outcomes, not just confirmation that each step was performed. Alternative explanations, independent review, trend displays, and decision checkpoints can expose a mistaken plan before it produces serious consequences.
Study instruction:
- Design a review checkpoint that challenges the diagnosis behind a plan rather than merely checking whether the planned steps were completed.
Key point:
- Correct execution can still produce harm when the plan is based on a wrong diagnosis.
- Verify goals and assumptions as well as individual actions.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
88. Q: What is the danger of explaining away mistakes?
A: Show answer
Explaining away occurs when an unusual observation is given a plausible reason and then dismissed. Most anomalies are harmless, so the explanation may be reasonable. However, a pattern of small anomalies can be an early indication of a larger problem. Once an explanation is accepted, people may stop looking for contradictory evidence. The chapter’s travel example shows how increasingly frequent signs for Las Vegas were rationalized until the travelers discovered that they had taken the wrong highway. In industrial settings, apparently unrelated malfunctions may similarly be discounted until they combine into a serious incident.
Study instruction:
- Review a recent near miss or maintenance log and identify any anomalies that were individually explained but might form a pattern.
Key point:
- Plausible explanations can delay recognition of a developing failure.
- Record anomalies and revisit explanations when new evidence appears.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
89. Q: How should accident investigators account for hindsight bias?
A: Show answer
Investigators should reconstruct the conditions as they appeared to the people involved, including what they knew, what they could see, how much time they had, and what other tasks competed for attention. They should distinguish signals that were truly available from facts discovered only afterward. The chapter recommends caution about immediate public explanations from people who lack substantive information and recognizes that reliable official investigations may take months or years. This approach does not excuse every decision; it creates a fair basis for judging whether the decision was reasonable given the information and constraints at the time.
Study instruction:
- Prepare an investigator’s checklist that separates contemporaneous evidence from information learned after the event.
Key point:
- Fair analysis avoids treating the final outcome as if it were obvious beforehand.
- Reconstruct the real-time decision environment before judging the operator.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
90. Q: How can a system make mistakes easier to reverse or recover from?
A: Show answer
Systems should provide undo or rollback where technically possible, preserve prior versions, and make high-consequence actions reversible until the user has reviewed the result. When reversal is impossible, the design should add safeguards before execution, such as a preview, staged release, independent authorization, or a controlled recovery plan. The chapter argues that a single simple error should not create widespread damage. Recovery design also includes clear procedures for stopping work, restoring a safe state, and communicating what has already happened. The goal is to reduce the cost of inevitable mistakes, not merely to prevent every mistake.
Study instruction:
- Identify irreversible actions in a workflow and propose a preview, approval, backup, rollback, or containment measure for each.
Key point:
- Recovery capacity limits the consequences of errors that prevention cannot eliminate.
- Make actions reversible where possible and contain the impact where they are not.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
91. Q: What role should confirmation play in high-consequence operations?
A: Show answer
Confirmation should focus attention on the actual object, action, and consequence, rather than ask a generic question. It is most useful when it helps the user detect a mismatch between intention and system state. The interface can highlight the target, state what will change, identify whether the operation is reversible, and offer a clear cancellation route. Repeated prompts for routine actions can become background noise, while a well-designed confirmation can interrupt an unsafe or unexpected action. For especially serious operations, confirmation may need to be combined with independent authorization and a reasonableness check.
Study instruction:
- Design a confirmation sequence for a destructive operation and explain why each prompt is meaningful rather than repetitive.
Key point:
- Confirmation should expose a consequential mismatch, not simply slow the user down.
- Use targeted confirmation with clear consequences and cancellation.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
92. Q: How should an organization prioritize error-prevention investments?
A: Show answer
Prioritization should consider the severity of potential harm, the likelihood and frequency of the error, the number of people or systems exposed, the detectability of the failure, and the effectiveness of possible safeguards. Recurrent low-severity slips may justify simple physical or interface changes, while rare catastrophic events may require independent barriers, forcing functions, emergency procedures, and resilience testing. Organizations should also consider whether a safeguard creates new hazards or excessive workload. The chapter’s overall approach is to reduce opportunities for error and minimize impact rather than pursue unrealistic promises of zero human error.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a risk-based prioritization table for five failure modes using severity, likelihood, detectability, exposure, and proposed control.
Key point:
- Prevention effort should reflect risk and the strength of available safeguards.
- Prioritize high-consequence failure paths and verify that controls work in practice.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
93. Q: How can a team evaluate whether a corrective action has actually improved safety?
A: Show answer
A corrective action should be evaluated against the mechanism it was intended to change. If the cause was a confusing control, test whether users now distinguish it under realistic conditions. If interruptions caused omissions, test whether task state is preserved and whether required steps are completed after interruptions. If a procedure was impractical, observe whether workers can follow it under normal workload without relying on workarounds. Teams should monitor recurrence, near misses, usability, and unintended consequences over time. A completed action item is not proof of effectiveness; the system must be observed after implementation.
Study instruction:
- Define before-and-after measures for a corrective action and specify when the team will review the evidence.
Key point:
- Implementation is not the same as demonstrated risk reduction.
- Verify controls under real operating conditions and watch for new failure modes.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
94. Q: What are the most important design lessons from Chapter 5?
A: Show answer
The chapter’s central lessons are to investigate causes rather than stop at blame; distinguish slips, mistakes, and deliberate violations; design around attention, memory, and cognitive limits; make modes and system states visible; use constraints and forcing functions to prevent predictable errors; preserve task information through interruptions; use checklists and reporting systems to support learning; make actions reversible and apply sensibility checks; and build multiple independent defenses. It also emphasizes that resilience and automation must be designed around changing conditions and human-machine cooperation. The goal is to assist people, not scold them, and to make the system safer even when people behave imperfectly.
Study instruction:
- Summarize the chapter under prevention, detection, recovery, organizational learning, and resilience. Give one concrete design action for each category.
Key point:
- Good design reduces error opportunities, exposes failures, limits harm, and supports learning.
- Treat human error as a design signal and improve the entire system.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
95. Q: How can the chapter’s principles be applied to medical equipment and clinical workflows?
A: Show answer
Clinical work combines complex decisions, interruptions, fatigue, high consequences, and multiple handoffs. Medical equipment should use sensible dose and range checks, clear units, patient-specific confirmation, distinctive controls, visible operating modes, and recoverable settings where possible. Workflow design should minimize interruptions during critical tasks, preserve information when staff hand over or resume work, and use collaboratively completed checklists for complex procedures. Reporting systems should capture near misses and errors without discouraging honest disclosure. These safeguards complement professional judgment and clinical standards; they do not replace training, qualified staffing, or local safety protocols.
Study instruction:
- Choose a medication, infusion, or imaging workflow and map where patient identity, units, dose, mode, interruption, and handoff errors could occur.
Key point:
- Clinical safety depends on interface design, workflow, communication, and organizational learning together.
- Use layered safeguards at every point where an error could reach the patient.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
96. Q: How can the chapter’s principles be applied to financial and administrative systems?
A: Show answer
Financial systems should anticipate mistyped amounts, wrong currencies, incorrect recipients, duplicate actions, and selection of the wrong account or period. Sensibility checks can flag values outside normal ranges, while confirmation should display the amount, currency, beneficiary, and consequence. Reversible drafts, approval stages, transaction limits, duplicate detection, and independent review can contain mistakes before funds move. Interfaces should preserve work after interruptions and clearly show which record or reporting period is active. These controls should be proportionate to the transaction risk and should support legitimate exceptions through documented approval rather than silently blocking valid work.
Study instruction:
- Review a payment workflow and propose safeguards for wrong amount, currency, beneficiary, duplicate payment, and interrupted approval.
Key point:
- Financial errors often arise from predictable input and selection problems that systems can detect.
- Combine reasonableness checks, clear target identification, approval, and recoverability.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
97. Q: What should a designer test when evaluating a product for human error?
A: Show answer
Testing should go beyond whether users can complete the ideal task in a quiet environment. It should include novices and experts, infrequent users, interruptions, fatigue where ethically and safely simulated, time pressure, mode changes, similar controls, unusual inputs, partial failures, and recovery after an error. Observers should record not only whether an error occurred but what conditions preceded it and whether the interface made it visible. Tests should also assess alarm burden, procedure workability, and whether users can resume a task without reconstructing hidden state. The results should inform iterative redesign rather than merely classify users as careless.
Study instruction:
- Create a usability and safety test plan that includes normal use, slips, mistaken assumptions, interruptions, exceptions, and recovery.
Key point:
- Realistic testing exposes failure modes that ideal-path demonstrations miss.
- Test the system under foreseeable imperfect conditions, not only perfect use.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
98. Q: How can managers balance productivity targets with safe operating behavior?
A: Show answer
Managers should examine whether staffing, schedules, output targets, maintenance windows, and performance incentives make safe work achievable. If employees must bypass procedures to meet deadlines, the organization has created a conflict between formal expectations and practical rewards. Leaders should protect stop-work authority, recognize early reporting, and avoid treating a safety pause as poor performance. They should also ensure that critical checks and rest requirements are built into plans rather than left to individual discretion. Productivity should be measured alongside quality, safety, and long-term reliability, not at the expense of them.
Study instruction:
- Review a performance dashboard and identify whether it rewards output while hiding workarounds, near misses, or deferred maintenance.
Key point:
- Unsafe shortcuts can be a predictable response to conflicting organizational demands.
- Align performance incentives with safe, sustainable execution.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
99. Q: Why should a safety system include both prevention and mitigation?
A: Show answer
Prevention reduces the likelihood that an error will occur, while mitigation limits harm if prevention fails. A physical constraint may stop an incorrect connection, but a sensor and alarm may still be needed if the connection is forced or a component fails. An independent shutdown, emergency response, and recovery plan provide further protection. The Swiss cheese model explains why no single safeguard should be assumed perfect. Systems should combine measures that reduce error opportunities with measures that detect failures and contain their consequences. This layered approach is more robust than relying on training or a single warning.
Study instruction:
- Classify the controls in a process as preventive, detective, mitigating, or recovery controls and identify any missing layer.
Key point:
- Prevention cannot eliminate every failure; mitigation limits the consequences.
- Build a balanced defense that covers before, during, and after an error.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
100. Q: How can a checklist avoid becoming a bureaucratic burden?
A: Show answer
A checklist should cover essential items without reproducing every detail of the procedure or forcing an artificial order. It should be developed iteratively with subject-matter experts and professional designers, tested in the actual work environment, and refined when users identify omissions, ambiguity, or unnecessary steps. The checklist should reflect the task’s real dependencies: mandatory sequence should be enforced where required, while independent items can be completed in a flexible order. Poorly designed checklists may be resisted for good reasons, so feedback from users is essential. The objective is reliable completion, not paperwork for its own sake.
Study instruction:
- Pilot a checklist with intended users and record which items are unclear, redundant, mistimed, or impossible to complete in the listed order.
Key point:
- Checklist quality depends on usability and fit with real work.
- Iterate the checklist until it is complete, clear, and proportionate.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
101. Q: Why is a jointly performed checklist often stronger than separate execution and later review?
A: Show answer
In a collaborative checklist, one person reads the item while another performs or verifies the action, allowing the instruction and the work to be connected in real time. A later independent check can be weakened by assumptions on both sides: the first person may work too quickly because someone else will check, and the second may review superficially because the first person is trusted. Joint performance makes responsibilities explicit and reduces the diffusion of responsibility that can arise when many people assume someone else will detect a problem. Roles should be clear, and the checker must be empowered to question the action regardless of seniority.
Study instruction:
- Design a two-person checklist protocol and specify who reads, performs, confirms, records, and escalates each item.
Key point:
- Collaborative checking links the instruction directly to the action.
- Assign explicit roles and make verification an active task, not a passive second look.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
102. Q: How can an organization prevent checklist use from weakening professional judgment?
A: Show answer
A checklist should support memory and consistency, not replace expertise or critical thinking. Users need to understand why each item matters, when a condition requires escalation, and how to respond when the checklist does not fit the actual situation. The list should allow for documented exceptions where appropriate and should not pressure users to mark an item complete when evidence is missing. Leaders should explain that checklists protect competent professionals from predictable slips under stress and interruption. Professional judgment remains necessary for diagnosis, interpretation, and decisions outside the checklist’s scope.
Study instruction:
- Identify tasks where a checklist can standardize routine checks but cannot decide the underlying professional question.
Key point:
- A checklist is a cognitive aid, not a substitute for expertise.
- Preserve judgment and escalation when conditions fall outside the checklist.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
103. Q: How can digital checklists improve on printed sequential lists?
A: Show answer
Electronic checklists can record completed and skipped items, preserve the current state, and prevent a task from being marked complete while required items remain unfinished. This is useful when some checks depend on external events, such as refueling being completed before the fuel quantity can be verified. A printed list may force users to skip an item and remember to return to it, creating a memory-lapse risk. Digital systems can support flexible ordering where dependencies allow it, while enforcing sequence where safety requires it. They should also provide clear status, offline or backup arrangements, and recovery if the device fails.
Study instruction:
- Map which checklist items are sequentially dependent and which can be completed in any order, then design digital status behavior for both.
Key point:
- Digital checklists can preserve skipped-item state and support non-linear task completion.
- Enforce only genuine dependencies and make incomplete items visible.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
104. Q: What is the difference between an alarm, a reminder, and a checklist?
A: Show answer
An alarm signals a condition that needs attention, often because a threshold or abnormal state has been reached. A reminder prompts a person to perform an intended action at a particular time or point in a workflow. A checklist is a structured aid for confirming that multiple required steps or conditions have been addressed. These tools solve different problems and can complement one another. An alarm cannot reliably track every procedural step, a reminder may be ignored, and a checklist may not detect a changing abnormal condition unless it includes relevant checks. Selecting the right aid requires understanding the failure mechanism and timing.
Study instruction:
- Choose a task with multiple steps and identify where an alarm, reminder, checklist, or combination is most appropriate.
Key point:
- Different cognitive aids support different information and timing needs.
- Choose the tool that matches the type of error and the point at which it can be detected.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
105. Q: How should organizations respond when employees resist checklists?
A: Show answer
Resistance may reflect concerns about professional status, unnecessary bureaucracy, poor timing, excessive length, or a belief that experienced people should not need prompts. Organizations should investigate these concerns instead of assuming that all objections are irrational. Involve users in design, demonstrate the evidence for the checklist, remove redundant items, and explain how the tool protects both novices and experts under stress. Senior leaders should model its use and make clear that following a checklist is a sign of disciplined practice rather than incompetence. The organization should also monitor whether the checklist improves performance in actual use.
Study instruction:
- Interview checklist users and separate objections about the concept from problems caused by the particular checklist design.
Key point:
- Resistance can reveal poor implementation as well as cultural attitudes.
- Improve the tool with users and establish visible leadership support.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
106. Q: How can an organization avoid diffusion of responsibility when several people check the same task?
A: Show answer
Adding more reviewers does not automatically improve reliability. When each person assumes that others have already checked or will check later, individual attention may decline. The chapter describes this as a paradox of group checking: more people can produce less careful review if responsibility is vague. The remedy is to assign specific ownership for each check, define what evidence the checker must inspect, and make the verification active. A collaborative checklist is one approach because it connects each instruction with a named performer and verifier. Independent checks should be genuinely independent and should not become ceremonial sign-offs.
Study instruction:
- Create a responsibility matrix for a multi-person inspection and identify how each reviewer demonstrates that a check was actually performed.
Key point:
- More reviewers do not help when each assumes another person is responsible.
- Define individual accountability and evidence for every verification step.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
107. Q: How can a reporting system protect learning while handling serious misconduct?
A: Show answer
A learning-oriented reporting system should make it easy to disclose honest mistakes and near misses, while retaining a fair process for deliberate, reckless, or knowingly harmful conduct. The chapter emphasizes that punishment for every error suppresses information, but it also recognizes deliberate violations as a distinct issue. Organizations should define what information is collected, who can access it, how confidentiality is protected, and which circumstances require formal investigation or external reporting. Reporters should receive feedback about how their information is used. The system must avoid both extremes: punitive concealment and an absence of accountability.
Study instruction:
- Draft reporting principles that distinguish honest error, at-risk behavior, routine rule violations, and intentional misconduct.
Key point:
- Learning and accountability are compatible when the categories and processes are clear.
- Protect honest reporting while maintaining proportionate responses to deliberate wrongdoing.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
108. Q: How can a design team use near-miss information to improve a product?
A: Show answer
Near misses reveal weaknesses before they produce injury or major loss. A design team can group reports by task, control, mode, user type, environment, and sequence of events to identify recurring patterns. It should examine whether the same error occurs across different people, whether feedback is missing, whether controls are similar, and whether workarounds have become normal. The team can then prototype changes, test them with users, and monitor whether the pattern decreases without creating new problems. Near-miss data is most useful when reporters trust the process and when the organization closes the loop by communicating improvements.
Study instruction:
- Develop a near-miss analysis template that captures task context, error mechanism, contributing conditions, severity potential, and proposed design change.
Key point:
- Near misses are early evidence of system vulnerability.
- Turn reports into patterns, redesign, testing, and feedback to reporters.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
109. Q: What are the risks of relying on training as the primary response to recurring errors?
A: Show answer
Training can improve knowledge and skill, but it cannot compensate reliably for confusing controls, impossible procedures, hidden modes, excessive workload, poor staffing, or conflicting incentives. If the system continues to invite the same error, a training session may produce only temporary improvement. The chapter criticizes “blame and train” when training is used instead of redesign. Training is appropriate when a genuine knowledge or competence gap is identified, but it should be paired with changes to the task and environment where those factors contribute. The effectiveness of training should be tested in actual operating conditions, not inferred from attendance.
Study instruction:
- Choose a recurring error and identify which parts could be addressed by training and which require equipment, interface, procedure, or organizational redesign.
Key point:
- Training addresses capability gaps but does not repair defective system design.
- Use training as one layer, not as a substitute for correcting the underlying cause.
Source links:
