- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
110. Q: How can an organization tell whether a procedure is realistic?
A: Show answer
A realistic procedure can be completed by qualified staff using the available equipment, time, staffing, and information under the conditions in which the work actually occurs. It should reflect dependencies, interruptions, abnormal conditions, and the need for emergency action. If workers routinely skip steps, use informal workarounds, or rely on local knowledge to complete the task, the procedure may not match reality. Observation, worker interviews, task analysis, and near-miss review can reveal these gaps. A procedure should then be revised and tested with the people who use it, while preserving essential safety requirements.
Study instruction:
- Observe a task from start to finish and compare actual work with the written procedure. Record every deviation and its reason.
Key point:
- A procedure is only effective if it can be followed in real operating conditions.
- Validate procedures through observation and user participation.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
111. Q: How should a designer handle a necessary but dangerous irreversible action?
A: Show answer
If an action cannot be undone, the system should reduce the chance of accidental initiation and make its consequences unmistakable. Possible safeguards include a staged process, a preview of the target and effect, independent approval, physical or logical interlocks, transaction limits, a delay that permits cancellation, and a recovery plan. The level of protection should reflect the severity and reversibility of the outcome. Excessive confirmation can become routine and ineffective, so each safeguard should provide distinct value. The design should also preserve an audit trail of who initiated, reviewed, and authorized the action.
Study instruction:
- Choose an irreversible operation and design a layered pre-execution safeguard and post-execution recovery plan.
Key point:
- Irreversible actions require stronger prevention and clearer consequence awareness.
- Use staged commitment, independent review, and traceable authorization.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
112. Q: What does the chapter imply about the relationship between usability and safety?
A: Show answer
Usability and safety are closely connected because a difficult interface increases the likelihood of selection errors, misinterpretation, omissions, and workarounds. Clear mappings, visible modes, distinctive controls, meaningful feedback, and accessible information help users act correctly and recognize problems. However, a superficially simple interface can hide complexity if it relies on modes or requires the user to remember state. Safety therefore depends on the quality of the underlying interaction, not merely on a clean appearance. Good usability makes safe behavior easier while preserving the information needed for complex decisions.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate an interface for both ease of routine use and visibility of state, exceptions, and recovery options.
Key point:
- An interface can look simple yet impose hidden cognitive work.
- Assess usability by the quality of action, understanding, and recovery.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
113. Q: How should designers decide whether to eliminate a mode or improve its visibility?
A: Show answer
Eliminating modes is preferable when the same functions can be provided through dedicated controls or a clearer task structure without creating excessive physical or visual complexity. Where space, cost, or functional density makes modes necessary, the active mode must be visible, persistent, and closely associated with the control whose meaning changes. Designers should also test whether users can recognize a mode after interruption or long delay. The decision should consider error severity, frequency of mode switching, similarity between modes, and the user’s ability to recover from a wrong selection.
Study instruction:
- Compare mode elimination with mode redesign for a device and justify the choice using risk, complexity, and space constraints.
Key point:
- Mode design is a trade-off between control economy and state-awareness burden.
- Eliminate unnecessary modes and make necessary ones unmistakable.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
114. Q: How can an organization recognize that automation has created a new risk?
A: Show answer
Warning signs include operators who rarely monitor the system, difficulty explaining its current mode, delayed recognition of failures, loss of manual skill, and handover procedures that assume instant human understanding. Automation may also hide degraded performance or switch to a fallback mode without a clear signal. Organizations should test failures and mode changes, observe how quickly operators regain situational awareness, and ensure that manual procedures remain practiced. Automation should be assessed not only by routine efficiency but also by the quality of its failure behavior and human handover.
Study instruction:
- Conduct a failure-mode review of an automated process and measure detection time, diagnosis time, response time, and manual recovery capability.
Key point:
- Automation changes the distribution of work and creates new monitoring and handover risks.
- Evaluate failure conditions and human readiness, not just normal automated performance.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
115. Q: What is the danger of overreliance on a system that has worked reliably for years?
A: Show answer
Long periods of successful operation can create trust that discourages independent checking. Users may stop examining displays, verifying inputs, or maintaining manual skills because they expect the automation to be correct. If a sensor disconnects or the system changes mode, the failure may persist unnoticed. The Royal Majesty example illustrates how routine success can make a hidden transition to dead reckoning difficult to detect. Trust should be supported by transparent system status, independent verification, and periodic tests rather than by a history of success alone.
Study instruction:
- Identify which independent checks have been dropped because an automated system is considered reliable and assess whether they should be restored.
Key point:
- Reliability can encourage complacency when system status is not independently verified.
- Maintain meaningful cross-checks and expose degraded or fallback operation.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
116. Q: How can a system support a safe transition from automation to human control?
A: Show answer
The system should detect when it can no longer perform reliably, communicate the nature and urgency of the problem, and provide enough time for the operator to understand the current state. It should identify what automation has already done, what remains active, and what control authority is being transferred. The operator should receive actionable information rather than a generic failure alert. Training and practice should include degraded modes, and the interface should avoid abrupt handovers that require instant diagnosis. If there is insufficient time for human intervention, the system needs automatic fallback or containment measures.
Study instruction:
- Design a handover sequence for an automated vehicle or industrial process, including warning, state summary, time allowance, and fallback behavior.
Key point:
- Safe handover requires awareness, time, authority, and a fallback plan.
- Do not assume a human can instantly take over an unfamiliar or rapidly changing system.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
117. Q: How can the chapter’s ideas improve the design of everyday household products?
A: Show answer
Household products can reduce errors through clear control-to-function mapping, distinct knobs and switches, visible operating states, and feedback that confirms the selected action. The chapter’s stove example shows how ambiguous control placement can cause users to activate the wrong burner; teaching the mapping is less effective than redesigning it. Appliances should also make dangerous settings conspicuous, prevent accidental activation where appropriate, and support recovery from common mistakes. Labels and instructions are useful, but physical layout and meaningful constraints are often stronger because they guide action at the moment of use.
Study instruction:
- Inspect a stove, washing machine, or home entertainment system and identify one likely slip, one mode error, and one improvement for each.
Key point:
- Everyday products should communicate their controls and states without requiring users to memorize hidden relationships.
- Improve mapping, distinctiveness, feedback, and constraints at the point of use.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
118. Q: How can the chapter’s ideas improve safety in transportation?
A: Show answer
Transportation systems combine dynamic environments, time pressure, automation, communication, and high consequences. Safety improves when controls and modes are clear, warning systems are coordinated, operators can recognize abnormal states, and procedures protect attention during critical phases. Checklists, independent verification, training, rest limits, and incident reporting add further layers. Designers should also account for weather, visibility, traffic, and degraded equipment rather than assuming ideal conditions. The chapter’s aviation examples show that social hierarchy, schedule pressure, and hidden system modes can be as important as technical component failure.
Study instruction:
- Select a transportation incident and analyze the contribution of design, workload, automation, communication, and organizational pressure.
Key point:
- Transport safety depends on technical defenses and the human conditions in which they are used.
- Combine clear interfaces, operational discipline, and independent safety barriers.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
119. Q: How should a designer distinguish an honest mistake from a deliberate violation during analysis?
A: Show answer
The designer or investigator should examine whether the person intended to follow the prescribed process, what the person believed the situation required, and whether the action was consciously chosen as a departure from the rule. An accidental wrong selection, forgotten step, or misdiagnosis is different from knowingly bypassing a procedure. Yet the distinction does not end the analysis: a deliberate deviation may still be encouraged by impossible rules, production pressure, or a culture that rewards shortcuts. Evidence should come from the sequence, interviews, procedure design, normal work practices, and organizational expectations rather than assumptions about motive.
Study instruction:
- Prepare interview questions that establish intent, awareness of the rule, available alternatives, and pressures at the time of a deviation.
Key point:
- Intent matters, but context still explains why a deliberate deviation occurred.
- Classify the behavior accurately and examine the conditions surrounding it.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)
120. Q: What should a final safety review ask before approving a new product or procedure?
A: Show answer
A final review should ask whether the system supports correct action, reveals its current state, detects implausible inputs, preserves progress through interruption, and allows recovery from foreseeable errors. It should check whether similar controls are distinguishable, modes are visible, warnings are coordinated, checklists are usable, and emergency actions remain possible. It should also test whether staffing, timing, training, and incentives make safe operation realistic. Finally, the review should confirm that errors and near misses can be reported, investigated, and translated into verified improvements. Approval should be based on evidence from realistic testing, not solely on the absence of observed problems in ideal demonstrations.
Study instruction:
- Use these questions as a design-review checklist and document evidence for each requirement before approval.
Key point:
- Safety review must examine people, technology, procedures, and organizational context together.
- Approve designs on demonstrated resilience, not assumptions of flawless use.
Source links:
- Chapter 5: Human Error? No, Bad Design (provided source file)

Table 6
Design Thinking and Human-Centered Product Development
1. Q: What is design thinking, and why should a designer question the problem before trying to solve it?
A: Show answer
Design thinking is an iterative way of discovering the real human problem and then exploring and testing possible responses before committing to a solution. The request presented to a design team is often a symptom, a proposed solution disguised as a problem, or a narrow interpretation of a more complex situation. A technically brilliant response can therefore fail if it optimizes the wrong objective. Designers begin by investigating what people are trying to accomplish, the circumstances in which they act, and the obstacles they encounter. They deliberately broaden the inquiry before narrowing it to a defensible problem statement. Only after that definition has been tested do they explore solutions and converge on a proposal. The discipline is useful beyond professional design: innovators in engineering, science, business, and public services can all benefit from challenging the initial framing rather than treating it as unquestionable.
Study instruction:
- Take a design brief and identify which parts describe an observed need, which parts are assumptions, and which already prescribe a solution.
Key point:
- Solve the underlying human problem, not merely the task as initially assigned.
- A polished solution to the wrong problem can make matters worse.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
2. Q: How can a stated design problem be distinguished from its underlying root problem?
A: Show answer
A stated problem is the issue as a stakeholder currently describes it; a root problem is the underlying condition that explains why people are struggling to achieve a goal. For example, a request to add more buttons to a machine may actually reflect poor visibility of its current state, unclear feedback, or a workflow that does not match the user's activity. Designers should ask why the difficulty occurs, observe the task in context, examine workarounds, and trace consequences rather than accepting the proposed feature as the need. The Five Whys technique can help reveal causal layers, but it should be used as an inquiry aid rather than a mechanical rule: complex situations may have several interacting causes, and each answer needs evidence. A credible problem definition explains who experiences the difficulty, in what circumstances, what goal is blocked, and why the difficulty matters.
Study instruction:
- Apply a Five Whys analysis to a real product complaint, then validate each causal assumption through observation or evidence.
Key point:
- A complaint or requested feature is a clue, not proof of the root problem.
- Root-cause discovery requires context and validation.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
3. Q: What is human-centered design, and what outcomes is it intended to achieve?
A: Show answer
Human-centered design (HCD) is a disciplined process for ensuring that a product or service fits people's needs, capabilities, goals, and real circumstances. It aims for more than technical operation. A successful design should support the intended task, be understandable and usable, work reliably, and provide an experience that people can value or enjoy. It must also coexist with constraints such as cost, efficiency, appearance, manufacturability, serviceability, and business viability. HCD places human needs and capabilities at the center of the inquiry while recognizing that a product exists within a larger social and technical system. It is not a final usability inspection added after engineering; it shapes how the problem is discovered, how requirements are framed, how ideas are explored, and how the evolving solution is tested. Its central discipline is to solve the right problem in a way that people can successfully use.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate a familiar product against need satisfaction, understandability, usability, reliability, and the quality of the use experience.
Key point:
- HCD connects problem definition and solution development to people's actual needs and abilities.
- Usability is one necessary quality among several interacting design requirements.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
4. Q: What are the four stages of the double-diamond model, and how do they relate to the two design problems?
A: Show answer
The double-diamond model separates design into two broad phases, each with a divergence followed by convergence. In the first diamond, Discover broadens understanding of the initial brief by investigating people, contexts, and possible underlying issues; Define narrows that learning into a well-supported problem statement. In the second diamond, Develop expands the range of potential responses through ideation and prototyping; Deliver tests, refines, and selects a viable solution. The first diamond asks whether the team is solving the right problem. The second asks whether it has found an effective solution to that problem. The model is not a rigid sequence in which every activity happens only once. New evidence during development may reveal that the problem was framed incorrectly, requiring the team to revisit discovery or definition. The value of the model is that it makes both forms of convergence deliberate rather than premature.
Study instruction:
- Map a product project to Discover, Define, Develop, and Deliver, identifying the evidence needed at each transition.
Key point:
- The first diamond converges on the problem; the second converges on the solution.
- Reframing is legitimate when later evidence challenges the original definition.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
5. Q: Why does design thinking deliberately alternate between divergent and convergent thinking?
A: Show answer
Divergent thinking expands the space of possibilities; convergent thinking evaluates evidence and narrows that space to a useful direction. If a team converges too early, it may lock onto the first plausible explanation or idea and overlook better alternatives. If it diverges indefinitely, it produces possibilities without making decisions or delivering value. Design thinking therefore uses both modes intentionally: explore broadly, synthesize what has been learned, define a focus, explore solutions broadly, and then select and refine. This can look disorderly to managers who expect linear progress, but the apparent detours reduce the risk of expensive commitment to an untested assumption. Convergence should be based on clear criteria, evidence, and constraints rather than personal preference alone. Teams should also retain enough flexibility to reopen a decision when testing exposes a material weakness.
Study instruction:
- Review a recent project decision and identify whether the team diverged sufficiently before converging, and what evidence justified its choice.
Key point:
- Divergence protects options; convergence turns learning into decisions.
- The timing of convergence is as important as the decision itself.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
6. Q: How should a team develop a defensible problem statement before generating product solutions?
A: Show answer
A defensible problem statement should describe the people involved, the activity or goal they are pursuing, the context, the obstacles they experience, and the consequences of those obstacles. The team should distinguish observed facts from stakeholder interpretations and proposed features. It should compare accounts from different participants, observe actual work, examine environmental and cultural influences, and look for workarounds that reveal unmet needs. A useful statement is focused enough to guide design but not so narrow that it dictates a particular implementation. It should be testable: researchers should be able to identify evidence that supports or challenges it. Teams can record assumptions, uncertainties, and excluded issues so that the definition remains transparent. The statement is a working hypothesis, not a permanent contract; testing may show that it needs revision.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite a feature-led brief as a user-and-activity problem statement, listing evidence and unresolved assumptions separately.
Key point:
- Define users, goals, context, obstacles, and consequences before prescribing features.
- Treat the problem statement as evidence-based and revisable.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
7. Q: What is applied ethnography, and why is it valuable in design research?
A: Show answer
Applied ethnography adapts observational methods from anthropology to understand how intended users behave in the environments where a product or service will actually be used. Researchers observe routines, tools, interruptions, social interactions, workarounds, and environmental constraints in homes, workplaces, schools, public spaces, or other relevant settings. This reveals needs that people may not articulate in interviews because habits are automatic, problems have become normalized, or users cannot easily imagine an alternative. Design research is usually more time- and budget-constrained than long-term academic ethnography, so the study is focused on actionable product questions. It should nevertheless preserve the central principle of observing real activity rather than relying only on a laboratory description or a survey response. The aim is not to intrude unnecessarily but to understand the context that shapes use.
Study instruction:
- Plan a contextual observation for one product, specifying the setting, activities, consent arrangements, behaviors to record, and questions to investigate.
Key point:
- Observe people where the activity naturally occurs.
- Context exposes workarounds and constraints that self-report may miss.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
8. Q: How should design researchers select people for contextual observation?
A: Show answer
Participants should reflect the intended users and the activities the product must support. Traditional demographic variables such as age, education, or income may be relevant, but they are not automatically the most useful selection criteria. For many products, differences in goals, experience, task frequency, physical capabilities, work setting, or access to technology matter more. A product aimed at a specific subculture or age group requires closer attention to that population because the product may also communicate identity or status. Researchers should identify meaningful user segments and include variation that could change the design requirements. A small qualitative sample can reveal patterns and important exceptions, but it should not be presented as a statistically representative estimate of an entire market. Sampling decisions must follow the research question rather than convenience alone.
Study instruction:
- Build a participant matrix based on activity, experience, context, and relevant capabilities; explain why each group is included.
Key point:
- Recruit for the intended activity and audience, not merely convenient demographics.
- Qualitative insight and statistical representativeness are different objectives.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
9. Q: Why must designers conduct research in the actual cultural and geographic settings where a product will be used?
A: Show answer
Products are shaped by local routines, infrastructure, language, social expectations, climate, household arrangements, regulations, and available resources. Interviewing visitors or students from a target country while remaining in the design team's home environment cannot reliably reproduce the context in which the product will be used. Direct observation and interaction with local participants reveal practical differences that may be invisible from a distance. Some activities, such as basic communication or driving, may be broadly similar across countries, while the details of execution and the meaning of a product can vary substantially. Designers should include local knowledge in the research team and avoid assuming that a global product is culturally neutral. Research must be proportionate to the intended market: a widely used tool may need activity-focused consistency, while a product tied to identity or local custom may require deeper cultural adaptation.
Study instruction:
- Identify three contextual factors that could change the use of a product in another country and determine how to observe each directly.
Key point:
- Local context cannot be reliably inferred from remote stereotypes or second-hand accounts.
- Global consistency and local adaptation must be balanced by evidence.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
10. Q: How does design research differ from market research?
A: Show answer
Design research investigates what people need, how they behave, and how they accomplish activities in context. It often uses qualitative observation, interviews, and small samples to obtain deep insight into real use. Market research investigates customers, purchase decisions, market size, preferences, price sensitivity, and the likelihood that people will buy an offering. It commonly uses quantitative surveys, focus groups, sales data, and larger samples. The methods are complementary, not competing substitutes. A person may need a product feature but not be willing to pay for it, or may buy a product for its brand or appearance and then struggle to use it. Product development must therefore understand both the use experience and the purchase decision. Teams should be explicit about which question a method can answer and avoid treating evidence about buying as proof of usability or evidence about use as proof of commercial demand.
Study instruction:
- For a proposed product, write one design-research question and one market-research question, then choose an appropriate method for each.
Key point:
- Design research explains needs and use; market research explains customers and buying.
- A successful product must satisfy both use requirements and purchase realities.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
11. Q: What can big data and A/B testing reveal, and what can they fail to explain about users?
A: Show answer
Large-scale analytics can reveal behavioral patterns, conversion rates, feature engagement, navigation paths, and differences between variants. In an A/B test, comparable groups are exposed to alternative designs and measured against a defined outcome. This can provide strong evidence about which variant performs better for that outcome under the test conditions. However, numerical correlations do not automatically explain why people behave as they do, what they genuinely need, or whether a measured outcome represents a good experience. Clicks, purchases, and time-on-page can be misleading proxies if the underlying goal is misunderstood. Data collection also raises privacy and consent obligations, especially when personal attributes, location, or browsing histories are captured. Quantitative analysis should therefore be paired with contextual research that explains motives, barriers, and unintended effects.
Study instruction:
- Choose an A/B test metric and list at least three reasons why a higher score might not mean a better overall user experience.
Key point:
- Analytics measure selected behaviors; they do not automatically explain human motives.
- Combine behavioral data with contextual inquiry and privacy safeguards.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
12. Q: How should design and marketing teams work together when customer needs and purchasing behavior differ?
A: Show answer
Design and marketing teams should share research questions and product decisions while preserving the distinction between their evidence. Designers contribute understanding of tasks, capabilities, unmet needs, and the conditions of successful use. Marketers contribute understanding of customer segments, purchasing criteria, price expectations, distribution, and competitive positioning. The product specification must reflect both sets of requirements. A product that people love using but cannot afford or do not recognize as valuable may fail commercially. A product that sells well but creates confusion, support costs, or abandonment may fail after purchase. Joint work should translate insights into explicit trade-offs and test assumptions about both purchase and use. Neither function should claim authority over the other's domain: purchase data does not settle usability questions, and small-sample observation does not estimate market demand by itself.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a shared requirements sheet with separate columns for purchase evidence, use evidence, and unresolved trade-offs.
Key point:
- People who buy a product and people who use it may be different.
- Commercial appeal and effective use must both be designed and evaluated.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
13. Q: What is ideation, and when should it occur in the double-diamond process?
A: Show answer
Ideation is the deliberate generation of possible ideas or approaches. It can occur in both diamonds: while exploring and reframing the problem, and later while developing solutions to the defined problem. Early ideation can help the team imagine different explanations, services, or ways of structuring an activity; later ideation explores specific product responses. Ideation should begin after enough research exists to make the exploration meaningful, but it should not be restricted to a single approved concept. Its purpose is to increase the range of options before selection. Teams should capture ideas in a form that can be compared, combined, or prototyped. The number of ideas is not the sole measure of quality; variety, relevance to evidence, and the possibility of learning from an idea are more important than novelty for its own sake.
Study instruction:
- Run a short ideation session at both the problem-framing and solution stages, and compare the questions each session is intended to answer.
Key point:
- Ideation supports both discovery of the right problem and development of a response.
- Generate alternatives before committing to one concept.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
14. Q: What rules make brainstorming more productive during early design exploration?
A: Show answer
Productive brainstorming encourages participants to generate many ideas, defer criticism, and avoid rejecting unusual proposals too early. Quantity matters initially because a small set of obvious ideas can create fixation and make the team believe that the available options are narrower than they are. Deferring judgment does not mean that every idea will be implemented; it protects the exploratory phase from premature evaluation. A seemingly impractical suggestion may contain a useful principle that can be adapted into a feasible design. Facilitators should make participation safe, capture ideas visibly, invite different disciplines and user perspectives, and separate idea generation from later evaluation. Once the group has expanded the options, ideas can be assessed against user evidence, feasibility, cost, risk, and strategic goals.
Study instruction:
- Facilitate a brainstorming exercise with a no-criticism period, then evaluate the resulting ideas using explicit user-centered criteria.
Key point:
- Delay judgment while generating ideas; apply disciplined evaluation afterward.
- Unusual ideas can contain transferable insights even when their original form is impractical.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
15. Q: Why should designers ask apparently obvious or “stupid” questions?
A: Show answer
An apparently obvious question can expose assumptions that have become invisible through habit. Teams often accept a process because it is familiar, not because its logic is necessary or its outcomes are good. Asking why a control is located in a particular place, why a task must be completed in a certain order, or why a user must perform an intermediate step can reveal inherited constraints, outdated conventions, or opportunities to simplify the activity. The question should be treated seriously rather than dismissed because everyone believes the answer is known. It may uncover the real reason for a requirement, or show that no one has verified the reason at all. Challenging assumptions does not mean ignoring safety, regulation, or technical constraints; it means identifying which constraints are real and which are merely customary.
Study instruction:
- List five assumptions embedded in a familiar product workflow and formulate a basic question that could test each one.
Key point:
- What appears obvious may be an unexamined convention.
- Fundamental questions can reveal new design directions.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
16. Q: What is a prototype, and why should designers build one before committing to a full solution?
A: Show answer
A prototype is a representation or simulation of a proposed product, service, interface, or interaction that allows the team and intended users to examine an idea before full implementation. Its purpose is to make assumptions observable and testable. Prototypes can reveal whether users understand a concept, whether a sequence of actions is practical, whether a service handoff works, or whether a physical form can be handled. They reduce the cost of learning because changes to a sketch or model are usually less expensive than changes to a manufactured product or deployed system. A prototype need not be complete or technically functional; it needs enough fidelity to answer the question being investigated. The team should define the learning objective before selecting the prototype form and avoid mistaking a convincing demonstration for proof of operational readiness.
Study instruction:
- For a proposed solution, identify the riskiest assumption and choose the simplest prototype that could test it.
Key point:
- Prototype to learn, not merely to demonstrate progress.
- Fidelity should match the question under investigation.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
17. Q: Which low-fidelity prototyping methods can be used before engineering development begins?
A: Show answer
Early prototypes can be made from pencil sketches, index cards, sticky notes, cardboard, foam, simple drawings, spreadsheet models, presentation slides, or short role-play scenarios. A service may be explored through a skit that represents the interactions among a customer, staff member, and back-office system. A digital interface can be represented by paper screens that a facilitator changes as the participant makes choices. These methods are inexpensive, quick to revise, and useful for testing structure, language, sequence, and expectations before investing in detailed code or tooling. Low fidelity is not a weakness when the research question concerns the basic concept. It becomes a limitation when the team tries to infer performance characteristics that the model cannot represent, such as response time, physical durability, or real-world reliability.
Study instruction:
- Create two different low-fidelity prototypes for the same concept and state what each can and cannot validly test.
Key point:
- Simple materials can expose major usability and workflow problems early.
- Do not draw conclusions about properties the prototype does not reproduce.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
18. Q: What is the Wizard of Oz prototyping method, and when is it useful?
A: Show answer
Wizard of Oz prototyping simulates a system's apparent intelligence or automation through a hidden human operator. The participant interacts with what seems to be a functioning system, while a researcher or team member supplies responses manually behind the scenes. This makes it possible to test an ambitious service or conversational interface before the required technology exists. The method can reveal the range and wording of real user requests, the context needed to respond helpfully, and the work users expect the system to perform. It is particularly useful when building the underlying automation would be expensive or premature. Researchers must document which functions were simulated, avoid treating the test as evidence that the technology is already feasible, and debrief participants appropriately according to the study's ethics and consent requirements.
Study instruction:
- Design a Wizard of Oz test for a proposed automated service, specifying the operator's role, participant disclosure or debrief, and learning goals.
Key point:
- Simulate the experience to test demand and interaction before building the technology.
- A human-assisted prototype does not prove that automation is technically achievable.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
19. Q: How can prototyping help a team discover that its original problem definition is too narrow?
A: Show answer
A prototype can expose needs that were not visible in the initial brief because users respond to the experience in ways the team did not anticipate. For example, a simulated travel assistant may appear to require only language recognition, but real requests may depend on airport locations, meeting schedules, ground transport, parking, luggage collection, and time constraints. The prototype reveals that the actual need concerns completing a journey, not simply interpreting sentences. In the first diamond, prototypes and tests are therefore tools for understanding the problem as well as tools for evaluating solutions. Teams should treat unexpected user behavior as evidence to investigate rather than as user error. When the underlying need changes, the problem statement and requirements should be revised before additional investment is made.
Study instruction:
- Identify a product idea whose apparent function may conceal a broader activity, then describe a prototype test that could reveal the broader need.
Key point:
- Prototyping can challenge the problem definition, not just validate a proposed interface.
- Real user behavior may reveal a larger system of needs and dependencies.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
20. Q: How should participants be selected and situated for a prototype test?
A: Show answer
Participants should resemble the people for whom the product is intended in the characteristics that influence the activity, including experience, capabilities, context, and relevant cultural factors. The test should reproduce the normal use situation as closely as practical: a one-person device is usually tested individually, while a collaborative service should be tested with the relevant group or roles. Instructions and tasks should reflect realistic goals without coaching participants toward the expected solution. The team should make the prototype's limitations clear when those limitations could affect behavior, and should obtain appropriate consent for observation and recording. Convenience samples can be useful for early exploration, but they should not be confused with a well-targeted sample. Test conditions must be recorded so that findings can be interpreted correctly.
Study instruction:
- Write a test recruitment and setup plan that links each participant characteristic to a design question and a realistic use context.
Key point:
- Test with people who represent the intended activity and use environment.
- Realistic conditions make observed behavior more meaningful.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
21. Q: Why should a product normally be tested in the same social arrangement in which it will be used?
A: Show answer
The social arrangement affects how people coordinate, interpret information, divide responsibilities, and recover from mistakes. A tool normally used by one person should generally be tested one person at a time because another person may provide help that would not exist in real use. A collaborative product, however, needs a group test because interaction among participants is part of the activity. Norman also describes paired testing in which one participant operates a prototype while another helps verbalize actions and interpretations. This can reveal hypotheses and frustrations, but it is a research technique rather than a substitute for testing natural solo use. The researcher must distinguish what the design itself enables from what a partner contributes. The correct arrangement depends on the study question and normal usage pattern.
Study instruction:
- Compare an individual test and a paired test for one product, identifying the different evidence each can provide.
Key point:
- Match the test arrangement to the real social structure of use.
- Paired testing can reveal reasoning but may alter natural behavior.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
22. Q: What is the purpose of think-aloud or paired verbalization during usability testing?
A: Show answer
Verbalization helps researchers understand participants' expectations, interpretations, and decisions while they interact with a prototype. A participant may explain what they believe a control will do, what information they are seeking, or why they hesitate. In a paired method, one person operates while another helps articulate observations, which can make otherwise hidden reasoning and frustration more visible. These techniques are useful because behavior alone does not always reveal the participant's interpretation. However, speaking changes the task: it can slow performance, draw attention to details, or encourage more reflection than ordinary use. Researchers should therefore treat verbal comments as one evidence source and compare them with observed actions, task outcomes, and post-test questions rather than assuming that every spoken explanation perfectly reflects the participant's mental process.
Study instruction:
- Draft neutral prompts that encourage participants to explain expectations without telling them how to complete the task.
Key point:
- Verbalization reveals interpretations that may not be visible in actions alone.
- Think-aloud evidence should be triangulated with observed behavior and outcomes.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
23. Q: How should researchers observe usability tests without interfering with participant behavior?
A: Show answer
Researchers should observe attentively while minimizing unnecessary prompting, correction, or visible reactions that could influence the participant. They may sit unobtrusively behind participants or observe remotely through video, provided participants know the procedure and recording arrangements. The team should record actions, errors, hesitation, workarounds, task completion, and relevant environmental conditions. Video can preserve details for later review and allow team members who were absent to examine the same events. It also creates privacy and data-management responsibilities: consent, access control, retention, and appropriate use should be established before recording. Observers should distinguish what they saw from their interpretations and avoid diagnosing the cause of a difficulty during the session. Follow-up questions can explore the participant's reasoning after the task is complete.
Study instruction:
- Prepare an observation sheet that separates timestamped behavior, participant comments, researcher interpretation, and follow-up questions.
Key point:
- Observe behavior systematically and avoid leading the participant.
- Recordings are useful evidence but require informed handling and consent.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
24. Q: Why should researchers conduct a post-test debrief after observing participants use a prototype?
A: Show answer
A debrief helps clarify what participants were trying to do, what they expected, and why they took particular actions. Researchers can retrace the steps, refer to notes or recordings, and ask participants to explain moments of hesitation, unexpected choices, or workarounds. This is more reliable than asking broad questions such as whether the participant liked the product, because specific events provide concrete prompts. A debrief can also reveal contextual factors that were not apparent during observation. It should not pressure participants to justify every action or imply that they used the prototype incorrectly. The purpose is to understand the interaction and test the team's interpretations. Findings should be synthesized across participants while preserving meaningful differences and exceptional cases.
Study instruction:
- Create a debrief guide using observed moments as prompts and include questions about expectations, context, and alternative approaches.
Key point:
- Use observed events to elicit specific explanations after the task.
- Debriefing helps separate user intent from researcher assumption.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
25. Q: What does the recommendation to test in small groups of about five people mean for iterative design?
A: Show answer
The chapter presents the practice of testing a small group, often around five participants individually, studying the findings, improving the design, and then testing a new group. The advantage is that learning is used to improve the next iteration rather than being postponed until a large one-time study is complete. Small rounds can expose major usability problems efficiently, especially when the objective is formative discovery and refinement. The number five is not a universal statistical rule, guarantee of completeness, or substitute for appropriate sampling. High-risk systems, diverse populations, accessibility evaluations, quantitative benchmarking, and rare failure modes may require more participants, specialized recruitment, or additional methods. The correct sample size depends on the research question, diversity of users, expected variability, and consequences of missing a problem.
Study instruction:
- Plan two formative test rounds and explain what changes after the first round and why the second sample may need different participants.
Key point:
- Small iterative rounds accelerate learning and redesign.
- Five participants is a heuristic for some formative studies, not a universal validation threshold.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
26. Q: What does “fail frequently, fail fast” mean in human-centered design?
A: Show answer
The phrase means that teams should expose weak assumptions early through inexpensive prototypes and tests, learn from the difficulties, and revise the design before those weaknesses become costly. It does not celebrate careless execution or repeated harm to users. A failed prototype is valuable when it reveals why an idea does not work and informs the next decision. If every early test appears perfect, the team may not be challenging its assumptions or may be testing too narrowly. Managers should distinguish controlled learning from production failure: the former is deliberate and contained; the latter can damage customers, safety, trust, and budgets. The process should include clear hypotheses, test objectives, records of findings, and decisions about what to change. The aim is to reduce uncertainty and risk through disciplined experimentation.
Study instruction:
- Define a low-cost experiment for a risky design assumption and specify what result would cause the team to revise its approach.
Key point:
- Early, contained failure can prevent expensive late failure.
- Learning requires explicit hypotheses and evidence, not experimentation without control.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
27. Q: Why are requirements written in the abstract or collected only by asking users often unreliable?
A: Show answer
Abstract requirements tend to omit the context in which people perform their activities. When asked what they need, people often describe familiar problems and preferred features but may overlook routine workarounds, rare but consequential situations, or larger failures they have normalized. They may also describe idealized behavior rather than what they actually do under interruptions, time pressure, or resource constraints. Repeated observation and prototype testing help convert assumptions into evidence. Researchers should compare what people say with what they do, investigate differences respectfully, and avoid dismissing exceptions as irrelevant simply because they do not fit the initial model. Requirements should emerge from patterns of observed activity, validated needs, and constraints, then be refined through successive tests.
Study instruction:
- Compare an interview statement with observed behavior for one task and document any differences that require further inquiry.
Key point:
- Stated preferences do not fully describe real-world behavior.
- Requirements improve through observation, testing, and revision.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
28. Q: How should a design team handle “special cases” discovered during field research?
A: Show answer
A special case should be investigated before being excluded. What appears exceptional to a designer may be routine for a particular user group, location, season, accessibility need, or operational context. If a system cannot accommodate a common variation, people may invent workarounds or abandon it. Researchers should record the frequency, consequence, and context of each variation, then decide whether the design should support it directly, provide a flexible path, or explicitly define a safe boundary. Not every rare case can be supported without unacceptable complexity or cost, but the decision should be informed by evidence and risk. Teams should distinguish a genuinely rare edge case from an unobserved but predictable variation and make the trade-off visible in the requirements.
Study instruction:
- Build an exception log for one workflow and rank variations by frequency, severity, and feasibility of support.
Key point:
- Many “special cases” are recurring realities of use.
- Exclusions should be deliberate and justified, not accidental.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
29. Q: How does iteration improve requirements, specifications, and prototypes over time?
A: Show answer
Each cycle of observation, ideation, prototyping, and testing produces information that changes the team's understanding. Early cycles may reveal broad misunderstandings about the activity; later cycles can focus on details such as terminology, control placement, feedback, or exception handling. As learning accumulates, the problem statement becomes more precise, ideas become more coherent, and prototypes increasingly resemble the intended product. Several alternative prototypes may eventually be combined or narrowed to one direction. Iteration should be purposeful: teams should identify what they learned, which assumptions remain uncertain, what changed, and what needs testing next. Without that synthesis, repeated cycles can become activity without progress. The process should preserve a traceable relationship between research findings, requirement changes, design decisions, and test results.
Study instruction:
- Create an iteration log showing the hypothesis, prototype change, observed result, decision, and next question for each cycle.
Key point:
- Iteration converts evidence into progressively better requirements and solutions.
- Record the learning so that design changes remain explainable.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
30. Q: When should an iterative design process converge, and who is responsible for the decision?
A: Show answer
Convergence occurs when the team has enough evidence to select a direction that meets the essential user needs and fits the project's quality, cost, and schedule constraints. It does not mean every uncertainty has disappeared or that the design can never change. The product manager is responsible for balancing the desired quality with delivery commitments and available resources, while the design team provides evidence about user needs, risks, and alternatives. A deadline can create useful discipline by forcing choices, but it should not be used to conceal untested critical assumptions. Teams should define decision criteria and escalation thresholds in advance, including which issues require more testing because of safety, accessibility, or business risk. The selected solution should retain a plan for monitoring and improvement after release.
Study instruction:
- Draft convergence criteria for a prototype project, distinguishing mandatory user and safety requirements from negotiable enhancements.
Key point:
- Converge when evidence and constraints justify a decision, not merely when the first idea feels complete.
- Product management balances quality, schedule, and budget.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
31. Q: What is activity-centered design, and how does it extend human-centered design?
A: Show answer
Activity-centered design organizes a product around the high-level activity people are trying to accomplish rather than tailoring every detail to one narrowly defined individual. It remains human-centered because activities are performed by people, but it can be more practical for products intended for broad, diverse populations. Many people across cultures perform similar activities even though their personal characteristics and local circumstances differ. Designing around the structure of the activity can therefore produce a coherent product for a wider audience, while remaining sensitive to capabilities and context. This approach does not mean ignoring individual differences; it means using the activity as the organizing model and providing flexibility or variants where user differences materially affect success. The product's conceptual model should reflect the activity's goals, sequence, dependencies, and meaningful operations.
Study instruction:
- Select a global product and identify the shared activity structure it supports, then list user differences that still require adaptation.
Key point:
- Activity-centered design organizes around shared human goals and workflows.
- It complements HCD rather than replacing attention to individual needs.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
32. Q: What is the difference between an activity and a task in product design?
A: Show answer
An activity is a high-level, goal-directed undertaking such as shopping, preparing a meal, commuting, or enjoying music. A task is a lower-level, cohesive action that contributes to that activity, such as finding a basket, comparing items, paying, or carrying purchases home. Tasks are often easier to specify and measure, but designing only for isolated tasks can fragment the user's overall experience. A product or service should support the set of tasks as a connected whole, ensuring that information and work created in one step remain useful in the next. Activities can contain subactivities, tasks, and basic operations, forming a hierarchy. Designers should therefore identify the high-level goal and map the tasks and transitions required to achieve it rather than optimizing a single interaction in isolation.
Study instruction:
- Break a familiar activity into subactivities, tasks, and operations, then identify where a product could create friction between steps.
Key point:
- Activities are high-level goals; tasks are component actions.
- Design should support the whole activity, not only isolated task completion.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
33. Q: How do be-goals, do-goals, and motor-goals help designers understand user experience?
A: Show answer
The three levels describe different layers of human intention. A be-goal is an abstract, enduring aspiration connected to identity or the kind of person someone wants to be. A do-goal concerns what the person wants to accomplish in an activity. A motor-goal concerns the physical or operational actions used to carry it out. For example, someone may want to be a capable, independent traveler, plan to reach a destination reliably, and then operate a ticket machine or navigation control. A product that supports only the motor action may still fail the broader do-goal if the overall journey is confusing. Understanding the hierarchy helps designers connect interface details to meaningful outcomes and emotional experience. It also discourages treating button presses as the whole user experience.
Study instruction:
- Analyze one user journey and write a be-goal, do-goal, and motor-goal for the same person, showing how each level affects design decisions.
Key point:
- User experience spans identity, activity outcomes, and concrete operations.
- A locally successful action can still fail the user's larger goal.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
34. Q: Why is it often better to design for an entire activity rather than a single task?
A: Show answer
An activity is usually completed through a chain of interdependent tasks, information exchanges, and decisions. Optimizing one task can shift effort to another part of the journey or create friction at a handoff. Designing for the whole activity encourages teams to understand how people discover, obtain, prepare, use, share, and maintain a product or service. The music-player example in the chapter illustrates this: success came from supporting music discovery, purchase, transfer, playlist creation, listening, and compatible accessories, rather than treating playback as the only task. Activity-level design can create a more coherent ecosystem and identify opportunities for complementary services. It also requires attention to dependencies, interoperability, and partners whose products extend the core experience.
Study instruction:
- Map an end-to-end activity and identify at least three tasks that must work together for the user's goal to be achieved.
Key point:
- The user judges the complete activity, not just one isolated interaction.
- Coherent task integration can create greater value than a feature in isolation.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
35. Q: How can activity-centered design support products intended for users across different countries?
A: Show answer
Many basic activities have common structures across cultures, which can support a consistent core product. A telephone, computer, or vehicle may serve similar goals in many places, even though local conventions, infrastructure, languages, regulations, and expectations differ. Designers can identify the stable elements of the activity and then determine which details require localization or configuration. A global design should not assume that all users behave identically; it should distinguish shared activity requirements from culturally specific practices and accessibility needs. Local research remains necessary to test those assumptions. The approach is most useful when it provides a common conceptual structure without forcing users to adopt arbitrary behavior that conflicts with their environment.
Study instruction:
- For a product used in two countries, separate shared activity steps from local variations in language, infrastructure, convention, and regulation.
Key point:
- Shared activities can support global consistency, but local context still matters.
- Standardize only what evidence shows can be shared safely and usefully.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
36. Q: What are the main differences between iterative design and the linear waterfall approach?
A: Show answer
A linear or waterfall process moves through stages in a planned sequence, with decisions made early and limited opportunity to revisit them. It can make responsibilities, budgets, handoffs, and schedules easier to manage, especially in large programs. Iterative HCD cycles through research, idea generation, prototyping, and testing, allowing teams to revise assumptions and requirements as they learn. It is particularly valuable early, when the problem and solution are still uncertain. The weakness of a rigid waterfall approach is that incorrect early requirements may be discovered only after expensive work. The weakness of unbounded iteration is that scope, schedule, and coordination can become difficult to control. The choice is not simply one method or the other; project size, uncertainty, risk, and organizational structure matter.
Study instruction:
- Compare both methods for a small digital service and a large infrastructure program, explaining where each is strongest and weakest.
Key point:
- Iteration improves learning under uncertainty; linear stages improve predictability and control.
- Method selection should reflect project scale and risk.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
37. Q: What are gated development methods, and what role do decision gates play?
A: Show answer
Gated methods divide development into stages separated by management reviews. At each gate, decision-makers examine progress, evidence, cost, schedule, risks, and readiness before authorizing the next stage. Gates provide formal control over investment and make it easier to coordinate large teams and functions. They can also force teams to document assumptions and resolve major issues before committing additional resources. However, preparing gate presentations and scheduling senior decision-makers can consume significant time, and a gate can become a bureaucratic ritual if it rewards polished presentations rather than credible evidence. A useful gate should make a clear decision: proceed, revise, pause, or stop. The criteria should be known in advance and should include user evidence, not only technical milestones.
Study instruction:
- Design a gate review checklist that includes user research findings, unresolved assumptions, technical feasibility, cost, schedule, and risk.
Key point:
- Decision gates control commitment between project stages.
- Their value depends on evidence-based decisions, not presentation formality.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
38. Q: How can iterative design and stage-gate management be combined effectively?
A: Show answer
A hybrid approach allows teams to conduct short cycles of research, prototyping, and testing within each formal stage, while using gates to control major commitments. Early iterations can refine the problem and product requirements before the organization approves expensive engineering or manufacturing work. Later stages may use more bounded iteration to resolve specific design and integration issues without reopening every strategic decision. The gate should assess what has been learned, which risks remain, and whether the evidence justifies moving forward. This arrangement preserves the adaptability of HCD and the governance of staged development. It also requires explicit rules for when new evidence is serious enough to reopen a prior decision, otherwise teams may either ignore important findings or destabilize the project with constant scope changes.
Study instruction:
- Sketch a hybrid development plan showing iterative loops within stages and the evidence required at each gate.
Key point:
- Iteration can happen between gates while management retains control of major commitments.
- Define when evidence can legitimately reopen a decision.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
39. Q: How can large or physical projects use prototypes when a full-scale working model is impossible?
A: Show answer
Large projects can use scaled physical models, 3-D printed components, detailed drawings, rendered walkthroughs, animation, video scenarios, and virtual-reality environments. These representations allow stakeholders and intended users to inspect layout, movement, sight lines, spatial relationships, and likely workflows before construction or production. A building, transport facility, or complex machine may be difficult to prototype at full scale, but a model can still reveal circulation problems or misunderstood controls. The team must be clear about what the representation can validate and what requires later physical testing. For example, a virtual walkthrough may assess wayfinding but cannot prove structural performance or long-term durability. Combining multiple prototype forms can reduce uncertainty while avoiding false confidence from a visually persuasive model.
Study instruction:
- Select a large project and match three prototype techniques to distinct questions about layout, operation, and safety.
Key point:
- Prototyping is possible even when a full-scale product cannot be built early.
- Each representation has limits that must be stated.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
40. Q: Why are large, long-duration projects especially vulnerable to coordination and knowledge-loss problems?
A: Show answer
Large projects involve many specialists, departments, suppliers, and decision-makers whose work must remain synchronized over long periods. During that time, technology, requirements, regulations, users, and organizational priorities may change. Team members may leave through promotion, retirement, illness, or changes of employer, taking experience and implicit knowledge with them. Replacements need time to understand the project's history and may never fully recover undocumented reasoning behind earlier decisions. Teams should preserve decision records, design rationales, interface definitions, assumptions, test results, and handover materials. They should also plan for succession, onboarding, and periodic reassessment of long-term assumptions. Documentation cannot capture every aspect of expertise, so structured knowledge-sharing and overlapping responsibilities are also necessary.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a knowledge-continuity plan for a multi-year project, identifying critical implicit knowledge and how it will be transferred.
Key point:
- Long projects face changing requirements and staff turnover as well as technical complexity.
- Preserve rationale and knowledge, not just final specifications.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
41. Q: What practical pressures cause organizations to depart from ideal human-centered design?
A: Show answer
Organizations may prioritize competitive feature matching, new technology, launch dates, factory schedules, revenue targets, and short-term budget limits over sustained research into human needs. Teams are often assembled after a project has already been scheduled, so the time required to recruit people, arrange fieldwork, observe users, and synthesize findings is not included. Managers may accept the value of research but conclude that there is no time or money to do it. Separate departments may also optimize their own objectives, causing user needs to be diluted during handoffs. These pressures explain why HCD is difficult to implement consistently; they do not make it unnecessary. Organizations should make research capacity continuous, plan for discovery before product launch decisions, and include user evidence in governance and budget assumptions.
Study instruction:
- Analyze a product launch plan and identify which research, recruitment, and cross-functional activities were omitted from the schedule.
Key point:
- Schedule and budget pressures can systematically crowd out user research.
- HCD must be planned and resourced before the project becomes urgent.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
42. Q: What is the practical meaning of Norman's “Law of Product Development”?
A: Show answer
The chapter's deliberately provocative law states that a product development process begins behind schedule and above budget. The point is that project plans often omit the time needed to assemble the team, move people from existing work, arrange research, resolve dependencies, and understand the actual problem. External launch dates and manufacturing windows may already constrain the schedule before design begins. As a result, the team is pressured to skip research and testing, even though those activities may prevent expensive redesign later. The statement is not a mathematical law or an excuse for poor planning. It is a warning that schedules are routinely unrealistic because they ignore the work required to start well. Managers should explicitly include mobilization, discovery, iteration, coordination, and contingency in project planning.
Study instruction:
- Build a realistic project initiation schedule that includes team mobilization, field research, synthesis, prototyping, testing, and decision time.
Key point:
- Projects often lose time before the visible design work begins.
- Plan for research and mobilization instead of treating them as optional extras.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
43. Q: How can organizations preserve design research when individual product teams have little time for fieldwork?
A: Show answer
The chapter proposes maintaining design researchers who continuously study potential customers, activities, and emerging needs outside the narrow timetable of a single product team. When a new project begins, relevant findings may already exist and can provide a more informed starting point. This does not eliminate the need for project-specific validation, because target users, context, technology, or market conditions may have changed. It reduces the repeated delay of starting all research from zero and makes discovery a sustained organizational capability. Research repositories should record the population, date, context, methods, limitations, and implications of findings so that product teams do not apply old insights indiscriminately. Continuous market research can be organized in a similar way.
Study instruction:
- Propose a continuous research program and define how findings will be indexed, refreshed, and validated by future product teams.
Key point:
- Continuous research can reduce the cost of starting discovery under deadline pressure.
- Reuse prior findings carefully and verify their current relevance.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
44. Q: Why should product development use a multidisciplinary team from the beginning?
A: Show answer
Design decisions affect engineering, software, manufacturing, procurement, marketing, sales, packaging, maintenance, customer service, and repair. Each discipline has legitimate requirements, but those requirements may conflict. If departments work in isolation and receive designs only at handoff, they often make late changes to satisfy needs that were not considered earlier. Piecemeal modifications can weaken the product's coherence and create cost, quality, or usability problems. A multidisciplinary team brings relevant perspectives into the process early, allowing trade-offs to be discussed while alternatives remain available. The product manager or facilitator must help participants understand one another's technical language and respect the constraints of other disciplines. Collaboration is not simply putting representatives in meetings; it requires shared decisions, clear responsibilities, and timely resolution of conflicts.
Study instruction:
- Map the disciplines involved in a product and list the design decisions each needs to influence before they become costly to change.
Key point:
- Cross-functional participation prevents late, fragmented redesign.
- Product quality depends on cooperation as well as specialist expertise.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
45. Q: How should a product team resolve conflicting requirements from engineering, marketing, manufacturing, and design?
A: Show answer
The team should make each requirement explicit, identify the stakeholder it serves, and understand the evidence and constraints behind it. For example, engineering may prioritize reliability, manufacturing may need repeatable assembly, marketing may need a recognizable feature, and design may prioritize task success. These positions are not automatically unreasonable; each reflects a different responsibility. The team should identify non-negotiable safety, legal, and performance requirements, then compare alternatives against user outcomes, lifecycle cost, feasibility, and business viability.
