Trade-offs should be documented with their consequences and owners. A decision should not be made through departmental bargaining alone. Shared prototypes and tests can reveal whether an apparent conflict can be resolved through a different concept or whether a conscious compromise is required.
Study instruction:
- Create a requirements conflict matrix for one product and propose a decision process that includes evidence, priorities, and accountable approval.
Key point:
- Conflicting requirements often represent legitimate perspectives that need integration.
- Use explicit criteria and shared evidence to make trade-offs.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
46. Q: Why should designers consider purchasers as well as end users?
A: Show answer
In many markets, the person who chooses and pays for a product is not the person who uses it. Developers, landlords, procurement departments, and managers may select appliances, office equipment, or software primarily on price, dimensions, or a list of features. If the purchasing specification excludes usability, training, maintenance, and support, the product may win the sale while creating high costs and frustration for users. Designers must understand the buyer's decision criteria because those criteria determine what gets purchased, but they must also make the value of good use visible to buyers. Evidence about productivity, service calls, training time, and lifecycle cost can help connect user experience to procurement decisions. The buyer and the end user should be treated as distinct stakeholders when their interests differ.
Study instruction:
- For a product bought by an organization, identify the purchaser, daily user, maintainer, and approver, then compare their priorities.
Key point:
- The customer who buys may not be the person who uses the product.
- Procurement criteria should account for the costs and benefits of actual use.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
47. Q: Why can choosing equipment mainly by purchase price create higher total costs?
A: Show answer
A low purchase price can conceal costs that occur during use, including staff training, lost time, service calls, downtime, errors, maintenance, and reduced morale. The copier example in the chapter illustrates how a procurement process may compare price and listed features while omitting usability and support requirements. The resulting equipment may be difficult to operate, requiring repeated assistance and reducing productivity. A better evaluation considers total cost of ownership over the product's expected life, including acquisition, installation, training, maintenance, consumables, support, and replacement. Usability should be specified and tested before procurement, not assumed from a feature list. Procurement teams should also involve representative users and service personnel in evaluation so that the selected product works in the environment where it will be used.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a lifecycle cost comparison for two products, including purchase price, training, support, downtime, and maintenance assumptions.
Key point:
- Purchase price is only one component of the cost of ownership.
- Usability can affect productivity and ongoing service expenditure.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
48. Q: Why are downstream departments also “users” of the design team's output?
A: Show answer
Engineers, developers, manufacturers, sales teams, service staff, and help desks use the specifications, models, and decisions produced by designers. If their needs are ignored, they may discover late that a concept cannot be manufactured, supported, explained, distributed, or repaired effectively. They may then alter it independently to meet operational constraints, potentially damaging the coherence of the user experience. Designers should therefore treat the development process as a system of users and handoffs. Downstream groups need clear requirements, design rationale, interfaces, tolerances, service information, and opportunities to raise concerns early. Their participation does not mean that every internal preference should override the end user's needs; it means that the product must be feasible to build, deliver, and support.
Study instruction:
- Trace one design decision through engineering, manufacturing, sales, and service, identifying what each downstream group needs from the design team.
Key point:
- The design specification is itself a product used by internal teams.
- Early downstream input reduces disruptive handoff changes.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
49. Q: Why is there no such thing as a single “average person” for whom every product can be designed?
A: Show answer
Human bodies, sensory abilities, strengths, preferences, experience, and environments vary substantially. An average measurement can conceal the range of people who fall outside it, and a design that fits a statistical midpoint may fit nobody particularly well. Even a design intended to accommodate a high percentile of a population excludes people beyond that range; the absolute number excluded can still be large. Some differences, such as left- and right-handed use, cannot be solved by averaging. Designers should examine the distribution of relevant characteristics, understand the consequences of exclusion, and decide whether adjustment, alternative versions, or different product configurations are needed. The target population and use context matter; anthropometric data should inform design rather than substitute for real user evaluation.
Study instruction:
- Identify the human dimensions relevant to a product and explain why an average value may fail to accommodate the full user population.
Key point:
- Population averages conceal meaningful individual variation.
- Design decisions should account for distributions and excluded groups.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
50. Q: How should designers use anthropometric percentiles without excluding large groups of people?
A: Show answer
Anthropometry provides measurements such as reach, seated height, and body clearance that can guide physical design. A percentile indicates a position within a measured population, not a guarantee that a product will fit everyone. Designing for the 95th percentile may still exclude people at the smaller or larger ends, and the number excluded can be substantial in a large population. Designers should select dimensions according to the functional risk: some clearances need to accommodate larger bodies, while some reach distances need to be usable by smaller or less mobile people. Adjustable components, alternative sizes, and different configurations may be more effective than one fixed dimension. Data should match the relevant population and be tested with users who represent the extremes and combinations of characteristics that matter.
Study instruction:
- For a workstation or control panel, identify which dimensions require upper-percentile clearance, lower-percentile reach, and user adjustment.
Key point:
- Percentiles are design tools, not proof of universal fit.
- Select ranges and adjustment based on the function and consequences of mismatch.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
51. Q: What is inclusive or universal design, and why can it benefit people beyond the intended group?
A: Show answer
Inclusive or universal design seeks to make products and environments usable by people with a broad range of abilities and circumstances. Features introduced for people with specific difficulties often improve use for many others. Larger lettering and strong contrast can help people with low vision and anyone reading in dim light. Adjustable seating or display settings can support users with different bodies, preferences, or temporary limitations. The benefit is not guaranteed: a feature may help one group while creating barriers for another, so it must be evaluated in context. Universal design should be treated as an aspiration to broaden access, not a claim that one solution will meet every need. Flexibility, alternative ways of interacting, and testing with diverse users are often more effective than assuming a single default fits everyone.
Study instruction:
- Identify a feature designed for a specific access need and test how it could improve use for other populations without introducing new barriers.
Key point:
- Accessibility improvements often produce wider usability benefits.
- Universal design requires evaluation across varied users and contexts.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
52. Q: Why do assistive products sometimes fail even when they solve a functional problem?
A: Show answer
A product can be technically effective and still be rejected if it makes the user feel stigmatized, dependent, conspicuous, or defined by an impairment. People may avoid a cane, walker, hearing aid, or other aid because its appearance signals a social identity they do not wish to display. Some may also resist acknowledging a difficulty, even when the product would help. Designers should therefore consider aesthetics, dignity, identity, comfort, social acceptance, and practical performance together. The OXO peeler example shows how a product initially motivated by the needs of a person with arthritis became appealing to a much wider audience when it was presented as a better tool for everyone. Designing for a specific need need not mean visibly labeling the user as different.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate an assistive product for functional benefit, appearance, stigma, ease of adoption, and how its value is communicated.
Key point:
- Adoption depends on social meaning as well as functional performance.
- A broadly appealing design can reduce stigma and expand the market.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
53. Q: What does the OXO vegetable peeler example teach about designing for special needs?
A: Show answer
The OXO peeler was developed after Sam Farber sought a more comfortable tool for his wife, who had arthritis. The larger, more comfortable handle reduced pain and made the tool easier to use, but the product was marketed as a better peeler for everyone rather than as a medical aid. This avoided stigmatizing its intended beneficiaries and demonstrated that designing for a particular limitation can reveal a superior solution for a broad market. The example also shows that a product can command a higher price when its improved experience is clear and valuable. Designers should investigate the real difficulty, create a solution that preserves dignity, and communicate benefits in terms that appeal to users without implying that only one group needs the improvement.
Study instruction:
- Apply the OXO principle to a current product category and identify how a targeted accessibility improvement could become a mainstream advantage.
Key point:
- Design for a specific need can improve the product for many people.
- Inclusive positioning can reduce stigma and encourage adoption.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
54. Q: How should designers account for aging without relying on stereotypes about older people?
A: Show answer
Aging is associated with changes in some physical and sensory capabilities, such as strength, agility, reaction time, vision, hearing, and the ability to divide attention quickly. At the same time, knowledge, experience, judgment, and some forms of expertise can continue to improve. Older adults are not a uniform group, and age alone does not reveal a person's abilities or preferences. Designers should observe the actual task demands and recruit people with varied capabilities rather than assume that every older person needs the same solution. Design teams also benefit from intergenerational perspectives: younger people may bring agility and willingness to experiment, while older people may contribute accumulated knowledge and reflection. Products should support capability and independence without reducing people to a deficit-based stereotype.
Study instruction:
- Review a product used by adults of different ages and separate assumptions about age from observed sensory, physical, and cognitive task requirements.
Key point:
- Aging changes some capabilities but does not define a person's overall competence.
- Design from individual abilities and task demands, not stereotypes.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
55. Q: Why is flexibility often a better strategy than one fixed design for a diverse population?
A: Show answer
Flexibility allows users to adapt a product to their bodies, preferences, environments, and changing circumstances. Adjustable chair heights, table angles, font sizes, contrast, image scaling, lighting, and control positions can accommodate variation that a single fixed configuration cannot. Flexible systems can also support temporary changes, such as working in dim light or using a device while fatigued. However, flexibility must itself be understandable: too many settings, hidden controls, or confusing configuration steps can create new barriers. Designers should provide useful defaults, make adjustments discoverable, and preserve predictable behavior when settings change. Where adjustment cannot solve the problem, alternative product versions or interaction modes may be necessary. Flexibility is a strategy for managing diversity, not a substitute for research and testing.
Study instruction:
- Identify fixed design assumptions in a workstation or digital interface and propose adjustments that are discoverable and meaningful to users.
Key point:
- Adjustable designs accommodate variation better than many one-size-fits-all solutions.
- Flexibility must remain understandable and easy to control.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
56. Q: What is the difference between complexity and confusion in product design?
A: Show answer
Complexity is the amount of structure, functionality, and coordination required by a real activity. Confusion occurs when users cannot understand that structure or determine how to act. A kitchen contains many tools because cooking involves many distinct operations; removing tools indiscriminately would not necessarily make cooking easier. The problem arises when users cannot find the needed item or understand how the environment is organized. A product can therefore be complex yet understandable, or simple in appearance yet confusing. Designers should not equate fewer features or controls with better design. They should match the product's complexity to the task while providing a coherent organization, clear relationships, and appropriate feedback. Simplification is valuable when it removes unnecessary work, not when it removes capabilities needed to complete the activity.
Study instruction:
- Analyze a complex environment and distinguish necessary task complexity from avoidable confusion caused by poor organization or unclear relationships.
Key point:
- Complexity may be necessary; confusion is the design problem.
- Remove unnecessary difficulty without stripping away essential capability.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
57. Q: How does a good conceptual model make a complex product easier to understand?
A: Show answer
A conceptual model gives users a coherent explanation of how a system is organized and how its parts relate to their goals. When the arrangement of controls, tools, information, and actions reflects a meaningful structure, users can predict where things belong and what will happen. The kitchen example illustrates that an arrangement may look random to a visitor but make sense to the person who organized it, including exceptions that have a practical reason. Designers should make the system's organizing logic visible through consistent groupings, labels, mappings, and feedback. A good conceptual model does not eliminate all learning, but it makes the learning coherent and transferable. If users repeatedly need memorized exceptions or external instructions, the underlying model may be unclear or inconsistent.
Study instruction:
- Draw a conceptual model for a familiar complex product and identify the visible cues that help users predict its behavior.
Key point:
- Understandable structure turns complexity into something manageable.
- Consistency and meaningful organization reduce reliance on memorization.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
58. Q: How do automation and standardization improve usability in technological products?
A: Show answer
Automation reduces the number of actions or skills users must perform, while standardization makes similar controls and conventions behave consistently across products or contexts. The early automobile required substantial strength and expertise; features such as starter motors and automatic functions removed some burdens. Standardized conventions for steering, pedals, and road positioning allowed people to transfer learning from one vehicle to another. Both approaches reduce cognitive or physical effort, but they solve different problems. Automation performs or assists an operation; standardization makes the remaining operation predictable. Designers should ensure that automation is transparent enough for users to understand system state and retain appropriate control, while standards should preserve consistent mappings and terminology across related products.
Study instruction:
- Identify one task improved by automation and one improved by standardization, explaining how each changes user effort and learning.
Key point:
- Automation reduces work; standardization reduces variation and relearning.
- Both can improve usability when users can still understand what the system is doing.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
59. Q: Why is standardization considered a cultural constraint in design?
A: Show answer
A standard is a convention shared by a community, such as the direction of clock movement, the placement of vehicle controls, units of measurement, or electrical connections. Once people learn a standard, they can apply that knowledge across products without relearning the mapping each time. A logically consistent alternative may still feel difficult because it violates learned expectations. The nonstandard clock in the chapter demonstrates this: its mechanism is internally coherent, but the reversed direction and relocated twelve require users to reinterpret a familiar display. Standards therefore shape what feels natural and predictable. Designers should respect established conventions unless there is a compelling benefit to changing them, and should provide migration support when a change is necessary.
Study instruction:
- Identify three conventions in a product category and explain what users would need to relearn if each were changed.
Key point:
- Familiar standards reduce cognitive effort through shared expectations.
- A logically valid alternative can still be unusable if it conflicts with learned convention.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
60. Q: Why can the process of establishing national or international standards become slow and political?
A: Show answer
Standard-setting involves organizations with different technical approaches, commercial interests, existing investments, and expectations about future technology. Companies already selling products that conform to one proposal may gain an advantage if that proposal becomes the standard. Professional bodies, regulators, manufacturers, and users may disagree about which features are essential, which trade-offs are acceptable, and whether the technology is mature enough to standardize. Proposals are reviewed, challenged, revised, and returned through multiple organizational levels, so agreement can take years. The final standard may be a compromise or may permit several incompatible options. Designers and managers should understand that standards are governance decisions as well as technical specifications, and should consider who benefits, who bears transition costs, and how interoperability will be maintained.
Study instruction:
- Map the stakeholders in a standard-setting decision and identify their technical, economic, and institutional incentives.
Key point:
- Standards reflect negotiations among technical, commercial, and political interests.
- A compromise may preserve compatibility while allowing inconsistent implementations.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
61. Q: What are the risks of standardizing too early or too late?
A: Show answer
Standardizing too early can lock an industry into immature technology, inefficient rules, or design choices that later prove error-prone. Standardizing too late allows incompatible approaches and installed bases to proliferate, making conversion expensive and politically difficult. Existing infrastructure, training, software, manufacturing, and user habits can create strong resistance to change even when a newer system is technically simpler. The metric system and the persistence of older measurement conventions illustrate the difficulty of replacing established standards. Good timing requires balancing the benefits of interoperability and predictable behavior against uncertainty about the technology and the cost of migration. A standard should also provide a path for revision so that it does not prevent useful innovation.
Study instruction:
- Analyze a current technology transition and list the costs of early lock-in, late convergence, and migration from legacy systems.
Key point:
- Early standards risk premature lock-in; late standards risk fragmentation and costly conversion.
- Standardization needs a realistic transition and revision strategy.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
62. Q: What does the HDTV standards example teach about technology change and standard-setting?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a lengthy debate over high-definition television formats, including resolution, interlaced versus progressive scanning, aspect ratios, and frame rates. The eventual agreement accommodated multiple alternatives, reflecting competing industry interests and technical constraints. Yet technology continued to advance during the process: better displays, processors, and conversion algorithms reduced the importance of some distinctions that had dominated the debate. The example shows that standards can be necessary for interoperability while still becoming outdated before widespread adoption. It also illustrates the danger of spending excessive effort on details whose practical importance to users may be limited. Standards bodies should focus on durable user and interoperability needs, consider technology trajectories, and avoid freezing transient implementation choices unnecessarily.
Study instruction:
- Choose a technology standard and identify which requirements are durable user needs and which may become obsolete as technology improves.
Key point:
- Long standard-setting cycles can be overtaken by technical progress.
- Standardization should prioritize interoperability and enduring user value.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
63. Q: Why did decimal-time proposals struggle to replace conventional timekeeping?
A: Show answer
Decimal time offered a simpler arithmetic structure by dividing the day into ten hours, each hour into one hundred minutes, and each minute into one hundred seconds. The chapter notes that decimal time was proposed during the French Revolution and later revisited by Swatch through a system of one thousand daily “beats.” These schemes did not become widespread because timekeeping is embedded in social routines, daylight, time zones, schedules, infrastructure, and shared expectations. A globally synchronized clock does not make sunrise, meals, work schedules, or local daily patterns identical. The example demonstrates that technical simplicity alone is insufficient to displace a deeply established convention. Adoption depends on coordination costs, compatibility, practical benefit, and the willingness of institutions and communities to change together.
Study instruction:
- Compare a technically simpler standard with an established convention and identify the social and infrastructural costs of migration.
Key point:
- Technical elegance does not guarantee social adoption.
- Standards succeed when they fit coordinated human practices, not just mathematical logic.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
64. Q: When is deliberately difficult design appropriate, and how does it differ from accidental poor usability?
A: Show answer
Most products should be understandable and usable, but some systems must prevent unauthorized, unsafe, or accidental actions. Examples include controlled doors, security systems, hazardous equipment, child-resistant medication containers, safety interlocks, and confirmation steps before irreversible deletion. Games are another special case: difficulty may be part of the intended challenge. In these situations, friction is purposeful because it controls access, slows a risky action, or requires deliberate confirmation. Accidental poor usability, by contrast, creates confusion without protecting a legitimate objective. Designers should identify exactly which action must be restricted and for whom, then preserve clarity and ease of use for legitimate users wherever possible. Difficulty should be proportionate to the risk and evaluated for unintended consequences.
Study instruction:
- Select a product that intentionally restricts an action and explain the protected risk, intended user, and unnecessary friction that should be removed.
Key point:
- Some difficulty is a safety or security requirement rather than a design defect.
- Restrict the risky action without making the entire system confusing.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
65. Q: How can designers make a system secure without relying on confusing or cryptic design?
A: Show answer
Security should be achieved through appropriate authorization, access controls, authentication, and carefully designed barriers rather than by making the entire product unintelligible. The chapter recognizes that some information or operations must be restricted, but emphasizes that even deliberately difficult systems should retain good design principles outside the protected action. Authorized users need to understand system state, locate essential controls, and complete legitimate work reliably. A design that hides everything or provides no feedback may frustrate authorized users, encourage workarounds, and create new safety risks. Designers should distinguish secrecy of sensitive information from usability of the authorized workflow, test both unauthorized resistance and legitimate operation, and consider how security procedures affect recovery, accessibility, and emergency response.
Study instruction:
- Draw an access-control flow and mark which information or actions require restriction and which must remain clear to authorized users.
Key point:
- Security and usability are not opposites when controls are targeted appropriately.
- Obscurity alone is not a substitute for a sound security mechanism.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
66. Q: Which design principles can be deliberately reversed to create controlled difficulty, and what risks follow?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies several ways to make an action difficult: hide critical components, use unnatural mappings, make physical actions awkward, require precise timing, withhold feedback, or make system state hard to interpret. These are the reverse of principles that ordinarily support discoverability, natural mapping, ease of action, and evaluation. Such measures should be used only when they serve a defined protective or experiential purpose. They should be narrowly applied, because broad confusion can cause authorized users to make mistakes, delay emergency action, or develop unsafe workarounds. Designers must test the barrier with the people it is intended to stop and separately test the rest of the system with legitimate users. A controlled obstacle should be understandable to those authorized to operate it, or supported by clear procedures and training.
Study instruction:
- For a safety or security control, identify the exact point of deliberate friction and assess its effect on authorized and unauthorized users.
Key point:
- Controlled difficulty is created by reversing usability principles deliberately.
- Unnecessary friction can create new errors and operational hazards.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
67. Q: Why can a safety feature create a secondary hazard, and how should designers manage that risk?
A: Show answer
A safety measure changes the environment and can introduce a new hazard even while reducing the original one. Street barriers protect people from falling into an excavation, but the barriers themselves can obstruct traffic or create collision risks if they are poorly placed or not visible. Warning signs, lights, and controlled access may be needed to manage the new danger. Emergency doors, alarms, and protective interlocks can also create trade-offs if they are inaccessible, misunderstood, or difficult to operate when needed. Designers should assess the whole system, identify hazards introduced by the control, and evaluate foreseeable misuse and emergency conditions. Safety review should be iterative and include the people who install, operate, maintain, and encounter the protective measure.
Study instruction:
- Conduct a simple hazard analysis of a safety intervention, listing the original hazard, the control, new hazards introduced, and additional mitigations.
Key point:
- A protective feature can create a secondary risk.
- Evaluate the complete system, including the hazards created by the safeguard itself.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
68. Q: How should two-person controls and simultaneous-action safeguards be designed?
A: Show answer
Two-person controls require separate people to perform coordinated actions before a system operates, reducing the chance that one individual can initiate an unauthorized or dangerous operation alone. They may be appropriate in security-sensitive or safety-critical contexts where independent authorization is necessary. The controls should be physically and logically arranged so that one person cannot easily defeat the intended separation, and the system should clearly indicate its state and whether both actions have been registered. Designers must also consider availability, emergency response, accessibility, staffing, and the risk that coordination failures delay legitimate work. A safeguard that is too cumbersome may encourage bypassing. The requirement should be justified by a defined threat or hazard, supported by procedures, and tested under realistic operating conditions.
Study instruction:
- Specify a two-person control for a high-risk operation and test it against collusion, accidental activation, emergency need, and staffing constraints.
Key point:
- Dual controls can reduce single-person misuse in carefully selected high-risk contexts.
- Their reliability depends on clear state feedback and workable procedures.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
69. Q: How should designers balance child protection with usability in medication packaging and restricted environments?
A: Show answer
Child-resistant packaging and controlled doors deliberately add difficulty to prevent access by people who should not use the contents or enter the space. The design must consider the capability of the protected group, the legitimate user's ability to operate the control, and the consequences of delay or failure. A child-resistant container should not create an unreasonable barrier for adults with reduced strength or dexterity; alternative accessible packaging or assistance may be necessary where appropriate. A school exit control must prevent unsupervised access without obstructing emergency evacuation. Designers should test the control with representative users, assess foreseeable workarounds, provide clear instructions, and comply with applicable safety standards and regulations. The goal is controlled access, not indiscriminate difficulty.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate one child-protection design for both its intended protective function and the accessibility needs of legitimate adult users.
Key point:
- Protection must be balanced against legitimate access and emergency use.
- A barrier should be tested with both protected and authorized user groups.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
70. Q: What is the central lesson of Chapter 6 about designing technology for people?
A: Show answer
Design brings together human needs and capabilities with technology, business, manufacturing, politics, culture, and commerce. A product must work technically, be feasible to produce and support, remain financially viable, satisfy purchasers, and help intended users accomplish meaningful activities. These requirements can conflict, and no single discipline can resolve every issue in isolation. The chapter's central lesson is that good design begins by discovering the right problem, uses human-centered research and iterative experimentation to develop a solution, and integrates the perspectives of all relevant stakeholders. Complexity is unavoidable, but confusion can be reduced through coherent conceptual models, flexibility, appropriate standards, and purposeful controls. The best result is not simply an elegant object; it is a system of technology and organizational decisions that assists people and enriches their lives.
Study instruction:
- Review a product from problem discovery through purchase, use, manufacture, maintenance, and safety, then identify how each chapter principle contributes to its success.
Key point:
- Effective design integrates people, technology, organizations, and real-world constraints.
- The measure of success is a coherent solution to a genuine human problem.
Source links:
- Source: Provided Chapter 6 text, “Design Thinking,” in The Design of Everyday Things, Revised and Expanded by Don Norman (Box file ID 2576439622930; no external source URL was supplied).
